Now through Christ <note>original: "p̅ xpm" (per Christum)</note> we imitate Him in all things; for we shall not say that this [struggle] exists in that blessed life—not only is it not perfected there, but it will not even be permitted to be thought of. <meta>This page begins the preface of Marsilio Ficino's translations of Neoplatonic texts, establishing the philosophical foundation of his friendship with Giovanni Cavalcanti.</meta>

# THE PREFACE OF MARSILIO FICINO THE FLOREN-
# TINE TO THE SHORT WORKS OF SPEUSIPPUS, ALCINOUS, AND
# PYTHAGORAS, TRANSLATED BY HIM FROM THE
# GREEK LANGUAGE INTO LATIN: TO GIOVANNI CAVALCANTI,
# A FLORENTINE CITIZEN AND HIS ONE
# TRUE FRIEND:

<note>Marsilio Ficino (1433–1499) was the leader of the Platonic Academy in Florence; Giovanni Cavalcanti was his closest friend and patron.</note>

TRUE FRIENDSHIP, MY DEAREST GIOVANNI,
the Platonic philosophers have best defined as the stable
union of the lives of two people. Indeed, we believe that there is
only one life in those men who, by a single labor, walk toward a single end,
as if by the same path toward the same
destination of their journey. And we consider that their
partnership will finally be stable when it is not only a union,
but when that which they have proposed to pursue through a certain
mutual duty between them is also stable and firm.

Truly, since every pursuit and association of men is
always accustomed to strive toward that which is thought to be good,
and there appear to be three types of goods for mortals: namely, the goods of the soul,
of the body, and external things; either the virtue of the soul is sought by them,
or the pleasure of the body, or an abundance of riches. Of these,
the first is certainly certain and perpetual; the remaining two
are fleeting and mortal. For which reason,
a stable union of life—which is true
friendship—can only exist between those who seek neither to accumulate riches

---

<insert>From the books of Isaac Cavalli</insert> <note>original: "ex libris Isa: Cavalli"</note>

<summary>Marsilio Ficino opens his translation of Platonic authors with a dedication to Giovanni Cavalcanti, defining true friendship as a stable union of souls pursuing virtue rather than fleeting material goods.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, Giovanni Cavalcanti, Neoplatonism, definition of friendship, virtue vs. pleasure, Speusippus, Alcinous, Pythagoras</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues a philosophical discourse on the nature of the soul and the pursuit of wisdom, likely part of a Renaissance Neoplatonic treatise or sermon.</meta>

...nor for the sake of satisfying the lusts of the body, which are all fleeting and perishable. Instead, they should set out with a certain shared zeal and with the full fire of the mind to seize and cultivate the one stable virtue of the soul. Our Plato, that master and leader of all philosophers, wished for this virtue of the soul to be called "Wisdom." They <note>referring to the Platonic school</note> considered Wisdom to be the knowledge of divine things. Furthermore, in his books *On the Republic* <note>original: "de re p." referring to Plato's Republic</note>, he demonstrates that divine things cannot become known to our minds in any other way than by God illuminating us, just as the forms of bodies cannot be seen by the eyes unless the sun illuminates the eyes. Therefore, it is God himself whom we desire to see; He who illuminates the sharp edge of the mind so that we may be able to perceive, and who finally shows Himself more clearly to the mind. In showing Himself, He delights us. God is likewise the Way for us, the Truth, and the Life <note>A reference to John 14:6, "Ego sum via et veritas et vita," integrated here into Platonic philosophy</note>. He is the Way because He turns us toward Himself with His rays, and leads us, and carries us away. He is the Truth because He shows Himself as true to us who have turned toward Him. Finally, He is the Life because He feeds and delights our soul—the contemplator of Him—forever with that blessed vision. Therefore, all those who desire to taste the sweetest liquids of wisdom must thirst for this perennial fountain of wisdom. It is necessary that all who desire to obtain the virtue of the soul follow after wisdom. From this it follows that whoever resolves to cultivate their soul is compelled to worship God. Friends...

<summary>The author synthesizes Platonic philosophy with Christian theology, arguing that true wisdom is the knowledge of divine things which can only be achieved through God's illumination of the mind.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Wisdom, Virtue, Divine Illumination, Republic, Knowledge of God, Soul, Truth, Life, Way</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that true friendship is a spiritual bond rooted in the shared pursuit of wisdom and the worship of God. It transitions from the definition of virtue to a historical list of famous philosophical friendships mediated by the divine.</meta>

...or alternatively, we have defined those among the former who strive toward virtue with a similar and equal zeal, and who cherish their friends with mutual favor. The cultivation of the soul consists in virtue alone. Virtue is wisdom. Wisdom is the knowledge of divine things. Divine light is bestowed upon us through knowledge of this kind. Therefore, the cultivation of the soul is the worship of God himself. Since friendship, then, strives by the consensus of two people toward cultivating the soul with virtue, it seems to be nothing other than the highest harmony of two souls in that very cultivation. Moreover, whoever worships God with a pious mind is loved by God. Therefore, friends are not merely two and alone, but there are always three by necessity: namely, two humans and one God.

This God—that is, Jupiter <note>original: "Jupiter"; here used in the Neoplatonic tradition to represent the supreme Deity or the Divine Mind</note>, the savior of human life—is He whom Plato always worshipped and whom Socrates always honored. He is the leader of human life; He reconciles us into one. He is the indissoluble bond <term>bond: original "nodus" (knot), a central concept in Neoplatonic friendship where God binds souls together.</term> of friendship and its perpetual guardian. With this God once acting as the reconciler, those ancient theologians <note>Refers to the *Prisca Theologia*, the "ancient theology" that Renaissance thinkers believed formed a single, continuous chain of wisdom from Adam to Plato.</note> whose memory we venerate are said to have shared a holy bond of friendship among themselves.

Among the Persians, Zoroaster is said to have had Arimaspas as a constant companion in the religious mysteries of philosophy, through the inspiration of the divine. Among the Egyptians, Hermes Trismegistus <term>Mercurius Trismegistus: A legendary Hellenistic figure believed to be the author of the Hermetic Corpus and a source of ancient wisdom.</term> similarly had Asclepius; Orpheus in Thrace had Musaeus. There also, Aglaophemus <note>The teacher who initiated Pythagoras into the Orphic mysteries.</note> had Pythagoras. The Athenian Plato had Dion first at Syracuse...

<summary>The author defines friendship as a three-way relationship between two people and God, arguing that true social bonds are forged through the shared pursuit of divine wisdom. He supports this by citing a lineage of "ancient theologians" and philosophers who practiced this sacred companionship.</summary>
<keywords>friendship, wisdom, Neoplatonism, Prisca Theologia, Zoroaster, Hermes Trismegistus, Pythagoras, Plato, divine light</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of historical philosophical friendships, moving from ancient examples to the author’s contemporary circle in Renaissance Florence. It emphasizes that a human companion, alongside divine guidance, is essential for the soul's ascent to truth.</meta>

...sleep. Then, after Dion <note>Dion of Syracuse (c. 408–354 BCE), a disciple of Plato who attempted to implement Platonic political reforms.</note> had died, he was with Xenocrates. To such an extent do wise men judge that, in order to proceed safely and calmly on the heavenly journey, it is necessary to have God as a leader and a human as a companion. I trust that I can most firmly follow their footsteps, which are imprinted upon the regions of the high heavens. In this, I seem to have already achieved one thing in abundance: that for the sake of practicing the studies of sacred <gloss>philosophy</gloss> and investigating truth through virtue, I do not lack the advantageous and pleasant society of an excellent man. 

For I believe that my current intimacy with Giovanni Cavalcanti and Marsilio Ficino <note>Giovanni Cavalcanti was the close friend and "beloved" of Marsilio Ficino, the leader of the Platonic Academy in Florence. This passage places their friendship in the same lineage as the great philosophers of antiquity.</note> should be placed among those friendships which we mentioned just before. And with God as our leader—who happily established and forged this bond for us—it will be of the greatest benefit to both of us for conducting our affairs, for the tranquility of life, and for the investigation of divine matters. 

Furthermore, I have decided to commit greater monuments of this singular goodwill to posterity at other times. For now, I offer certain small works recently translated by me from the Greek language into English <note>original: "Latinam" (Latin). The author is referring to his own Latin translations from the original Greek; here they are presented in English for the reader.</note> which seem most likely to contribute to that Platonic school <term>school: original "sectam" (sect), referring to the Neoplatonic tradition or "chain" of wisdom.</term> of <gloss>our</gloss> own, into whose sacred mysteries you have been initiated. 

Accept therefore, my unique friend, the book on the *Definitions* of Plato, composed by his nephew Speusippus; furthermore, the book by Alcinous the Platonist regarding the doctrines of this school; and finally, the *Laws and Symbols* of Pythagoras, whom Plato followed above all others.

<summary>The author identifies his friendship with Giovanni Cavalcanti and Marsilio Ficino as a modern continuation of the "sacred" friendships of antiquity. He introduces a collection of translated Neoplatonic texts—Speusippus, Alcinous, and Pythagoras—intended to aid in the study of divine truth.</summary>
<keywords>Neoplatonism, Giovanni Cavalcanti, Marsilio Ficino, Speusippus, Alcinous, Pythagoras, sacred friendship, spiritual journey, Florence, translation</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the introductory letter to Giovanni Cavalcanti and begins the primary text: a series of philosophical definitions attributed to Speusippus, Plato's successor. It transitions from the personal praise of friendship to the formal study of Platonic concepts.</meta>

By reading these things, once you are instructed in the secrets of the mysteries of divine philosophy, you will despise the empty dreams of this shadowy life for which the anxious common folk gape; and you will raise yourself to heavenly things, through the contemplation of which you will obtain tranquility in this present life and blessedness in the life to come. Farewell.

# The <gloss>Books on</gloss> the Definitions of Plato, by Speusippus <note>Speusippus (c. 408–339 BCE) was Plato's nephew and his successor as the head of the Academy in Athens.</note>, a disciple of Plato, translated by Marsilio Ficino <note>Marsilio Ficino (1433–1499) was the primary translator and philosopher responsible for the revival of Platonism in the Renaissance.</note> for Giovanni Cavalcanti.

---

<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'S' in blue, red, and gold within a rectangular frame.</image-desc>

<margin>Eternal</margin>
**ETERNAL:** that which existed before time, and exists now; subject to no corruption.

<margin>God</margin>
**GOD:** a living, immortal being, self-sufficient in his own blessedness; an eternal essence; the cause of goodness itself.

<margin>Generation</margin>
**GENERATION:** <note>original: "generatio" (the process of coming into being)</note> a movement toward essence; the receiving of existence; the process of coming into being.

<margin>Sun</margin>
**SUN:** celestial fire which alone can be seen from sunrise to sunset by the same observers; an eternal living being; a star by nature; the daily and greatest [star].

<margin>Time</margin>
**TIME:** the motion of the sun; the measure of progression.

<margin>Day</margin>
**DAY:** the sun's journey from sunrise to sunset; light contrary to night.

<margin>Dawn</margin>
**DAWN:** the beginning of the day; the first light from the sun.

<margin>Midday</margin>
**MIDDAY:** the time in which the shadows of bodies are at their shortest.

<margin>Sunset</margin>
**SUNSET:** the end of the day.

<margin>Night</margin>
**NIGHT:** the shadow contrary to day; the deprivation of the sun.

<summary>This page marks the beginning of Speusippus's "Definitions," a collection of Platonic philosophical terms translated by Marsilio Ficino. It provides concise, metaphysical descriptions of the divine, the cosmos, and the measurement of time.</summary>
<keywords>Speusippus, Marsilio Ficino, Neoplatonism, definitions, metaphysics, God, time, cosmology, Giovanni Cavalcanti</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of philosophical definitions attributed to Speusippus and translated by Marsilio Ficino. It moves from celestial phenomena (like the sun and time) into the definitions of natural elements, biological components, and the primary moral virtues.</meta>

<margin>Fortune</margin>
**FORTUNE:** A movement from the hidden to the hidden, occurring by a certain chance, the cause of a successful action. <note>Fortune is defined here as a "cause" that operates outside of human visibility or predictable logic.</note>

<margin>Old Age</margin>
**OLD AGE:** A decrease of life brought about by time.

<margin>Wind</margin>
**WIND:** An agitation of the air around the earth.

<margin>Air</margin>
**AIR:** An element whose every change of place is according to nature. <note>original: "secundum naturam"</note>

<margin>Heaven</margin>
**HEAVEN:** A body embracing all bodies, except for the supreme air itself. <note>In the Platonic cosmology used here, "Heaven" refers to the celestial sphere that contains the physical universe.</note>

<margin>Soul</margin>
**SOUL:** That which moves itself; the cause of vital movement in living beings.

<margin>Power</margin>
**POWER:** That which is effective through itself.

<margin>Sight</margin>
**SIGHT:** A <unclear>power</unclear> <note>The original "vitus" is likely a typo for "virtus" or "vis," meaning power or faculty.</note> by which bodies are discerned.

<margin>Bone</margin>
**BONE:** Marrow consolidated by heat. <note>This reflects the ancient biological theory that bones were formed by the "cooking" or hardening of marrow.</note>

<margin>Element</margin>
**ELEMENT:** That from which things are composed and into which composed things are dissolved.

<margin>Virtue</margin>
**VIRTUE:** The best disposition or habit of a mortal living being, praiseworthy in itself; the habit according to which that which possesses it is called good.

**COMMUNITY:** <note>original: "communio"</note> A just institution of laws by which that which is endowed [with it] is called upright; a habit of concord and piety.

<margin>Prudence</margin>
**PRUDENCE:** A power which leads by itself toward human happiness; the knowledge of goods and evils; providing a happy disposition by which we judge what is to be done and what is not.

<margin>Justice</margin>
**JUSTICE:** An agreement of the soul with itself and of its parts... <note>The definition ends abruptly, likely continuing on the next page; it refers to the Platonic idea of justice as internal harmony between the parts of the soul.</note>

<summary>This page lists definitions for natural phenomena and moral virtues, defining the soul as the source of movement and virtue as the highest state of a living being. It emphasizes the internal harmony of the individual and the community through justice and prudence.</summary>
<keywords>fortune, soul, air, heaven, virtue, prudence, justice, Speusippus, Ficino, Platonic philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the sequence of philosophical definitions. It completes the definition of Justice (begun on the previous page as "Community") and proceeds to define the other "cardinal virtues": Temperance, Fortitude (Courage), and the beginning of Constancy. These definitions reflect the Platonic tradition, emphasizing the "habitus" or stable disposition of the soul.</meta>

<page-num>4.</page-num>

->Definitions<-

...a mutual order toward one another. A habit <note>The Latin "habitus" refers to a stable, acquired state of character or a "disposition" rather than a mere repetitive action.</note> distributing to each according to their dignity; a habit by which one, being so endowed, always chooses what appears just; a habit of a life obedient to the laws; the equality of common things; a habit that always obeys right laws.

<margin>Temperance</margin>
**TEMPERANCE**: Moderation of the soul regarding natural desires and pleasures; the arrangement and order of the soul toward natural pleasures and pains; the harmony of the soul for commanding and obeying; a voluntary pursuit of those things which are according to nature <note>original: "secundum naturam"</note>; an action of the rational soul; a conduct regarding things honorable and shameful; a habit by which we choose and preserve what is fitting.

<margin>Fortitude</margin>
**FORTITUDE**: A fearless habit of the soul; military boldness; skill in those things pertaining to war; constancy of the mind against those things which usually cause fear; boldness as the attendant of prudence; fearlessness <unclear>unmoved</unclear> in expectation; a disposition preserving a rational purpose in dangers; strength resisting danger; vigor enduring all things for the sake of virtue; the soul's perseverance in those things which, to right reason, seem worthy of being dared or feared; the observance of those things which reason dictates in frightening matters; skill in military affairs; a habit persevering immovably in the law.

<margin>Constancy</margin>
**CONSTANCY**: The power of enduring hardship se- <note>The entry for Constancy is interrupted at the end of the page.</note>

<summary>This page provides concise, multi-layered definitions for the primary moral virtues of Justice, Temperance, and Fortitude, characterizing them as stable dispositions of a rational soul. It highlights the classical view of virtue as a harmony between reason, law, and natural desire.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical definitions of virtues and character traits, following the discussion of Fortitude. It concludes the description of a steadfast mind and proceeds through several specific moral qualities including Self-Sufficiency, Equity, and Liberty. These definitions are heavily influenced by Stoic and Platonic ethics, defining virtues as stable "habits" or "dispositions" of the soul.</meta>

...following ~~those things which~~ <note>The scribe appears to have crossed out "eijs que" in the original Latin.</note> right reason commanded; a power which is never cast down from the purpose of reason.

**Self-Sufficiency**: The perfect possession of goods; a habit <note>The Latin "habitus" refers to a stable, acquired state of character.</note> by which each person is their own master.

<margin>Equity</margin>
**EQUITY**: A relaxation <note>original: "remissio"; in a legal or moral sense, this refers to the tempering of strict justice with mercy or fairness.</note> of what is strictly just and beneficial; moderation in social interactions; a rational order of the soul regarding things honorable and shameful.

<margin>Endurance</margin>
**ENDURANCE**: The undergoing of trouble for the sake of what is honorable; the undergoing of labors for the sake of what is honorable.

<margin>Boldness</margin>
**BOLDNESS**: A sense of security, by which one neither expects future evil nor dreads the present.

<margin>Freedom from Pain</margin>
**FREEDOM FROM PAIN**: A habit by which we never fall into pains.

<margin>Perseverance</margin>
**PERSEVERANCE**: A state of mind <note>original: "affectio"</note> which completes whatever it has chosen; a voluntary endurance; an unshaken habit against labors.

<margin>Shame</margin>
**SHAME**: A restraint of boldness; a spontaneous and willing acceptance of what appears best; a dread of true reproach.

<margin>Liberty</margin>
**LIBERTY**: The mastery of life, an absolute dominion in all things; a license proper to one's life; a state of mind in the possession and use of things that is by no means stingy.

<margin>Generosity</margin>
**GENEROSITY**: A habit which preserves what is fitting in seeking and spending money; a decent acquisition and accumulation of money.

<summary>This page provides concise ethical definitions for various virtues and mental states, such as Equity, Boldness, and Liberty, framing them as stable dispositions of a rational soul. It emphasizes the importance of self-mastery and the pursuit of the "honorable" over mere avoidance of pain or accumulation of wealth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical lexicon of virtues and ethical terms. Following the definitions of endurance and liberty from the previous page, this section focuses on social virtues—such as mildness and modesty—and intellectual/spiritual qualities like happiness, piety, and the definition of "The Good" itself. The definitions continue to reflect a blend of Stoic and Platonic thought, categorizing virtues as specific "dispositions" or "habits" of the mind.</meta>

<margin>Mildness</margin>
**MILDNESS**: A constitution of the soul that remains unmoved against the impulse of anger; a moderate and tempered state.

<margin>Modesty</margin>
**MODESTY**: A voluntary yielding to that which is judged best. An orderly composure in the movements of the body.

<margin>Happiness</margin>
**HAPPINESS**: A good accumulated from all goods; a power for living well that is sufficient in itself; perfection according to virtue. A sufficient convenience of life. <note>original: "Beatitudo"; in this context, it refers to 'Eudaimonia'—a state of flourishing rather than a fleeting emotion.</note>

<margin>Magnificence</margin>
**MAGNIFICENCE**: An assessment according to right and honorable reason.

<margin>Diligence</margin>
**DILIGENCE**: A force of natural talent with which one is equipped, whereby they easily achieve what is fitting in every instance. Sharpness of mind. <note>original: "Industria"; here it implies both hard work and the cleverness to apply it effectively.</note>

<margin>Frugality</margin>
**FRUGALITY**: A rational simplicity of life. A devotion to the customs of good men.

<margin>Goodness</margin>
**GOODNESS**: A disposition <note>original: "affectio"</note> by which we choose the best things.

<margin>Magnanimity</margin>
**MAGNANIMITY**: A moderate use of chance occurrences. Courteous <note>original: "urbana"; implying the refined social conduct of a city-dweller.</note> conversation with men. A magnificence of the soul joined with reason.

<margin>Philanthropy, that is, humanity</margin>
**PHILANTHROPY** <gloss>that is,</gloss> humanity: A disposition of friendship that wins over men; a habit beneficial to men; the returning of thanks; a beneficial gratitude.

<margin>Piety</margin>
**PIETY**: Justice toward God; a disposition worshiping God; a voluntary veneration of God. A right knowledge of divine honor.

<margin>The Good</margin>
**THE GOOD**: That which is to be sought for its own sake.

It concludes with a fundamental definition of "The Good" as something desirable for its own inherent value.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical or thematic lexicon of virtues and social dispositions. The previous page concluded with "Ph..." (likely Philanthropia or Philosophia), and this page picks up with "Intrepiditas" (Intrepidity/Fearlessness). The focus remains on defining ethical states as "habitus" (habits or dispositions) of the soul, drawing heavily on Stoic and Aristotelian definitions of social harmony and personal character.</meta>

Intrepidity, Soundness, Peace, Humility, Shrewdness, Friendship, Nobility, Choice, Goodwill, Kinship, Accord, Charity, Fellowship, Sagacity

<margin>Intrepidity</margin>
### **INTREPIDITY**: A habit through which we are moved by no fears. <note>original: "Intrepiditas"; here defined as a state of emotional stability where fear does not disrupt one's actions.</note>

<margin>Soundness</margin>
### **SOUNDNESS** or a habit not succumbing to the passions. <note>original: "Sanitas"; while often meaning physical health, in this philosophical context, it refers to the "health of the mind" or being "sound of mind," where one is not overwhelmed by strong emotions.</note>

<margin>Peace</margin>
### **PEACE**: A state of rest, by which we cease from enmities and war.

<margin>Humility</margin>
### **HUMILITY**: A gentleness and a state entirely devoid of wrath. <note>original: "Humilitas"; the author defines this not as low self-esteem, but as a social ease and the absence of anger toward others.</note>

<margin>Shrewdness</margin>
### **SHREWDNESS**: A disposition by which we aim for our own end. <note>original: "Astutia"; the transcription "sine p̄priū" likely represents "fine proprio," meaning aiming toward one's own specific purpose or advantage.</note>

<margin>Friendship</margin>
### **FRIENDSHIP**: An agreement in honorable and just matters:
The choice of the same life with agreement in deliberation
and action. Concord in the sharing of life with
goodwill. A communion in the giving and receiving
of benefits.

<margin>Nobility</margin>
### **NOBILITY**: A virtue of natural talent; an ease of character 
of the soul for speaking as well as acting. <note>original: "Generositas"; refers to "generosity of spirit" or "nobility of birth/character," characterized by an effortless ability to do and say the right thing.</note>

<margin>Choice</margin>
### **CHOICE**: A right approval. <note>original: "Electio"; the act of choosing the correct path after evaluation.</note>

<margin>Goodwill</margin>
### **GOODWILL**: A pleasing and accepted choice of one human toward another.

<margin>Kinship</margin>
### **KINSHIP**: A communion in the same kind or family.

<margin>Accord</margin>
### **ACCORD**: A sharing of all things, 
thoughts, and a concord even of suspicions. <note>original: "Consensio"; a deep level of agreement between people where even their private doubts or guesses are in harmony.</note>

<margin>Charity</margin>
### **CHARITY**: A complete declaration of the mind: a civic knowledge
of honorable and useful things; a knowledge 
bringing justice into the republic. <note>original: "Caritas"; here "Charity" is treated as a political or civic virtue—a form of social love that informs how a citizen applies justice to the state (rem p. / rem publicam).</note>

<margin>Fellowship</margin>
### **FELLOWSHIP**: A friendship forged from the long-standing habit 
of equals. <note>original: "Societas"; the bond created by people of the same status living together over a long period.</note>

<margin>Sagacity</margin>
### **SAGACITAS**: An innate virtue of reasoning. <note>original: "Sagacitas"; a natural sharpness or "quick-wittedness" in logical thought.</note>

Intrepidity, Soundness, Peace, Humility, Shrewdness, Friendship, Nobility, Choice, Goodwill, Kinship, Accord, Charity, Fellowship, Sagacity

<meta>This page continues the thematic lexicon of virtues and mental dispositions. <page-num>6.</page-num>

<margin>faith</margin>
**FAITH** · a correct expectation / that it will lead to happiness as it seems to oneself. <unclear>Asorg</unclear> <note>original: "Asorg"; possibly a corruption of a Greek term for "stability" or "steadfastness," common in Stoic definitions of faith/trust.</note> stability.

<margin>truthfulness</margin>
**TRUTHFULNESS** · a right habit in affirming and denying. The knowledge of the truth.

<margin>will</margin>
**WILL · APPETITE** according to right reason; a rational affection. An affection tending toward nature combined with reason. <note>original: "Voluntas · Appetitus"; here, the author defines "will" not as a random impulse, but as a "rational desire" that aligns with the natural order.</note>

<margin>counsel</margin>
**COUNSEL** · the advice of another regarding the logic of things to be done.

<margin>opportunity</margin>
**OPPORTUNITY** · the imminence of time: in which it is necessary either to act or to undergo anything. <note>original: "Oportunitas"; refers to "Kairos" or the "right moment" for action.</note>

<margin>caution</margin>
**CAUTION** · the turning away from evil; the diligence of guarding.

<margin>order</margin>
**ORDER**: a harmony of operations in mutual communion; a proportion; the cause for the alternation of members. A contemplation suitable for perception.

<margin>attention</margin>
**ATTENTION** · an application of the mind toward learning.

<margin>natural ability</margin>
**NATURAL ABILITY** · speed of learning; a good birth of nature. A virtue implanted in nature. <note>original: "Ingenium"; often translated as "genius," but in this context, it refers to innate intellectual capacity and the quickness of the mind.</note>

<margin>teachability</margin>
**TEACHABILITY** · regarding natural ability and the speed of perceiving. <note>original: "Docilitas"; refers to the readiness or ease with which one is taught.</note>

<margin>judgment</margin>
**JUDGMENT**: a settled declaration concerning that which has come into dispute.

<margin>law</margin>
**LAW**: a standard / by which it is asked whether someone has deserved a wrong, or not? <note>original: "Lex"; the definition focuses on the law as a diagnostic tool for justice and injury.</note>

**LAWFUL CIVILITY** · obedience to good laws. <note>original: "Legittima Civilitas"; suggests that true "civility" or citizenship is defined by its relationship to the rule of law.</note>

<margin>joy</margin>
**JOY** · gladness in the works of temperance. <note>original: "Gaudium"; distinguished from mere "pleasure," joy here is the spiritual result of self-control.</note>

<margin>honor</margin>
**HONOR**: a bestowing of good things on account of the duty of virtue.

<meta>This page continues the thematic lexicon of virtues and mental dispositions from page 6. Having previously covered internal qualities like truthfulness and will, the text now shifts toward social and civic virtues (concord, the commonwealth) and the practical mechanics of the mind (foresight, memory, intelligence).</meta>

<margin>Esteem</margin> **ESTEEM** and dignity acquired through virtue; the outward form of veneration and the preservation of one’s worth. <note>original: "Estimatio"; refers to the value or status a person holds within a community based on their moral character.</note>

**READINESS** is the mother of dispatch in the pursuit of carrying out affairs. <note>original: "Promptitudo"; defined here as the proactive drive that allows tasks to be completed efficiently.</note>

<margin>favor</margin> **VOLUNTARY FAVOR** is beneficence. It is the returning of a good service at a favorable time.

<margin>Concord</margin> **CONCORD** is the consensus between those who rule and those who are subjects, regarding the respect given to commanding and obeying. <note>original: "Concordia"; a classic political definition of social harmony where both leaders and citizens agree on their respective roles.</note>

<margin>Commonwealth</margin> **THE COMMONWEALTH** is a gathering of many people, sufficient in itself for attaining happiness; a lawful union of a multitude. <note>original: "Res. p." (Res publica); the author uses "conspiratio" for "union," which literally means "breathing together" or acting in total agreement.</note>

**FORESIGHT**: a preparation for things to come.

**DELIBERATION**: the consideration of future things and the manner in which they may be brought together. <note>original: "Consultatio"; the process of weighing options before taking action.</note>

**VICTORY**: a force that prevails in a struggle.

**ELOQUENCE**: perspicacity and the ability to obtain that which has been proposed. <note>original: "Facundia"; here, eloquence is not just beautiful speech, but the strategic skill of persuasion.</note>

**A GIFT**: a mutual exchange of gratitude.

**OPPORTUNITY**: the very flower of time, contributing to the conduct of affairs; a time that brings about a certain good work. <note>original: "Occasio"; the Latin "flos ipse temporis" (the flower of time itself) suggests that opportunity is the peak moment for action.</note>

**MEMORY**: a disposition of the soul that guards the truth inherent within it.

**INTELLIGENCE**: the intensification of thought.

**UNDERSTANDING**: the beginning of knowledge. <note>original: "Intellectio"; distinguishes the initial grasp of a concept from the broader "intelligence" of the mind.</note>

**HOLINESS**: a caution that turns away from sins committed against God. A diligent observance of divine worship according to the laws of nature.

<summary>This page defines a series of civic and intellectual virtues, ranging from the structure of a successful Commonwealth to the internal workings of Memory and Intelligence. It emphasizes that social order (Concord) and personal success (Eloquence, Opportunity) rely on both moral preparation and timely action.</summary>

<keywords>esteem, readiness, favor, concord, commonwealth, foresight, deliberation, victory, eloquence, gift, opportunity, memory, intelligence, understanding, holiness</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the lexicon from page 6, moving from social and civic virtues into the realm of epistemology (the study of knowledge) and the faculties of the mind. The author provides structured definitions for how humans perceive, reason, and communicate, drawing heavily on Aristotelian and Platonic philosophical traditions.</meta>

**DIVINATION**: Knowledge that foretells things without the need for logical proof. <note>original: "Divinatio"; here defined as "presaga" (foreboding/prophetic), a type of insight that bypasses standard demonstration or evidence.</note>

**PROPHECY**: The knowledge by which it is discerned what is currently present, or what the future holds for a living mortal. <note>original: "Vaticinum"; while similar to divination, this is framed specifically as a gift for understanding the human timeline.</note>

**WISDOM**: Knowledge that presupposes absolutely nothing. It is the knowledge of those things that are eternal. It is the knowledge that contemplates the cause of all things. <note>original: "Sapientia"; this follows the classical definition of wisdom as "the study of first causes" and "eternal truths," rather than temporary or changing facts.</note>

**PHILOSOPHY**: The desire for wisdom. The contemplation of truth, as truth itself. Care and concern according to right reason. <note>original: "Philosophia"; literally "love of wisdom." The text emphasizes that philosophy is both an intellectual pursuit and a moral "care" (cura) guided by reason.</note>

**KNOWLEDGE**: The soul’s grasp of things that are unchangeable by means of reason. It is the comprehensive power over a specific thing or several things, made immutable by reason. It is true reason, unchanging in its understanding. <note>original: "Scientia"; in this period, "Science" did not mean laboratory experiments, but rather certain, demonstrated knowledge of things that do not change.</note>

**OPINION**: A perception joined with belief and reasoning. The discourse of reason. A thought <unclear>falling</unclear> into truth or falsehood through the process of reasoning. <note>original: "Opinio"; unlike "Knowledge," "Opinion" is described as something that can be either true or false, representing a lower level of certainty.</note>

**SENSE**: A progression of the soul; a movement of the mind. The announcement to the soul through the body regarding the forms of men, from which is created the irrational power of the soul that learns through the body. <note>original: "Sensus"; this refers to sensory perception. The author views it as the "messenger" that brings physical information to the soul.</note>

**HABIT**: An affection of the soul, according to which we are said to be of a certain quality. <note>original: "Habitus"; an Aristotelian term referring to a stable disposition or state of being (like being "courageous" or "learned").</note>

**VOICE**: An utterance from the mouth according to a thought. <note>original: "Vox"; defined here simply as the physical manifestation of an internal thought.</note>

**SPEECH**: A voice that can be written. It is the proclamation of any matter. A composition of a verb and a noun without the harmony of song. <note>original: "Oratio"; defined here in a grammatical and logical sense—plain discourse or a sentence, distinguished from music or poetry.</note>

**NOUN**: A simple word signifying that which... <note>original: "Nomen"; the text cuts off here, but it refers to a "Name" or "Noun" as the most basic unit of meaning in language.</note>

<summary>This page defines the cognitive and linguistic tools of the human mind, distinguishing between certain knowledge (Wisdom, Science) and uncertain perception (Opinion, Sense). It concludes with basic definitions of language, such as Voice and Speech.</summary>
<keywords>Epistemology, Wisdom, Philosophy, Knowledge, Opinion, Sense Perception, Habit, Logic, Grammar</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical lexicon from the previous page, shifting focus from the internal faculties of the soul (like wisdom and knowledge) to the building blocks of language and the fundamental concepts of ethics. The definitions continue to draw upon the Platonic and Aristotelian tradition, categorizing the world through logic and virtue.</meta>

...is predicated according to essence; and everything which is least of all said according to itself. <note>This fragment likely concludes a definition of "Substance" or "Essential Property" from the preceding page.</note>

**DICTION**: The human voice that can be written down; a certain common sign signifying something without the harmony of song. <note>original: "Dictio"; here distinguished from music or animal sounds by its ability to be recorded in text and its specific meaning.</note>

**SYLLABLE**: An articulation of the human voice that can be written down.

**DEFINITION**: A statement composed of a genus and a difference. <note>original: "Definitio"; this is the classic logical formula for defining a thing—identifying its broad category (genus) and the specific trait that sets it apart (differentia).</note>

**CONJECTURE**: A sign of a hidden thing. <note>original: "Coniectura"; an inference based on evidence of something not yet fully visible or understood.</note>

**DEMONSTRATION**: A true reasoning, a statement showing something through things already known. <note>original: "Demostratio"; in formal logic, this is a proof where the conclusion follows necessarily from established premises.</note>

**ELEMENT OF THE VOICE**: A simple sound, of which the other sounds are composed. <note>original: "Elementum vocis"; equivalent to a basic phoneme or letter-sound.</note>

**USEFUL**: The cause by which each thing is well-disposed; the cause of some good.

**CONDUCIVE**: That which leads toward the good. <note>original: "Conferens"; referring to things that contribute to or "bring together" a positive outcome.</note>

**BEAUTIFUL**: That which is good.

**GOOD**: That which is the cause of well-being in all things that exist; the cause of everything that tends toward it; that from which come all things that are to be chosen.

**TEMPERAMENT**: An ornament and a right established by Law, from which justice proceeds. <note>original: "Temperamentum"; rather than just a mood, the author defines this as a "proper ordering" or "tempering" of society through legal and moral harmony.</note>

**VOLUNTARY**: That which leads itself; that which is to be chosen for its own sake; that which is brought about through thought.

**FREE**: That which is master of itself.

**MODERATE**: A middle point between excess and deficiency, sufficient according to skill. <note>original: "Moderatum"; this definition relies on the classical "Golden Mean," where virtue is found in the balance between having too much and having too little.</note>

It emphasizes the structural nature of language and the Aristotelian idea of virtue as a "middle point" between extremes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical and political lexicon from the previous page. Having previously defined elements of logic and language, the text now moves into definitions of ethical virtues, the nature of humanity, and the structures of the state (the city, law, and kingship).</meta>

**MEASURE**: The middle between excess and deficiency. <note>original: "Mensura"; this definition reflects the Aristotelian "Golden Mean," where virtue is found in the balance between two extremes.</note>

**PALM**: The reward of virtue, to be desired for its own sake. <note>original: "Palma"; the palm branch was a traditional classical symbol of victory and merit.</note>

**IMMORTALITY**: A living essence and eternal perseverance.

**HOLY**: The worship of God; that which is pleasing to God. <note>original: "Sanctus".</note>

<margin>man.</margin> **MAN**: A two-legged animal without feathers, with broad nails; the only living thing that participates in the science of practical affairs. <note>original: "Homo"; this is a famous classical definition often attributed to Plato. The detail about "broad nails" was historically said to have been added after the philosopher Diogenes mocked the definition by presenting a plucked chicken as a "Platonic man."</note>

**SACRIFICE**: A gift of a victim offered to God.

<margin>vow</margin> **VOW**: A prayer of men to God, by which they demand either good things or what seem to be good things.

**KING**: Ruling according to the laws; subject to no one; the blameless founder of the civil order.

**PRINCIPALITY**: Providence for all. <note>original: "Principatus"; refers here to the office of a ruler or sovereignty, defined as a form of "providence" or caring foresight for the whole community.</note>

**LICENSE**: A concession of the law. <note>original: "Licentia"; permission or freedom granted by legal authority.</note>

**LAWGIVER**: The author of laws by which the city ought to be governed.

**LAW**: A civil institution for the multitude, without a fixed limit of time. <note>original: "Lex"; the author distinguishes a general law from a temporary decree by its lack of an expiration date.</note>

**SUPPOSITION**: An unprovable principle; a summary of a discourse. <note>original: "Suppositio"; in logic, a foundational assumption that serves as the starting point for an argument.</note>

**DECREE**: A civil ordinance regarding a fixed time. <note>original: "Decretus"; unlike a permanent law, a decree is defined here as being for a specific duration.</note>

**STATESMAN**: Skilled in those things that pertain to the administration of the Republic. <note>original: "Civilis Homo"; literally a "civil man," referring to one expert in "Res publica" (public affairs or the state).</note>

<margin>City.</margin> **CITY**: A dwelling of many people using common laws; a gathering of people bound by the same laws. <note>original: "Civitas"; this refers to the organized political community or "polis" rather than just the physical buildings.</note>

It frames the state and its leadership through the lens of providence, virtue, and the rule of law.</summary>

9.

**ANIMOSITY**: A violent, irrational impulse, or one without the reason and order of the mind. <note>original: "Animositas"; in this context, it refers to a sudden surge of irrational spirit or passion rather than long-term ill-will.</note>
**DREAD**: Trembling in the expectation of evil. <note>original: "Pavor".</note>
**FLATTERY**: Socializing for the sake of pleasure without the consideration <gloss>reason</gloss> of what is good; a habit tending toward the enjoyment of social custom and exceeding the middle path. <note>original: "Adulatio"; the "middle path" or "mean" is a reference to the Aristotelian idea that virtue is the balance between two extremes.</note>
**ANGER**: A provocation of the irascible part of the soul specifically toward seeking revenge. <note>original: "Ira"; the "irascible" soul is the part of human nature that responds to challenges with spirit or aggression.</note>
**INSULT**: An injury which is inflicted through contempt.
**INCONTINENCE**: A disposition pulling one toward things that seem pleasant, contrary to the judgment of right reason <gloss>reason</gloss>. <note>original: "Incontinentia"; in classical ethics, this refers to "akrasia" or a lack of self-control, where a person knows what is right but acts on impulse anyway.</note>
**LAZINESS**: An escape from the labors which arise from governing.
**TIMIDITY**: The initial cause of an oncoming impulse. <note>original: "Timiditas"; here defined as the first internal reaction or hesitation when faced with an action.</note>
**CALUMNY**: The disparagement of friends carried out through words.
**OPPORTUNITY**: The time in which everything is fit either to be experienced or to be done.
**INJUSTICE**: A habit which despises the laws. <note>original: "Iniquitas".</note>
**INDIGENCE**: A reduction or lack of good things.
**SHAME**: Fear in the expectation of disgrace. <note>original: "Verecundia".</note>

**SHAMELESSNESS**: A habit or a disgrace <unclear>committed</unclear> for the sake <gloss>cause</gloss> of profit. <note>original: "Impudentia".</note>

<meta>This page concludes the series of philosophical and ethical definitions found in the previous section. While the preceding page focused on emotional responses and social vices (like Anger and Flattery), this page moves toward intellectual habits, social order, and the nature of individual strength. It finishes with a formal closing marker for the lexicon.</meta>

**FIERCENESS**: An excess of boldness that does not wrongly fear those things which are to be feared; <note>original: "Ferocitas". This defines a specific type of courage that borders on recklessness but maintains a distinction about what truly warrants fear.</note>

**AMBITION**: A habit of accepting all honors for the sake of popularity or influence, beyond the standard of reason. <note>original: "Ambitio".</note>

**DEGENERATION**: A corruption in nature; a turning away from that which is according to nature. Its measure is defined in relation to nature. <note>original: "Degeneratio". The scribe uses the abbreviations "s̅m̅ n̅a̅m̅" for "secundum naturam," a common scholastic phrase meaning "according to nature."</note>

**HOPE**: The expectation of good;

**FURY**: The corruption of true judgment. <note>original: "Furor"; in this philosophical context, it refers to a mental state where one's ability to assess reality is damaged, rather than just extreme anger.</note>

**LOQUACITY**: An unbridled lack of restraint in speaking, conducted without reason <gloss>logic</gloss>.

**CONTRARIETY**: The greatest possible distance between those things which are held within the same genus <gloss>category</gloss> through specific differences. <note>original: "Contrarietas"; a term from formal logic describing opposites that belong to the same class, such as "hot" and "cold" both being types of temperature.</note>

**INVOLUNTARY**: That which is done contrary to one's intent or opinion;

**DISCIPLINE**: A faculty that cares for the soul; <note>original: "Disciplina"; here understood as a systematic way of life or study that improves the internal self.</note>

**ERUDITION**: The passing down of discipline <gloss>instruction</gloss>;

**ESTABLISHMENT OF LAWS**: The knowledge that renders a city free from civil unrest;

**ADMONITION**: Speech that chastises based on judgment. A discourse that deters one from errors;

**AID**: The driving away of an evil, whether it has already matured or is merely approaching; <note>original: "Auxilium".</note>

**CHASTISEMENT**: A remedy or cure applied because of crimes committed;

**POWER**: Excellence in actions or in speech; the habit by which we are rendered capable. <note>original: "Potestas".</note>

**STRENGTH**: A force inherent to each thing’s nature that preserves it by avoiding harm; <note>original: "Robur"; often used to describe the "hardwood" or core strength of a person or object.</note>

<meta>This page provides a clean break from the previous section's encyclopedic list of ethical and logical definitions. It serves as a title page and introduction for a new work: the *Handbook of Platonism* (Didaskalikos) by the 2nd-century philosopher Alcinous. This specific text is a Latin translation by the renowned Renaissance humanist Marsilio Ficino, dedicated to his close friend and patron Giovanni Cavalcanti. The text shifts from brief definitions to a cohesive philosophical argument about the nature of a philosopher.</meta>

->End of the definitions<-

### The book of Alcinous the Platonist on the doctrine of Plato, trans-
### lated by Marsilio Ficino the Florentine for Giovanni
### Cavalcanti, his unique friend

<image-desc>Large illuminated initial 'P' in blue, decorated with red, green, and gold floral and scrollwork motifs within and around the letter.</image-desc>

This is the primary teaching of **PLATO’S** instructions. Philosophy <note>original: "phia", an abbreviation for "philosophia"</note> is the appetite for wisdom; or the release of the soul from the body and a certain turning toward those things which are true and divine. Wisdom is the knowledge of divine and human matters. A philosopher <note>original: "phus", an abbreviation for "philosophus"</note>, moreover, is one who takes his name from philosophy, just as a musician does from music. 

And he who is to be a philosopher must be so fashioned by nature itself that, first, he exists as someone easy and ready for every kind <note>original: "gnā", an abbreviation for "genera"</note> of discipline. Second, that he should apply his mind not to those things which are in a state of flux <note>The "flux" refers to the physical world of change and decay, which Platonists contrasted with the eternal world of Ideas.</note>, but to those things which always remain in the same state. In the third place, he must be truthful by nature, entirely foreign to every lie. Furthermore, it is necessary for him to be born temperate <gloss>self-controlled</gloss> in some way; he should receive from nature those parts of the soul which are prone to be carried away by passions <note>original: "affectibus"; referring to the emotional or irrational parts of the soul.</note> in a state of being already mastered. For whoever, greedy for truth, turns his mind toward the contemplation of divine things, [regards] pleasures... <note>The text ends mid-sentence at "voluptates" (pleasures), likely continuing on the next page to explain how the philosopher disregards physical pleasures.</note>

<summary>This page marks the beginning of Alcinous’s "Handbook of Platonism," translated by Marsilio Ficino, defining philosophy as a soul-level pursuit of divine truth. It lists the natural requirements for a philosopher, emphasizing intellectual readiness, a focus on eternal truths, and innate temperance.</summary>
<keywords>Alcinous, Plato, Marsilio Ficino, Giovanni Cavalcanti, Philosophy, Wisdom, Soul, Temperance, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of natural qualities required for a philosopher, transitioning from the previous page's focus on truthfulness to the philosopher's relationship with the body and the distinction between the contemplative and active lives.</meta>

...to value the body but little. Moreover, it is necessary for the philosopher to possess a liberal spirit <note>A "liberal spirit" refers to a generous, free-thinking mind, unbecoming of a person obsessed with petty or worldly matters.</note>. For indeed, nothing is more contrary to one who intends to contemplate the exceptional truth of things than a lack of greatness in the soul. Furthermore, his will loves justice, especially since he is devoted to the study of truth, moderation, and generosity. Add to this that he has need of a sharp intellect and a strong memory; for these qualities bring the philosopher to perfection. And indeed, these natural gifts, when proper discipline and education are applied, produce a man perfected in virtue. But if they are neglected, they become the causes of the greatest vices. And Plato is accustomed to refer to these qualities by the common names of moderation, <unclear>temperance</unclear>, courage, and justice.

---

Since there are two kinds of life—the contemplative and the active—and the duty of the contemplative life consists in the knowledge of truth, while that of the active life consists in carrying out <note>original: "machinationi", here referring to the practical application or execution of plans</note> what reason dictates, the **contemplative** <margin>contemplative</margin> life is certainly to be held in higher honor. However, that life which is engaged in action is its necessary attendant and servant. <margin>active</margin> That this is so will be shown by the following: Contemplation is the operation of the mind as it understands divine things. Action, however, is the operation of the rational soul carried out through the body. For the soul, by contemplating the divine and divine intelligence, is said to be moved by the highest good. And this state of the soul is called **Wisdom**, <margin>Wisdom.</margin> which...

<summary>This page outlines the inherent virtues of a philosopher—such as a sharp memory and a love for justice—and defines the hierarchy between the contemplative life (understanding the divine) and the active life (practical action).</summary>
<keywords>virtue, contemplative life, active life, wisdom, Plato, justice, moderation, memory, intellect</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the distinction between the contemplative and active lives. It concludes the thought that the soul, in its highest function, seeks the divine, and then transitions into how a philosopher should interact with the state and the tripartite division of Platonic study.</meta>

...indeed, nothing else ought to be sought than the **likeness of God** <note>original: "dei similitudinem"; this refers to the Platonic concept of *homoiosis theo*, the idea that the ultimate goal of humanity is to become as similar to the divine as possible through reason and virtue</note>. For this reason, an operation of this kind is primary and venerable, and is to be desired most of all; it is also a good that is especially our own, which no obstacles prevent from remaining within our own power. And this is the cause of our proposed end. The **active** life, however, and its action, is greatly aided by the service of the body. Worldly things often hinder us, and those things which the contemplative life observes, men desire to transfer soon into duties. For indeed, the man devoted to learning only then betakes himself to civil administration when he perceives that it is being handled badly by other men. He considers certain external supports to be things such as military service, the command of legions, and the execution of legal judgments; but the primary and greatest of all duties to be performed are establishing **laws**, prescribing order for the **Republic**, and instituting the discipline for educating the youth. Wherefore, it is necessary for the philosopher never to omit the investigation of truth, but always to nourish and increase it; and only sometimes to touch upon human actions as though they were something inferior. Indeed, Plato wished the study of the philosopher to be occupied primarily in these matters: In the contemplation of reality; in the practice of virtues; and in the consideration of speech. The knowledge of things is called **contemplation**; the study of morals is called **active**; and the study of speech is called **dialectics** <term>dialectics: the art of investigating the truth of opinions through logical argumentation and the classification of concepts</term>. Truly, dialectics is distributed into many species: into division...

It introduces Plato's three branches of philosophy: contemplation (physics/metaphysics), action (ethics), and dialectics (logic).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the systematic division of philosophy based on Platonic thought, moving from the broad distinction between contemplative and active lives into the specific sub-disciplines of Dialectic, Ethics, and Physics.</meta>

<margin>Dialectic</margin> **Dialectic** <note>The art of investigating or discussing the truth of opinions</note> treats definition, induction, and reasoning. These are found either in demonstration, which is necessary reasoning, or in evidence, which is called perfect reasoning. And finally, it deals with **sophisms** <note>original: "sophismata"; clever but deceptive arguments that appear true but are actually false</note>, to which the lawful philosopher <note>abbreviated "pbs" for *philosophus*</note> will apply effort—not because he approves of them as primary subjects, but because he judges that they will sometimes be necessary to understand. Furthermore, of that philosophy which is concerned with action, the first part cares for the morals of the human being himself. The second governs the home. The last protects the Republic; the first is called **moral**, the second **domestic**, and the third **civil** <note>these correspond to ethics, economics (in the classical sense of household management), and politics</note>. And of that philosophy which strives toward contemplation, one part is unmoving and divine—

<margin>Theology {</margin> —and it investigates the primary causes of things, which we call **theology**. The second part observes the motion of the stars, the circuits and restorations of the heavenly bodies, and the order of the world; this is named **natural knowledge** <note>original: "nālis [naturalis] cognitio"; what we would today call physics or natural science</note>. Mathematics follows: which contains geometry and arithmetic. Since philosophy is distributed into three main species—the **contemplative**, the **active**, and the **dialectical**—one must first treat dialectic itself: what **Plato** thinks of it, and specifically, first concerning the faculty of judgment.

Since there is something that judges and something that is judged, it is necessary that something also arises from both: namely, that which is judging...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of dialectic and logic within the Platonic framework. ...we can call it. Therefore, properly, judgment itself is named "judicial." More commonly, however, that which judges is also called judgment. Furthermore, judgment appears to be twofold: one *from which*, and the other *through which* things are judged. The former is our intellect; the latter is a **natural tool** <note>original: "organu~ nāle"; here referring to the instrument or faculty of the mind</note>. This [tool] distinguishes first true things, and in the second place, false things. Nor is this anything else than <margin>the judge</margin> **natural reason**.

If you wish to have this more clearly and fully explained, the philosopher should be called the judge, by whom the things themselves are judged. Likewise, reason is the judge through which they are truly judged—which we have indeed called the tool. 

Moreover, reason is twofold. One is entirely incomprehensible and true; the other is infallible according to the knowledge of things. The first belongs to God and by no means to man. The second belongs to man. 

But this [human reason] is also twofold: one looks toward intelligible things <note>things understood by the mind alone, like mathematics or divine truths</note>, while the other looks toward sensible things <note>physical things perceived by the senses</note>. The first is **knowledge** <note>original: "scientia"; certain, demonstrative understanding</note>; the second, however, is **opinion**. And the former possesses stability, since it is occupied with stable things. The latter possesses likelihood and imagination, since it tends toward changeable things. 

Truly, for knowledge concerning intelligible things and for opinion concerning sensible things, <margin>sensation</margin> understanding and sensation are the principles. Sensation is surely a **passion** <note>original: "passio"; in this context, it means an impression or the state of being acted upon by an external object</note> of the soul through the body, first announcing the passive power. After it is truly in the soul...

It distinguishes between divine reason and human reason, the latter of which is divided into certain knowledge of eternal truths and opinion regarding the physical, changing world.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of human judgment and opinion within the Platonic framework, moving from the definition of opinion to the mechanics of how memory and sensation interact to create it. It specifically addresses the formation of true versus false opinion and introduces Plato's famous "wax tablet" and "internal painter" metaphors.</meta>

Through the organs of the senses, the **forms of things** <note>original: "species rerum"; refers to the mental images or appearances of objects</note> are impressed according to sensation, so much so that they remain firm and indelible; the preservation of these is indeed called **memory**. 

**Opinion**, however, is a combination of memory and sensation. For when a certain sensible object <note>a physical object that can be perceived by the senses</note> first occurs to us, a sensation is produced in us from it; from that sensation comes memory. Then, when the same object is presented to us again, we compare the preceding memory with the subsequent sensation. In doing this, we say, "Socrates," "a horse," "fire," and other things of this sort. This is called opinion: when we join the preceding memory with the recent sensation. 

Whenever these things that are brought together harmonize with each other, a **true opinion** exists; if they clash, it is a **false opinion**. For if someone, while keeping the form of Socrates in their memory, should happen upon Plato himself and think—because of a certain resemblance—that they are meeting Socrates again, and subsequently, drawing the sensation of Plato as if it were from Socrates, fits it to the memory of Socrates, a false opinion results. 

That [faculty] in which sensation and memory occur, Plato compares to a **wax impression** <note>original: "ceree effigiei"; a reference to the *Theaetetus*, where Plato describes the mind as a wax tablet upon which experiences are stamped</note>. But when the soul, reshaping through thought the concept formed from sensation and memory, looks upon these things as if upon the very sources from which they flowed, Plato calls this a certain "picture" and also **imagination** <note>original: "phantasiam"</note>. He calls **thought**, however, <note>text cuts off</note>

<summary>This page explains the Platonic theory of how the mind forms opinions by comparing new sensory data to stored memories, using the famous wax tablet metaphor to describe memory. It distinguishes between true and false opinions based on whether the current sensation correctly matches the stored mental image.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the mechanics of opinion (formed by memory and sensation) to the higher functions of the soul: thought, speech, and "intellection." It defines the various ways the mind interacts with truths both before and after its union with the physical body, drawing heavily on the Platonic theory of Recollection.</meta>

...the discourse and reasoning of the soul itself with itself. **Speech**, however, is thought poured out through the mouth by means of an articulate voice. 

**Understanding** <note>original: "intellectio"; the direct grasp of truth by the mind's eye</note> is the operation of the intellect as it contemplates primary "intelligible things" <note>original: "intelligibilia"; things that can only be known by the mind, like justice or mathematics, rather than the senses</note>. And this also appears to be twofold. One occurs before the soul descends into the body; for then intelligence itself is turned toward the intelligible things. The other occurs after the soul has been plunged <note>original: "morsa," literally "bitten" or "gripped," likely referring to the soul being trapped in the material world</note> into this body. 

Of these, the first is properly called **intellection**. The second, however, is called **natural intelligence** rather than intellection: a certain understanding residing within that soul. Therefore, whenever we call "intellection" the principle of knowledge, we do not wish to be understood as referring to the kind we just proposed [the embodied kind], but rather to that which belongs to the soul when it is released from the body. Indeed, as we said, that state is called intellection, not natural intelligence. 

Plato calls this very thing intelligence, as well as "simple science," "pre-soul knowledge," and **reminiscence** <note>original: "reminiscentia"; the theory that learning is actually a process of "remembering" truths the soul knew before it was born into a body</note>. Finally, from these simple sciences, "natural science"—the reason inherently dwelling in the soul by nature—is forged. 

Since, therefore, reason is both "knowing" and "opining," and since there are both intellection and sensation, it is necessary that subjects exist for these faculties, such as **intelligible things** and **sensible things**. And since some intelligible things are primary—such as the **Ideas** <note>the eternal, perfect archetypes of all things in the Platonic system</note>—and some are secondary—such as the forms inherent in matter which cannot be separated from it—intellection...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on "intellection" (the mind's direct grasp of truth), transitioning from the definition of the soul's innate knowledge to how the mind distinguishes between different levels of reality—the "intelligible" world of ideas and the "sensible" world of physical objects.</meta>

...will be twofold: one is primary, the other secondary. Again,

in the same way, the primary sense belongs to primary things, 
and the secondary sense to secondary things. **Intellection** <note>original: "intellectio"; the highest form of understanding</note> judges 
**primary intelligible things** <note>original: "prima intelligibilia"; eternal truths or Platonic "Forms" like Justice or Beauty</note> not without **scientific reason** <note>original: "scientifica ratione"; logical, demonstrative knowledge</note> 
through a certain grasp and discourse. Secondary things, 
however, are judged by scientific reason not without **opinion-based reason** <note>original: "ratione opinativa"; the faculty that forms beliefs about the physical world</note> 
as a composite, or by opinion-based reason not without sensation. 
And since the **intelligible world** is the primary "intelligible thing," 
and the **sensible world** is a certain composite, the intellect—with reason—
judges the former (and not without reason). Opinion-based 
reason, not without sensation, judges the latter. And since there is 
**contemplation** as well as **action**, right reason does not discern 
in the same way those things subject to contemplation and those subject 
to action. Furthermore, in contemplation it considers what is true and 
what is false; in action, what is appropriate, what is foreign, 
what is to be done, and what is not. Because we also 
possess a **natural intelligence** <note>innate moral or aesthetic sense</note> of the Beautiful and the Good, 
using reason and referring back to these natural understandings 
as if to predefined **norms** <note>original: "normas"; standards or measuring sticks</note>, 
we judge whether individual things are good or bad.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the methods of dialectic—the tools used by the mind to categorize and understand reality. It follows the previous discussion on how the soul uses reason to judge both the physical world and the world of eternal truths.</meta>

...adjacencies, either from those things contained through **induction** <note>original: "inductionem"; the process of drawing a general conclusion from specific examples</note>, or from those contained through **reasoning**, so that it is deservedly a **fivefold division**: **division, definition, resolution, induction, and reasoning**. 

Furthermore, one kind of **division** <note>original: "divisio"; the logical process of breaking a broad concept into smaller parts</note> is the distribution of a **genus** <note>a general category, like "animal"</note> into **species** <note>specific types within a category, like "human" or "horse"</note>. Another is the division of a whole into its parts, as when we divide the soul into a **rational part** and a part lacking reason. And we further distribute this [irrational part] into the **desiring** and the **spirited** parts <note>referring to Plato's tripartite soul: the rational (logos), the spirited (thymos), and the appetitive (epithymia)</note>. 

There is, moreover, the division of a word into its meanings, which occurs whenever the same name signifies multiple things. To this is added the division of **accidents** <note>original: "accidentia"; non-essential qualities, like being "tall" or "musical"</note> into **subjects**, as when we say that among "goods," some concern the soul, others the body, and some are external things. 

Nor is there a lack of partitioning subjects into their accidents, as when we say: of men, some are good, others are bad, and some are in between. Therefore, we must first use the division of a genus into species when we wish to discern what each individual thing is. 

But this [identification] cannot happen without a **definition**, which is completed because it is composed from division in this manner: the genus of the thing to be defined must first be set forth, such as the genus of "man" being "living being" <note>original: "animatum"</note>. This genus should then be divided according to the most immediate **differences**, descending all the way to the species, such as into "rational" and "irrational," and into "mortal" and "immortal." Therefore, if these most immediate differences of the genus are added together, the definition of "man" will be established.

There are three species of **resolution** <note>original: "resolutio"; a method of analysis that works backward from a conclusion to its starting principles</note>. One moves from sensible things <note>objects perceived by the senses</note> to the primary **intelligible things** <note>pure ideas or forms</note>. The second is the approach through things already demonstrated and sub-demonstrated toward things that can be demonstrated, reaching the limit of the means and **propositions**. The last is the movement from a **supposition** <note>an assumption used as a starting point</note> to the principles which are not supposed at all, but are truly primary...

<summary>This page outlines the five core methods of logic—division, definition, resolution, induction, and reasoning—explaining how to define a concept like "man" by breaking down categories from general types to specific traits.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of dialectical methods, specifically finishing the section on definition and moving into the method of "resolution" (analysis), using the ascent of the soul and the nature of beauty as primary examples.</meta>

...**definition** <note>completing the word from the previous page</note> is of this kind: it is as if we were to transcend from the beauty of bodies to the beauty of the soul; from this to the beauty of **duties** <note>original: "officiorum"; refers to moral obligations or right actions</note>; from that finally to the beauty that is in laws; and at last to the very source of beauty, so that by ascending step by step in this manner, we may gaze upon **Beauty itself** <note>this refers to the "Ladder of Love" described in Plato’s Symposium, where the mind moves from physical attractions to divine truth</note>.

Because the form of **resolution** <note>original: "resolutio"; in logic, this is the process of "analysis," or tracing an effect back to its cause</note> is as follows: That which is sought must be assumed, and those things which are above it must be considered and demonstrated by proceeding from later things to higher things, until I arrive at a first and clearly granted principle; and starting again from there, we may return to the thing sought by the method of **composition** <note>original: "compositio"; the process of "synthesis," or building a proof from first principles downward</note>.

As if someone, asking whether the soul is **immortal**, should first assume this very thing; he would then inquire whether it is always in motion, and then demonstrate it; he would further ask whether that which is always moved is moved by itself; and he would show this again, considering whether that which is moved by itself is the **principle of motion** <note>original: "principium motus"</note>; and he would demonstrate this in turn; finally, he would inquire whether a principle is **unbegotten** <note>original: "ingenitum"; something that was not created and has no beginning</note>, which is indeed admitted by all as clear; for that which is unbegotten is **incorruptible** <note>incapable of decaying or dying</note>.

But as if from something manifest, he gathers demonstrations of this kind: a principle is unbegotten and incorruptible; the principle of motion is that which is moved by itself; that which is moved by itself is the soul; therefore the soul is incorruptible and consequently the soul is unbegotten.

**Resolution from a hypothesis** <note>original: "resolutio ex suppositione"</note> is of this kind when anyone investigates something. He should first assume that thing; then he should observe what follows from the hypothesis; after this, if an opposition...

<summary>This page explains the dialectical method of "resolution" (logical analysis), illustrating how a philosopher can prove the immortality of the soul by tracing its self-moving nature back to an unbegotten first principle.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical explanation of "resolution" (analysis) from page 24. It completes the thought on the soul's nature—specifically that a first principle must be unbegotten—before transitioning into definitions of induction, propositions, and the methods used by Plato.</meta>

...**unbegotten** <note>completing the word "ingentitum" from the previous page; a principle must be unbegotten because if it were generated, it would not be the first principle</note>. He would then give an account of the **supposition** <term>original: "suppositio"; a premise or underlying assumption</term> by assuming another supposition for its sake, to see whether that which was first supposed followed in turn from a second supposition. And so he always observes this process until he ascends to a principle that is not supposed <note>this refers to reaching an "anhypotheticon," or a non-hypothetical first principle that is self-evident</note>.

**Induction** <term>original: "inductio"; a method of reasoning that moves from specific observations to general conclusions</term>, however, is every process of reason which either passes from a similar thing to a similar thing, or from individual particulars to the whole. And this form of argumentation contributes most greatly to the awakening of natural intellects.

Of that speech which we call a **proposition**, there are two species: **affirmation** and **negation**.
An affirmation is this: "Socrates walks."
A negation is this: "Socrates does not walk."

Furthermore, of affirmations and negations, some are **universal** and some are **particular**.
A particular affirmative is this: "Some pleasure is good."
A particular negative: "Some pleasure is not good."
A universal affirmative: "Every shameful thing is evil."
A universal negative: "No shameful thing is good."

Of propositions, some are **predicative** <term>also known as categorical propositions; they state a direct relationship between a subject and a predicate, e.g., "A is B"</term> and some are **suppositive** <term>also known as hypothetical or conditional propositions, e.g., "If A, then B"</term>. Predicative ones are those which are simple, such as "every just thing is good." Suppositive ones are those that imply a consequence or a contradiction.

Plato, moreover, makes use of **reasoning** <term>original: "ratiocinatio"; the formal process of logical deduction or the syllogism</term> both in refuting and in demonstrating. He refutes through assertions <note>original: "astiones," likely a variant of "assertionibus" or "interrogationes" (questions)</note> that are false, and demonstrates through teaching which is true. **Reasoning** is a certain type of speech in which, once certain things have been posited, a conclusion follows by necessity from them. Of reasonings, some are predicative...
