<meta>This page continues the classification of logic and propositions. The previous page defined simple affirmations and negations, and introduced "predicative" (categorical) propositions. This page moves into hypothetical and mixed propositions, and then describes the three "figures" of the syllogism using examples from Plato’s dialogues.</meta>

...some are **hypothetical** <note>original: "suppositiciae"; these are "if... then" statements where one thing depends on another</note>. The rest from those which are truly combined are: **predicative** <note>also called categorical; propositions that state a direct relationship, such as "A is B"</note>, of which both the **assumptions** <term>original: "assumptiones"; the minor premises of a logical argument</term> and the conclusions are simple. Hypothetical propositions are those which are made from hypothetical propositions—**Mixed** are those which are made from both types.

Plato uses **demonstrative** <note>logical proofs intended to produce certain knowledge</note> arguments in the dialogues which pertain to exposition <note>explanatory or didactic works where Plato is laying out his actual philosophy</note>. But he uses **probable** and **apparent** arguments against the **Sophists** and against contentious youths—those who are called **eristics** <term>professional debaters who argue to win a dispute rather than to find the truth; from the Greek 'eris' meaning 'strife'</term>, such as against Euthydemus and Hippias.

While there are three **predicative figures** <term>the standard patterns of a categorical syllogism</term>:
The **First** is that in which the **middle term** <term>original: "communis terminus"; the bridge-word that appears in both premises but not the conclusion</term> is predicated of one [term] and is the subject of the other.
The **Second** is that in which the middle term is predicated of both.
The **Third** is that in which the middle term is the subject of both.

By **"term"** I mean the parts of propositions—as in "man is an animal"—we call "man" and "animal" the terms. Plato disputes for the most part according to the first figure, though he also uses the second and third figures.

According to the manner of the **first figure**, he argues thus in the *Alcibiades*:
"Just things are beautiful.
Beautiful things are good.
Therefore, just things are good." <note>This is the most direct form of reasoning, where the middle term "beautiful" links "just" to "good"</note>

According to the manner of the **second figure**, thus in the *Parmenides*:
"That which has no parts is neither straight nor round.
But whatever partakes of a shape is either straight or round.
Therefore, that which has no parts partakes of no shape."

According to the manner of the **third figure**, thus in the same book:
"That which partakes of a shape has a certain quality, and that which partakes of a shape is finite; [therefore] that which..." <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence and will continue on the next page</note>

<summary>This page explains the structure of syllogisms (logical arguments), defining hypothetical and predicative propositions. It outlines the three "figures" of logic and provides specific examples of these reasoning patterns found in Plato's dialogues, specifically the Alcibiades and Parmenides.</summary>
<keywords>syllogism, logic, Plato, Alcibiades, Parmenides, Euthydemus, Hippias, middle term, categorical figures, eristics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the structure of logical arguments (syllogisms) as found in Plato’s works. Having previously defined the three "figures" of categorical logic, the text now applies these structures to hypothetical arguments (if-then statements) using examples from the dialogues Parmenides and Phaedo.</meta>

26.

Therefore, that which has a quality is finite. Furthermore, one can frequently find arguments in Plato which are woven together through **suppositions** <note>original: "suppositiones"; these are hypothetical premises, the "if" part of a logical statement</note>. One finds reasoning of this kind especially in the *Parmenides*. If the "One" itself does not have parts, it has neither a beginning nor an end, nor does it have a middle. But if it has no beginning, middle, or end, it will have no **limit** <term>original: "terminum"; a boundary or edge that defines a shape</term>; if it has no limit, it participates in no shape. Therefore, if the One itself has no parts, it is a participant in no shape.

Regarding the **second figure** of argumentation through supposition (which very many people call the third), in which the **common term** <term>the "middle term" that links the two parts of the argument</term> follows both extremes, he reasons in this manner: if the One itself has no parts, it is neither straight nor round. If it participates in a shape, it is either straight or round. Therefore, if it has no parts, it will not even be a participant in a shape.

Then, according to the **third figure** (which some wish to be the second), in which the common term precedes both extremes, he argues in this way in the *Phaedo*: if, having once obtained knowledge of equality itself, we have never surrendered it to **oblivion** <note>forgetfulness</note>, then we certainly know it. But if we have forgotten it, then we are **remembering** <note>original: "reminiscimur"; this refers to Plato’s theory of anamnesis, where learning is actually the soul recalling knowledge from before birth</note>. 

Moreover, he uses those arguments which proceed through **consequence** <note>logical necessity; if A is true, B must follow</note> in this way: If the whole of the world exists and is finite, it will have a beginning, a middle, and an end; it also participates in shape. Since the **antecedent** <term>original: "antecedens"; the first part of a conditional "if-then" statement</term> is true, the **consequent** <term>original: "consequens"; the second part of the statement that follows from the first</term> is therefore true. Now, let us consider the differences among those things which happen by consequence in this manner.

<summary>This page demonstrates how Plato uses complex logical structures, specifically hypothetical "if-then" arguments, in his dialogues. It uses the Parmenides to discuss the nature of the "One" and the Phaedo to explain the theory of recollection.</summary>

<keywords>Plato, Parmenides, Phaedo, logic, syllogism, middle term, antecedent, consequent, anamnesis, the One</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion on the logical structures (syllogisms) found in the Phaedo and Parmenides to a broader definition of the perfect orator and the philosophical foundations of language as explored in Plato’s Cratylus.</meta>

Therefore, when anyone has carefully considered the various powers and **natures of men** <note>original: "naturas hominum"</note>, as well as the different types of arguments; then, having considered the opportunity of the moment, if he adapts his reasonings to the specific emotions of men by which they can be moved, he will be a **perfect orator** <note>original: "perfectus orator"</note>. His rhetoric will then deservedly be called the art of **speaking well** <note>original: "benedicendi"</note>.

Let us now examine the topics of **sophisms** <term>original: "sophismatum"; clever but false arguments intended to deceive</term> and deceptions by looking at those suggested by Plato in the *Euthydemus*. For in that work, one finds both the sophisms that depend on words and those that depend on the things themselves, and furthermore, their solutions are found there. He also handed down the ten **categories** <note>original: "predicamenta"; these are the fundamental classifications of being, such as substance, quality, or quantity</note> both in the *Parmenides* itself and in other dialogues. However, he encompassed all the topics derived from etymology in the *Cratylus*.

Without a doubt, our Plato stood out as a man to be admired above all others in **dividing** and **defining** <note>original: "dividendo diffiniendoque"; the logical processes of breaking down a concept into its parts and establishing its exact boundaries</note>, which are the two skills that most strongly testify to the power of **dialectic** <term>original: "dialetice"; the philosophical art of investigating the truth through logical discussion</term>.

The teachings found in the *Cratylus* tend toward this: he discusses whether **names** <note>original: "nomina"</note> hold their power by **nature** <note>original: "natura"</note> or by **convention** <note>original: "positione"; literally "by being placed" or established by human agreement</note>. It pleases him to think that the correctness of names consists in a certain convention, yet not rashly or merely as chance has offered it. Rather, he believes that the convention itself followed the nature of things. Nor is the correctness of names anything other than an assignment that is in harmony with the nature of the thing itself. Furthermore, neither is just any assignment of names sufficient for correctness, nor, on the contrary, is the natural and first utterance of the voice alone enough.

<summary>This page defines the perfect orator as one who adapts logic to human psychology and introduces Plato’s linguistic theory from the Cratylus regarding whether language is natural or conventional.</summary>
<keywords>Rhetoric, Plato, Euthydemus, Cratylus, Sophisms, Dialectic, Etymology, Nature vs. Convention, Philology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Plato’s Cratylus, moving from the general introduction of the dialogue to the specific philosophical argument regarding the "correctness of names" (rectitudo nominum). It explores the idea that names are not arbitrary but must reflect the essential nature of the objects they describe.</meta>

...but that which is composed of both, so that every **name** <note>original: "nomen"</note> possesses a certain **correctness** <note>original: "rectitudo"</note> according to an assignment that agrees with the nature of things. For a name does not signify correctly if any random word is applied thoughtlessly to anything—as if, for instance, we were to apply the name "horse" to a man.

Indeed, speaking is one of our human operations; therefore, not everyone who speaks does so correctly, but only if they speak in the way the nature of the thing itself requires. Since naming is a specific part of speaking—just as a name is a part of a sentence—naming correctly or incorrectly is not achieved through any arbitrary assignment, but through the **natural kinship** <note>original: "naturalem cognationem"</note> of the name to the thing itself.

He will be a **correct founder of names** <term>original: "rectus nominis conditor"; in Platonic thought, this refers to the "Legislator" or "Name-giver" who first assigned words to realities</term> who signifies the nature of the thing through the name. For a name is an **organ** <term>original: "organum"; here used in the Greek sense of a "tool" or "functional instrument"</term> of a thing; not just whichever one chance provided, but that which is congruent with nature.

Through this instrument, in turn, we judge things for ourselves. When we discern, it follows that a name is a certain **judging and distributing instrument** <note>original: "instrumentum iudicans atque distribuens"</note> of the **substance** <term>original: "substantiam"; the essential reality or "what-ness" of a thing</term> of each thing, just as the comb is to the weaver’s cloth.

It also pertains to the **dialectician** <term>original: "dialecticum"; a philosopher skilled in the art of logical discussion and division</term> to use names correctly, just as the weaver uses the comb, knowing his own proper work even though a craftsman made the tool. In the same way, the dialectician will use the name that the "founder of names" has fashioned, as is fitting. For indeed, it is the craftsman's duty to build the oar, but the duty of the **pilot** <note>original: "gubernatoris"; the navigator or helmsman of a ship</note> to use it correctly. In the same way, the establisher of names will establish names correctly if...

<summary>This page discusses the Platonic theory of language from the Cratylus, arguing that names are functional tools (instruments) that must naturally fit the essence of the things they name. It compares the "name-giver" to a tool-maker and the "dialectician" to the skilled user (like a pilot or weaver) who employs those names to discern reality.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "correctness of names" (rectitudo nominum) in Plato's Cratylus, transitioning from the role of the dialectician to the broader structure of speculative philosophy. It establishes how specific disciplines—theology, physics, and mathematics—serve as a ladder to elevate human understanding from the physical world to divine truths.</meta>

...beheld by the present **dialectician** <term>original: "dialectico"; a philosopher skilled in the art of logical investigation and the pursuit of truth through reason</term>: since indeed the dialectician understands the nature of those things which lie hidden under a name. And let these things said about **dialectic** <note>original: "dialectica"</note> suffice; next, we must speak concerning **speculative philosophy** <term>original: "speculativa philosophia"; the branch of philosophy concerned with theoretical knowledge and the contemplation of truth for its own sake</term>. We say its members are **theology**, **physics**, and **mathematics**.

The goal of the **theologian** <note>original: "theologi finis"</note> is the knowledge of supernatural things. The goal of the **physicist** <note>original: "physici"</note> is to learn those things through the nature of the world: such as the nature of man, what point he occupies in the world, whether God provides for all things, and... the rest of the matters served by this subject; and also how men might be blessed in relation to the gods.

**Mathematics** <note>original: "Mathematica"</note> teaches us first to know the nature of planes and dimensions extended between them, and to look up at the motion and progress of the celestial bodies. And thus, in a certain summary, mathematical speculation is proposed. This was surely taken up by **Plato** <note>original: "Platone"</note> for the sharpening of thought, so that it might inspire the soul and sharpen the edge of the mind for the intuition of divine things.

Moreover, that part of the mathematical faculty which pertains to **numbers** <note>original: "numeros"</note> provides no small power for perceiving divine matters; indeed, it frees us from error and the ignorance that revolves around these **sensible things** <note>original: "sensibilia"; things that can be perceived through the physical senses, which Platonic thought often views as mere shadows of a higher reality</note>, bringing aid to the knowledge of **essence** <term>original: "essentiae"; the fundamental "what-ness" or true being of a thing</term>, and rendering a person ready for certainty because of their skill in the constancy and order of things.

**Geometry** <note>original: "Geometria"</note> seems to contribute the most toward understanding the **Good itself** <note>original: "ipsum bonum"; the supreme Platonic principle, the source of all truth, beauty, and existence</note>: one does not pursue it for the sake of mechanical measurement, but uses it in such a way that, by its assistance, one might ascend to higher things, and not [dwell] in the measurement of things that arise and perish...

<summary>This page outlines the divisions of speculative philosophy (theology, physics, and mathematics) and argues that these disciplines, particularly geometry and arithmetic, are essential tools for sharpening the mind to perceive divine truths beyond the physical world.</summary>
<keywords>speculative philosophy, theology, physics, mathematics, Plato, geometry, essence, the Good, dialectician</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on mathematics as a tool for sharpening the mind, specifically detailing the components of the Quadrivium—the four mathematical arts—and their role in elevating the soul from the physical world to the contemplation of divine essence.</meta>

<page-num>14.</page-num>

...contemplation <note>original: "tionē"; completing the word "contemplatione" from the previous page</note> <unclear>and</unclear> sets it firm. Indeed, even **Stereometry** <term>original: "Stereometria"; the mathematical study of three-dimensional objects or solid bodies</term>, that is, the measurement of solids, is extremely useful. For after the second increase <note>referring to the transition from the second dimension (planes) to the third</note>, its consideration follows, looking toward a third increase. Furthermore, there comes as if a certain fourth discipline: it is **Astronomy**, through which we behold the movements of the heavens and stars, and the author of day and night, and of months and of years. From these, as if by a certain compass <note>original: "pyxia"; literally a box or a container, here likely referring to a navigational instrument used for orientation</note> of the region, we track down the author of all these things; and from these disciplines, as if from certain benches <note>original: "subsellijs"; referring to the seating in a school or lecture hall, symbolizing foundational stages of learning</note>, elements, and steps, we progress toward higher things. It is necessary for us to learn **Music** <gloss>likewise</gloss>, applying our sense of hearing to it. For just as the eyes were created for astronomy, so the ears were made for harmony: so that, just as when we are occupied with astronomy, we are led from those things perceived by the eyes to an invisible essence; so also, drawing in the harmony of voices with our ears, we gradually approach intelligible things <note>intellectual truths that exist beyond the physical senses</note> from those things which they hear. Wherefore, unless we follow the mathematical arts for the sake of this end, their duty is surely considered a matter of mere curiosity, empty and of no value. For it is proper to cross over immediately from those things perceived by the eyes and ears to those things which reason alone discerns. For the consideration of **mathematical matters** is a certain prelude to the **contemplation of divine things**. **Arithmetic** and **Geometry**, and those who cultivate them, desire to see essence itself; nevertheless, they dream about those things in a certain way, nor [do they see] it in truth...

<summary>This page outlines the Platonic view of the Quadrivium—Stereometry, Astronomy, Music, Arithmetic, and Geometry—explaining how these sciences transition the human mind from sensory observation to the rational understanding of divine truths. It emphasizes that mathematical study is not merely for curiosity but is a necessary "prelude" to higher spiritual contemplation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the mathematical arts, specifically focusing on how they bridge the gap between physical perception and the higher intellectual grasp of divine truths, transitioning from the Quadrivium into the study of Dialectic and the nature of Matter.</meta>

<margin>5</margin>
...can discern. And they are ignorant of the principles and those things which are constituted from the principles. Nevertheless, they truly contribute to those things we have spoken of. Therefore, Plato judged that disciplines of this sort should not be called **science** <note>original: "scias" (scientias); here meaning certain, absolute knowledge or *episteme*</note>. But the **faculty of dialectic** <term>original: "dialectica facultas"; the method of philosophical inquiry that moves beyond assumptions to reach the ultimate truth of things</term> rises above geometrical suppositions toward the first principles, which are by no means presupposed. Wherefore he rightly called dialectic "science." He called the mathematical discipline neither **opinion** <note>original: "opinionē"; the lower level of understanding based on sensory perception</note>—because mathematical truths are clearer than sensible things—nor again "science," because they are more obscure than the primary intelligible things. Instead, he thinks that by the reasoning of mathematics, something is known that lies between bodily opinion and divine science; furthermore, he attributes trust, conjecture, and imagination to images and idols <note>original: "idolis"; referring to the shadows or reflections of true forms in the physical world</note>. Since dialectic is indeed more effective than mathematics because it is concerned with divine and eternal things, he therefore places it before all mathematical arts like a certain citadel and guardian of the rest.

---

<margin>4</margin>
<margin>Matter.</margin>
After these things, let us briefly discuss the principles and those things which pertain to **theology**, beginning from the primary things and descending from there to the generation of the world itself, and finally even to the birth of humans. First, therefore, we must treat of **matter**. This he calls the receptacle of all forms, the nurse, and the mother. He names it "Place" and the "Subject," and asserts that it is touched without the aid of the senses and is comprehended by a **bastard reasoning** <term>original: "adulterina ratiocinatione"; a translation of Plato's 'logismos nothos', referring to the way we conceptualize matter, which has no form of its own and thus cannot be 'seen' by the mind or the eye</term>. He also wishes it to be characteristic of this matter to receive all generations and to stand as the material...

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the fundamental principles of reality. Having established dialectic as the highest science on the previous page, the author now begins a detailed examination of **matter** (materia) and its relationship to **form** and the **divine mind**.</meta>

...it fosters them. It is capable of receiving all forms because, by its own nature, it is distinct from forms and is devoid of quality and species. Furthermore, when it is observed and shaped by forms, he asserts it is like an **effigy** <note>original: "effigiem"; a likeness or representation</note>, participating in no prior shape or quality. Indeed, a subject would not be suitably prepared for the various impressions of forms unless it entirely lacked every quality which it is intended to receive. 

For we see those who strive to prepare sweet-smelling ointments from oil seek oil that is completely untainted by any scent. And those who are about to impress forms, whether into wax or clay, first thoroughly polish and smooth the material itself until the former shapes are deleted. For it is appropriate to **universal matter** <note>the underlying "stuff" of the universe before it is given specific properties</note>, if it is to receive all forms, to have the nature of none of them, but without any quality or species, to be subjected to species. And since it is of this kind, it will be neither a body nor indeed incorporeal, but **body in potentiality** <note>original: "potentia corpus"; it has the capacity to become a physical thing but is not yet one</note>, just as bronze is potentially a statue, because by receiving the form it becomes a statue. 

Since indeed matter has the character of a certain principle, <margin>What an Idea is: the intellection of God</margin> Plato introduces other principles alongside matter. Namely, the first **exemplar** <note>original: "exemplar"; the perfect original model or pattern</note> to which the **Ideas** themselves are reduced, and the paternal principle which pertains to God, the creator of all things. 

<margin>Idea</margin> 
Ideas were, in relation to God, the **intellection of God** <note>original: "intellectio dei"; the internal thoughts or understanding of the Divine mind</note>. In relation to the sensible world, they are the **exemplar**; in relation to themselves, they are **essence** <note>original: "essentia"; the fundamental nature of what a thing is</note>. For whatever an intelligence may be, it must be referred to something; and that work is the exemplar.

<summary>This page defines matter as a quality-less "receptacle" that must be blank to receive forms, and introduces Plato's Three Principles: Matter, the Exemplar (Ideas), and God (the Father/Creator).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the principles of reality, moving from the definition of matter to the definition and nature of the "Idea." It explores why Plato and his followers believed in eternal forms as the thoughts of God, specifically excluding man-made objects and imperfections from this divine realm.</meta>

...it will be. Just as if something is made by someone—as, for instance, an image of myself. And even if the **exemplar** <note>original: "exemplar"; the perfect original model or pattern</note> is not entirely separate from the maker, just as any craftsman conceives the models of his works within himself, and then expresses the forms of these same things into

<margin>Plato's Idea.</margin>
matter. They define an **Idea** <note>original: "ideam"; here referring to the transcendent, perfect blueprint of a thing</note> as the eternal exemplar of those things that come to be according to nature. For many of those who followed Plato, it was not at all acceptable that an Idea of artificial objects should exist, such as for a shield or a lyre; nor likewise of those things that are contrary to **nature** <note>original: "naturam"; the inherent order of the world</note>, such as diseases and the vices of the intemperate; nor of individual people, such as Socrates and Plato; nor even of any of those things that are imperfect, such as filth or potsherds; nor of relations, such as "the greater" or "the exceeding." For they hold that Ideas are the eternal **intellections** <note>original: "intellectiones"; acts of the mind or divine thoughts</note> of God, perfect in themselves.

That Ideas exist, they prove in this way: Whether God himself is the Intellect, or some intellectual being, eternal and immobile notions are present within him. If this is so, then Ideas exist. And even if nature in itself lacks matter, it must receive its measures from another, superior source that is entirely free from matter. The first part of the argument is true; therefore, the conclusion follows. And if this is so, Ideas exist, standing apart without any mixture of matter. It follows that

<margin>The World.</margin>
the world did not come into the light by some rash accident; it was not only made *from* something, but was also made *by* someone. Nor that alone, but it was fabricated *according to* something. That thing, indeed, according to which it was made...

<summary>This page defines the Platonic "Idea" as the eternal, divine thought of God and argues that these Ideas serve as the blueprints for the natural world. It clarifies that while natural species have Ideas, man-made objects, individuals, and defects do not.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical proof for the existence of "Ideas" (Forms) and transitions into a discussion of the "First Principle" or the Primary God. It contrasts human cognition, which is clouded by sensory images, with divine intellection, which is pure and instantaneous.</meta>

...that nature exists as something other than the **Idea** <note>original: "ideam"; the perfect, non-physical blueprint of a thing</note>. From this it follows that the Ideas themselves exist. Likewise, if the **intellect** <note>original: "intellectus"; the faculty of reason and understanding</note> differs from **opinion** <note>original: "opinione"; subjective belief or sensory-based judgment</note>, then that which is **intelligible** <note>original: "itelligibile"; things known by the mind alone</note> will also differ from that which is **opinable** <note>original: "opinabili"; things that are the subject of mere opinion</note>. This is its own truth: there exist "intelligibles" distinct from "opinables," so that there are primary intelligibles just as there are primary **sensibles** <note>original: "sensibilia"; things perceived by the five senses</note>. From this, the existence of Ideas is concluded.

### Next, the uncovering of the Principle must be discussed.

That which Plato declares to be almost inexpressible, let us attempt to demonstrate in this manner. If the "intelligibles" are those things that are neither "sensibles" nor coherent with them, but rather there exist primary, simple intelligibles standing above the first intelligibles—just as there are primary sensibles—then the premise is true. Therefore, what follows is also true. Humans, being occupied by the disturbance of the senses, when they resolve to contemplate a certain "intelligible," they immediately return to a **phantasm** <note>original: "phantasiam"; a mental image or picture</note> of some "sensible" thing. To such an extent do they first apply either magnitude, or shape, or color to that which is to be understood, that it results in them being unable to understand the thing purely. But the Gods, without the mixture of bodily things, understand with extreme purity and sincerity. For no nature is better than the intellect. 

Indeed, that intellect which always understands all things simultaneously **in actuality** <note>original: "actu"; the state of being fully realized or active</note> is better than an intellect which is only **in potentiality** <note>original: "potentia"; having the capacity for thought but not currently exercising it</note>. Finally, this most beautiful thing, which is the cause of [the intellect] and which stands above all things: this is surely the **First God** <note>original: "deus ipse primus"</note> himself. And He is the cause that moves the world.

<summary>This page argues that because the mind is distinct from mere opinion, there must exist "Ideas" that the mind can perceive. It also defines the First God as the supreme, actualized intellect that remains unclouded by the sensory images (color, shape, size) that hamper human thought.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Ideas, Forms, Intellect, Opinion, Intelligible, Sensible, First Principle, Actualization, Potentiality, Phantasm</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the "First Principle" or God, specifically how this divine intellect moves the universe without being moved itself. It bridges the transition from the previous page's discussion on human cognition and the existence of Ideas into a theological description of the Supreme Being.</meta>

...the **mind** <note>original: "mens"; refers to the catchword "mens" from the previous page</note> always acts: I say it is always immobile itself, in exactly the same way as the sun acts upon the eye presented to it. And it moves things in that way by which that which is desired—being itself immobile—stirs the one who desires it. And in this manner, the supreme mind itself leads the mind of the whole heaven. And since this first **intellect** <note>original: "intellectus"; the highest faculty of reason</note> is most beautiful, it is necessary that the most beautiful **intelligible** <note>original: "itelligibile"; an object of pure thought, not of the senses</note> be subject to it. But nothing is more beautiful than it. Therefore, it always understands itself and its own notions.
<margin>God</margin>
And this operation of it is the **Idea** <note>original: "idea"; here meaning the divine blueprint or thought</note>. Furthermore, God himself is supreme, eternal, ineffable, perfect in himself, lacking nothing, and always and everywhere absolutely perfect. He exists as the principle of the essence of divinity: Truth, Harmony, and the **Good** <note>original: "bonū"; the ultimate Platonic principle</note>. Nor indeed are these things named by number in such a way that I would distinguish them from one another. Rather, I contemplate all of them together as one.
<margin>The Good.</margin>
He is certainly called "The Good" for this reason: because he fills all things by his benefits out of the poverty of each, and is the cause of all good things. And he is called "The Beautiful" because he himself exists as the very fullness and harmony of all things.
<margin>Truth.</margin>
And he is called "The True" because he is the principle of all truth, just as the sun is the principle of universal light.
<margin>Father.</margin>
He is also the "Father" because he is the source of all being: he adorns the mind of the whole heaven and the **soul of the world** <note>original: "mundi animam"; the "Anima Mundi" or the animating force of the universe</note> according to the pattern of himself. For by the gift of his will, he filled all things with himself, awakening the world to life and turning it toward himself, especially since he is the cause of its mind. And the mind itself is adorned by the benefit of the Father, for he adorns every mind of this kind.
<margin>Ineffable</margin>
Moreover, he is ineffable and alone...

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of the "First Principle" or God. Following the previous page’s description of God as the supreme "Idea" and the "Good," this section employs the method of negative theology—defining God by what He is not—and uses the famous Platonic analogy of the Sun to explain how the divine mind illuminates the world of thought.</meta>

<page-num>22.</page-num>

<margin>God</margin>
God is to be perceived by the **mind** <note>original: "mente"</note> for this reason: because there is neither genus around him, nor species, nor any difference, nor does any **accident** <term>accident: a philosophical term for a non-essential quality or property that can change without changing the essence of a thing</term> happen to him. For it would be a sin even to say he was evil. Nor indeed is he "good" in the sense that he would exist as something perfected by the **participation** <note>original: "participatione"; a Platonic concept where things have qualities only by "sharing" in a higher Form</note> of some other goodness.

<margin>-</margin>
Nor does any difference happen to him. And yet, he is not thereby "formless," for he is not deprived of any quality pertaining to himself. He is not a part of anything, nor is he a whole as if composed of parts.

<margin>~</margin>
Nor is he the same as anything else, nor different from another, because nothing happens to him by which he could be distinguished from others. He neither moves, nor is he moved. Whence the first understanding of him is through the **removal** <note>original: "ablationem"; the method of negative theology (apophasis), where one understands the divine by stripping away all limited human concepts</note> of all these things: just as we conceive of a **point** through removal—considering first the surface, then the line, and finally the point itself. The second way of understanding him is through the method of **proportions** <note>original: "proportionum rationem"; an analogy comparing two relationships</note> in this manner: Whatever relationship the sun has to sight and to those things which are seen—without being sight itself—providing to the one the power to see, and to the others the ability to be seen; exactly that same relationship the **first intellect** <note>original: "intellectus primus"</note> has to the **intelligence** <note>original: "intelligentiam"</note> that is present, and to those things which are understood. For he himself is not the same as the act of intelligence, but he extends the power of understanding to some so that they may understand, and to the **intelligibles** <term>intelligibles: objects of pure thought or abstract truths, as opposed to objects perceived by the physical senses</term> he provides the truth that is within them so that they may be understood.

<summary>This page describes God through negative theology, arguing that He transcends categories like genus, species, or physical movement. It concludes with an analogy comparing God's intellect to the sun, which provides the "light" of truth and the power of understanding to the mind.</summary>

<keywords>negative theology, apophasis, First Intellect, analogy of the sun, divine simplicity, intelligibles, participation, logic</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "ways of understanding" the Divine essence. Having previously discussed the method of removal (negative theology) and the method of proportions, the text now introduces the "third way," which is the Platonic ascent from physical beauty to absolute, incorporeal Beauty and the Good. It also begins a logical proof for God's incorporeality based on the impossibility of change.</meta>

The third way of understanding illuminates [the mind] in this manner. Observe: if someone directs themselves to the **beauty** <note>original: "pulchritudinem"</note> which is present in the body; then toward that beauty found in duties and laws; and finally to the very source of beauty itself, above which he perceives that the **Good** <note>original: "bonum"</note> itself is to be loved and sought. Just like a splendor, as if something suddenly flashing forth from that place, he recognizes that this light <gloss>light</gloss> <note>original: "lum[en]"</note> is God himself because of His excellence.

And he recognizes Him, first and foremost, as being without the magnitude of quantity. For a part, and that from which something is composed, always precedes the thing of which it is a part. Indeed, a plane exists prior to a body, and a line prior to a plane. Since, however, He has no parts, He is moved neither in terms of location nor in terms of alteration. For if He were to undergo change, He would necessarily be changed either by Himself or by something else. If by another, that other would surely be more powerful. If by Himself, He would transition either into something worse or something better. Both of these scenarios are impossible. 

From all these things, it is clear that He is **incorporeal** <term>incorporeal: having no physical body or material form</term>. Moreover, the same will be evident from what follows: if God were a body, He would consist of **matter** and **form** <term>form: translated from 'species', referring to the essential nature or 'blueprints' that give matter its specific identity</term>. This is because every body is a certain composite of both matter and form at the same time, which renders it similar to the idea, while [He acts] upon them by a certain ineffable reason...

<summary>This page describes the 'third way' of knowing God—the ascent from physical beauty to the source of all beauty—and argues that God must be incorporeal and changeless, as any change would imply He is either imperfect or subject to a higher power.</summary>
<keywords>ascent of beauty, the Good, incorporeality, divine immutability, matter and form, Neo-Platonism, theology</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical demonstration of God’s nature, specifically focusing on why the Divine must be incorporeal. The previous page established that God is moved neither by location nor alteration; this page extends that argument by examining the relationship between matter, form, and qualities.</meta>

participates. Moreover, it is absurd to think that God is constituted from matter and form <note>original: "specie"</note>.
<margin>God. Incorporeal.</margin> For if He were, He would be neither simple nor a first principle. For this reason, God is incorporeal. Furthermore, if He were a body, He would consist of matter. And for that reason, He would be either fire, or air, or earth, or water, or something forged <note>original: "conflatum"</note> from these elements. But in whatever way He might be disposed, He would not be a first principle at all; indeed, He would be posterior to matter itself, if He is built out of matter. Since these conclusions are impious <note>original: "nefaria"</note>, it is necessary that God be incorporeal. For indeed, if He were a body, He could be corrupted, He would have a beginning <note>original: "genitus"</note>, and He would be subject to change. Yet none of these things apply to God.

<margin>body quality</margin> Moreover, we shall demonstrate that qualities are incorporeal in the following way. Every body is a subject <term>subject: or substrate; the underlying substance or "thing" in which properties exist</term>. Quality is by no means a subject, but rather an accident <term>accident: a property that exists only within a substance, such as color or heat, rather than existing on its own</term>; therefore, a quality is not a body. No body exists *in* a subject; yet every quality exists *in* a subject. Therefore, a quality is not a body. Likewise, one quality can be the contrary of another quality. But no body is the contrary of another body, simply by virtue of being a body. Therefore, qualities are not bodies. Indeed, it is also most consistent with reason that just as matter is devoid of quality, so too should quality be devoid of matter. And if it were to lack matter, it could not be a body. But if qualities were bodies, then two or three bodies would occupy the same space at once, which is a notion far removed from the truth. But if qualities are in a body, and the Craftsman <note>original: "opifex"; referring to the divine architect or creator</note>...

<summary>This page provides a logical proof for God's incorporeality, arguing that if God were material, He would be subject to change, corruption, and would be secondary to matter itself. It also distinguishes between 'bodies' (substances) and 'qualities' (accidents), asserting that qualities cannot be physical bodies.</summary>

<keywords>incorporeality of God, matter and form, four elements, accidents and subjects, divine simplicity, first principle, opifex, Aristotelian logic</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the philosophical argument regarding the incorporeal nature of God. It marks a structural transition in the manuscript from "Theology" (the study of first principles and the Divine) to "Physics" (the study of the natural, material world). The text establishes that while bodies are subject to change and passivity, the active force behind creation must be incorporeal and based on eternal "Ideas."</meta>

...[He] is incorporeal. Furthermore, no active forces <note>original: "agentia"</note> could exist except for those which are incorporeal. This is because bodies are passive <term>passive: in this philosophical sense, 'passive' means capable of being acted upon or changed by an outside force</term> and fleeting, neither always remaining in the same manner nor persisting in the same state; and in those things where they might seem to produce an effect upon others, they will be found much more to be passive in that regard. Therefore, just as there exists something that acts entirely, we shall find that this is nothing other than the incorporeal.

The discourse on the principles of things, which is called **theology**, has thus now reached its end. For next, it seems we must descend to that discussion which is named **physics** <term>physics: derived from the Greek word for 'nature'; here it refers to 'natural philosophy' or the study of the physical universe</term>.

<margin>
The world,
the most beautiful
handiwork
of God
</margin>
For all **BODILY THINGS** which are distinct according to nature and which act upon one another, there must be certain pre-defined **models of the Ideas** <note>original: "exemplaria idearū"</note>, and knowledge and definitions must be referred back to them. For beyond individual men, it is right to understand "Man" himself; and beyond individual horses, the "Horse" itself; and beyond all created animals, one immortal and uncreated Animal <note>The author is likely referring to the Platonic concept of the 'Cosmos' as a single, living, divine being</note>. Just as many images are pressed from a single seal, and many images of a single man may exist—for all of whom the Idea itself is the reason they are of such a kind as the Idea is itself—so it appears that this entire world, the most beautiful handiwork of God, must necessarily have been so fashioned by God Himself.

Indeed, while the heavens were being generated, this matter was tossing about without any order; God called it back from this shapeless confusion into a beautiful order. page 25.

...provides. For by these elements matter is shaken, and these in turn are moved by matter. Therefore, he allowed us to investigate the powers from our own bodies. For since we discern individual things with the soul <note>original: *anima*</note>, he rightly instilled the principles of all things into it, so that whatever might ever be encountered, we might recognize it through a certain kinship and apply to those things an essence harmonious with them. Therefore, since he says that there is a certain intelligible and indivisible substance <term>substance: the essential reality or "stuff" of a thing</term>, and another which he described as being dispersed throughout bodies, he claimed that the soul can grasp both substances through the whole of its intelligence. 

And because he recognized that in those things pertaining to intelligence, as well as in those pertaining to sense itself, there exists "the Same" and "the Other" <note>original: *idem atque ipsum alterum*; these are the fundamental categories Plato used in his work, the *Timaeus*, to explain how the soul can understand both unchanging truths and changing physical things</note>, he rightly constituted the soul from all of these. For either "like is recognized by like," as the **Pythagoreans** <note>followers of the philosopher Pythagoras, who believed the universe was structured by numbers</note> believe, or as **Heraclitus the Physicist** <note>an early Greek philosopher famous for the idea that "everything flows"</note> held, "the unlike is always recognized by the unlike." 

When indeed **Plato** says the world was "begotten" <note>original: *genitum*; meaning created or having a beginning</note>, it should by no means be believed that he felt some time once preceded the world, nor that it [the world] persists forever in a state of becoming <note>original: *gnatione*; the process of generation or birth</note>, indicating a cause for its substance and something prior to it. Moreover, regarding the **Soul of the World** <note>original: *Anima mundi*; the spirit believed by some ancient philosophers to animate the entire universe</note> which has always existed: God does not "make" it, but rather adorns it. In that sense, he [Plato] asserted that God limits it, because He awakens it and turns its mind toward Himself as if from a certain deep sleep, so that looking toward those things that are understood, they might strive for divine notions.

...the **founders of the laws** <note>original: *Legum conditores*; referring to the lesser gods who act as celestial legislators</note> explained the fatal laws to them, so that he himself <note>the Creator/Demiurge</note> might remain free from the blame of their future transgressions. Those souls who should overcome these passions, and not allow themselves to be led by them, would live justly; and they would finally return to their **kindred star**. 

But those who were overcome by injustice would, in their second birth, migrate into the life of a **woman**. And those who did not yet cease from wickedness would finally degenerate into the nature of **brutes** <note>original: *brutorum*; wild animals or unreasoning beasts</note>. # 28.

...two [elements], fire and water, might more frequently irrigate the **sinews** <note>original: *nervos*; in ancient and medieval anatomy, this term often referred to tendons and ligaments as well as nerves</note>, and they arise from the bone and protect it. **Flesh**, however, is compacted from a certain salty and sharp fermentation, as it were. And when they gave marrow to the bones, they also provided sinews for the sake of the joining of the bones. Whence, by the obedience of the sinews, they may perform flexions. 

The joints emerged from the long bones. Finally, the covering of the **skin** was added to them, which was handed down as both white and abundant, for the sake of both beauty and utility. Furthermore, from these things the internal **organs** were filled, and the **intestines** were wound all around it. 

Above, however, from the mouth, extend the **artery** <note>original: *arteria*; here referring to the trachea or windpipe</note> and the pipe of the throat; one indeed leading into the **stomach**, the other however into the **lungs**. Foods are surely digested in the belly, being worn down and softened by breath and heat together, and from there they flow into the whole body through their own proper changes. 

Moreover, two **veins** erected around the spine entwine the head in a contrary manner, meeting one another. These divide into many branches. After the gods fashioned man and joined the **divine soul** to the body, they commanded that its principal seat be held in the **head**. From this, the origin of the marrow and the sinews is drawn; for when the head suffers various passions, an **alienation of the mind** <note>original: *mentis alienatione*; a state of mental derangement or loss of reason</note> follows. Wherefore, they fortified the **citadel of the body** <note>original: *corporis arcem*; a classic Platonic metaphor for the head as the seat of reason</note> itself with the external senses placed around it, as if with certain guards. For in that place dwells that power which reasons, judges, and contemplates. But truly the parts [of the soul] and to that which...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the body's organization according to Platonic physiology. It moves from the head as the seat of the divine soul to the placement of the "lower" passions and the mechanism of the senses, specifically sight.</meta>

...are moved by passions, they assigned them to lower seats. They placed the power of **wrath** <note>original: *irascendi*</note> in the **heart**, but the power of **desire** in those parts which are around the **navel** <note>original: *umbilicum*</note>, about which we shall discuss in the later sections of this work. 

But after they sculpted **light-bearing eyes** in the face, they caused fire to kindle within them. Since this fire was gentle and in a certain way dense, they considered it a relative of the **light of day** <note>original: *diurni luminis*</note>. This light surely flows out everywhere through the eyes, but it runs through the **pupils** <note>original: *pupillas*</note> more purely and more abundantly. When this internal light, being similar to the external brightness, is mixed with its like and reinforced, it provides the **sense of sight**. 

Therefore, at night, when the light has departed or is shaded, or when the rays are driven back, this internal light is not mixed with any nearby fire. From this, it happens that the light, withdrawing into the interior, soothes and relaxes our internal motions and sets the measure for **sleep** <note>original: *somnus*</note>; there, it also closes the eyelids once it has been retracted. And <insert>if</insert> sleep has induced a very great rest, it creeps in with little dreaming; but if some agitations still remain, frequent **apparitions** <note>original: *fantasmata*; these are the mental images or "phantasms" that form the basis of dreams</note> immediately encounter us. 

And in this way, fantasies—whether true or false—are born from a certain direct path. Following these are the **images** which are seen in **mirrors** <note>original: *speculum*</note> or other translucent and smooth bodies, which indeed result from nothing other than **reflection** <note>original: *reverberationem*</note>. For according to whether the mirror is **concave**, **convex**, or **oblong**, [the image changes].

29.

To that extent, the things seen will appear different to those watching them. For the **lights** will be reflected toward other parts, being repelled and cast down by a **convex** <note>original: *convexo*; a surface that curves outward</note> surface, but coming together in a **concave** <note>original: *concavum*; a surface that curves inward</note> one. For this reason, in certain mirrors, right and left appear reversed; in others, things that are low or turned backward are converted so that the upper parts appear lower.

**Hearing**, moreover, is established for the **perception of the voice** <note>original: *vocis perceptionem*</note>, arising from a motion that is produced around the head and reaches as far as the ear. The **voice**, however, penetrates through the ears, the brain, and the blood until it reaches the **soul** <note>original: *animam*</note>. High-pitched sounds move more quickly, while deep sounds move more slowly; loud sounds move with great force, and small sounds with very little.

Following these is the power of the **nostrils**, produced for the **perception of smell**. Now, **smell** is a certain <term>passion</term> <note>original: *passio*; here meaning a physical experience or "affection" felt by the body</note> flowing through the veins of the nostrils even down to the regions of the **navel** <note>original: *umbillici*</note>. The types of smell have no specific names, except for two common categories. These are none other than **good smell** and **bad smell**, which are also known by the names **pleasantness** and **nausea**. 

Certainly, every odor is thicker than air but thinner than water. Specifically, this kind of odor occurs in substances that have not yet undergone a complete transformation, but instead consist of a certain middle state between air and water. Such are the smells produced from **smoke** and **mist** <note>original: *fumo et calligine*</note>. For the sense of smelling is produced by the mutual transformation of these elements.

Regarding **Taste**, the gods formed it to serve as a variety of <term>dyes</term> <note>original: *fucorum*; literally "tinctures" or "pigments," used here metaphorically to describe the different "colors" of flavor</note>, extending the veins...

<meta>This page continues the exploration of sensory perception, moving from the sense of smell discussed at the end of the previous page to a detailed analysis of the sense of taste and the tactile qualities of bodies (touch), following the Platonic division of the four elements and their physical effects on the body.</meta>

...from that source to that same seat [of the tongue], by which different liquids are examined; for these are discerned as they are entangled and unraveled according to the variety of their **tridents** <note>original: *tridentium*; in Platonic physics, these are the tiny, three-pointed particles that prick or stimulate the tongue</note>. 

There are, moreover, seven categories of tastes: **sweet**, **sharp**, **pungent**, **harsh**, **salty**, **acute**, and **bitter**. 

**Sweetness**, indeed, is by nature the opposite of all the rest. For it spreads the moisture absorbed by the tongue, soothing, softening, and dispersing it. In contrast, the **acute** <note>original: *acutus*; refers to a sharp, burning sensation like that of spicy or acidic things</note> taste inflames and flies upward to the higher parts; that which violently scours and liquefies is called **bitter**. But that which cleanses and softens more gently is called **salty**. 

Among those tastes which block the passages of the tongue and contract them, those that do so more harshly are called **pungent** <note>original: *ponticus*</note>; those that do so more mildly are called **harsh** <note>original: *austerus*; often used of dry wines or unripe fruit</note>.

And now finally, from these arises the sense through which they may distinguish **hot** and **cold**, **soft** and **hard**, **light** and **heavy**, and **smooth** and **rough**. 

We call **yielding** those bodies which give way to touch. Those which do not yield at all are **resistant**; this occurs because of the "foundations" of the bodies themselves. For those that have wide foundations are stable and solid; conversely, those that use a small foundation are yielding, soft, and slippery. 

**Rough** is surely that which is uneven and hard; **smooth** is that which is full and rich <note>original: *pingue*; literally "fatty" or "oily," describing a dense, even surface</note>. Since, indeed, the <term>affections</term> <note>original: *passio*; the way the body is acted upon or moved by an external quality</note> of hot and cold are experienced in a certain measure, from various causes...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of tactile sensations from the previous page, specifically explaining the physical mechanisms of heat and cold. It then transitions into Platonic physics regarding weight and the nature of the cosmos, followed by a theory of respiration and the origins of disease, following the Timaeus.</meta>

...are gathered. For that which, through the sharpness and velocity of the spirit <note>original: *spirituum*; here referring to the fine, moving particles of air or heat</note>, produces a hot sensation. Cold, on the other hand, is that which enters more sluggishly; while the finer parts are expelled, the thicker parts are forced to seek their places. Then, from this constriction, a trembling arises; this sensation born in our bodies is what we call **rigor** <note>shivering or a cold chill</note>.

It is not proper to define **heavy** and **light** by the concepts of "higher" and "lower" locations. Indeed, nothing is truly "up" or "down." For since the heavens are entirely round and their convex surface is smoothed equally on all sides, it is not right to speak of one part as "higher" and another as "lower." Instead, **heavy** should be used to describe that which is difficult to pull out of its natural place, while **light** is that which is moved easily. Likewise, that which is composed of many parts is heavy, and that which is composed of very few parts is light.

**We breathe**, moreover, in this manner: The abundant air that surrounds us everywhere flows in through the mouth, the nostrils, and other hidden passages of the body. Once it has been warmed, it flows back out toward the external, kindred air. In the place from which it flowed out, it draws the external air back into the internal parts. In this unending circle, inhalation and exhalation follow one another.

Plato taught the causes of **diseases** in many ways. The first cause is the excess or deficiency of the elements <note>The four elements: earth, air, fire, and water</note>, and the displacement of these elements into regions of the body where they do not naturally belong...

...least suitable. The second cause of disease involves the mutual regeneration of related parts. This occurs, for example, if blood, **phlegm**, or bile is formed out of the flesh; or if phlegm is produced from it. 

<margin>phlegm</margin>

For all these processes are nothing other than liquefaction and putrefaction. Phlegm <note>original: *pituita*; one of the four humors, associated with cold and moisture</note>, indeed, is the liquefaction of fresh flesh. 

<margin>sweat</margin>

**Sweat** and tears are a certain outflow and the remnants of phlegm. When phlegm is left outside the vessels, it induces "white infections" <note>likely referring to *leukē*, a skin condition or vitiligo</note>. But when it is mixed with black bile internally, it produces the **falling sickness**, 

<margin>The sacred or comitial disease.</margin>

which is called "sacred" <note>original: *morbus sacer*; epilepsy was historically called the 'sacred disease' or the 'comitial disease' because it was thought to be a divine affliction or because its occurrence could stop a Roman 'comitia' or assembly</note>. When the phlegm is sharp and salty, it results in ailments through a "flux" or flow; for it is necessary that all those who are weighed down by both phlegm and bile suffer this. Then, bile combined with phlegm produces various diseases in living beings. He <note>Plato</note> believes that **continuous fever** happens due to an excess of fire; **quotidian** <note>daily</note> fever from an excess of air; **tertian** <note>every other day</note> from water; and finally **quartan** <note>every third day</note> from earth.

Next, we must begin to speak here about the **soul**, even if we seem to be returning to the same subject. 

<margin>Immortal soul.</margin>

Having received the immortal soul of humans from the First God, the gods who were the creators of the mortal races joined to it two mortal parts, as we have already said. But lest the power of the immortal and divine soul be corrupted by the delirious mortal parts, they established its seat in the very citadel of the composite body, as if it were the ruler of all things. 

<margin>The soul in the head.</margin>

Indeed, they intended its dwelling-place to be the **head**, fashioned in that same spherical shape in which the world itself was crafted. The rest of the body, however, acts as an assistant to this, like a

...serve as a vehicle. For the mortal parts of the soul, they assigned specific provinces to each. To anger <note>original: *iracundiae*; the spirited or "irascible" part of the soul in Platonic philosophy</note>, they assigned the midriff <note>original: *praecordia*; the area around the heart</note>; and to desire <note>original: *cupidini*; the "appetitive" soul</note>, they gave a place in the middle between the navel and the <unclear>lower belly</unclear> <note>original: *itre*; likely a transcription of *ventre* (belly) or referring to the diaphragm/waist area</note>. There they bound that power like a wild and unrestrained animal. 

<margin>lungs</margin>

They fashioned the **lungs** as a well-placed safeguard—soft, airy, hollow like a pipe, and similar to a sponge—so that when the heart's heat boils over, it might at least be calmed and mitigated by some cooling. 

<margin>liver</margin>

The **liver**, moreover, was designed both to excite and to soothe desire, possessing both sweetness and bitterness. Indeed, it was so ordained that divinations occurring through dreams would be reflected in it. For they believe that, due to its smoothness, polish, and shine, the power of the bitterness remaining within it is reflected there.

<margin>Spleen.</margin>

The **spleen**, however, exists for the sake of the liver, to cleanse it and make it bright, so that the corrosive impurities <note>original: *putredines corrodentes*</note> which flow around the liver during certain illnesses may immediately drain away into the spleen. 

When I have traced all things according to rational patterns, they are divided; yet Plato believes it happens that no one can be truly wise for long if they remain fearful or intemperate. Therefore, the **virtues** are, in an absolute sense, inseparable from one another. 

<margin>virtues</margin>

And since neither science nor art exists in such a part of the mind—which consists of the <term>irrational</term> <note>original: *irrationali*; the part of the soul governing emotions and desires rather than logic</note>—the virtues that concern the irrational parts cannot be taught, because they are neither arts nor sciences. **Prudence**, however, which is indeed a science, assigns to each its own: just as the pilot of a ship sometimes commands things that are not at all understood by the sailors; they, however, follow his lead. The same reasoning applies to the soldier and the leader of the army. Since, moreover, evils are both intensified and diminished, sins will by no means be equal, but some are greater, and others are lesser.

Wherefore the makers of laws punish some offenses more leniently, and others more harshly. And although the virtues themselves are certain peaks—because they are found to be perfect and right—in another way they are called **middle states** <note>original: *mediocritates*; this refers to the "Golden Mean," the philosophical idea that virtue is the balance between two extremes of vice</note>, because around each, or at least most of them, two vices are seen on either side, one of which arises from excess, and the other from deficiency.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of ethics from page 31, which began to define virtue as the "Golden Mean" between two extremes. Likewise, he who feels no grief when his parents have passed away is **senseless** <note>original: *insensatus*; lacking the natural human feeling or "sense" required for social and familial bonds</note>. Yet he who destroys himself with sorrow is **unrestrained** <note>original: *effrenus*; literally "unbridled," describing someone who has lost control of their rational reins</note>.

And the one who keeps to the middle ground is called moderate. He who trembles at the thought of death is fearful, while he who fears nothing at all is fierce. <margin>brave</margin> However, the **brave** man is he who maintains a **middle state** <note>original: *mediocritatem*; the balanced center point</note> between fearing and daring. The same reasoning applies to all other matters.

Since, indeed, a <margin>middle state</margin> **middle state** in the emotions is best, and since a middle state is nothing other than a middle path between excess and deficiency, virtues of this kind are called "middle states" because they make us moderately affected regarding human **disturbances** <note>original: *p[er]turbationes*; a technical term in Stoic and Aristotelian philosophy for the passions or agitations of the mind that cloud judgment</note>.

Furthermore, if anything exists within our own power and is not coerced, that thing is especially a virtue; for it would not be considered worthy of praise if it occurred either by nature or by divine chance. It follows, then, that virtue is **voluntary**. It arises through a certain burning, noble affection and a constant inner power.

Finally, from the fact that virtue is voluntary, it is concluded that **wickedness is involuntary**. For who, in their more skilled part <note>original: *peritiori*; referring to the rational mind or the intellect</note>, would ever willingly choose the extreme of those evils? If anyone falls into vice, they are at first inclined toward it not as an evil, but rather as if it were a good. And if into evil...

<meta>This page continues the exploration of moral philosophy, moving from the definition of virtue as a "middle state" on page 32 to the complex problem of whether wicked acts are voluntary or involuntary. It transitions from the mechanics of the soul into a new section on the nature of human friendship.</meta>

...in all things he is deceived, so that he might avoid some first evil that is greater. And for this reason, he acts **involuntarily** <note>original: *involuntarium*; in Aristotelian ethics, this refers to actions performed under duress or through ignorance, where the person's will is not the primary cause</note>. For it cannot be that anyone turns toward evils as if he actually desired them as evils, nor would he be driven to endure them except by some hope of good or fear of a greater evil. Wherefore, all things that the wicked man does are **involuntary**. For since **injustice** <note>original: *iniustitia*</note> is itself involuntary, it will be much more the case that the act of doing injustice is involuntary—as the action itself is certainly worse than the mere idle habit of it. And since these crimes are involuntary, should wicked men then be punished? Indeed, they ought to be subject to the grace of divine punishments, just as the losses of imposed penalties are distinguishing. Furthermore, the involuntary consists of a certain ignorance or **disturbance** <note>original: *perturbatione*; a technical term for passions or agitations of the soul that cloud rational judgment</note>. This surely stems from the author, though it is permitted through the reason and the habit of the mind and through diligence.

---

<margin>Friendship</margin>
**Friendship** <note>original: *Amicitia*</note> properly is nothing other than mutual **goodwill** <note>original: *benevolentia*; the act of wishing good things for another for their own sake, rather than for utility</note>. This truly exists when each person desires the good for the other just as much as for himself. Indeed, this **equality** occurs in no other way than through a **similarity of character** <note>original: *similitudine morum*; the philosophical principle that "like attracts like" and that true friendship requires a shared moral foundation</note>. For like is a friend to like and to the **moderate** <note>original: *moderato*; the person who maintains the "Golden Mean"</note> man. But those who are **intemperate** <note>original: *intemperanti*; those who lack self-control</note> can agree neither with each other nor with the moderate. Some also say that there are certain other kinds of friendship, though they are not based on virtue...

<summary>This page explores the paradox of involuntary evil, arguing that because no one truly desires evil for its own sake, wicked acts must stem from ignorance or fear. It then shifts to define friendship as a state of mutual goodwill founded on an equality of character and shared virtue.</summary>

<keywords>involuntary action, injustice, punishment, ignorance, friendship, goodwill, character, ethics, intemperance</keywords>

Instead, he will rather deter <note>original: *abiterrebit* (likely *absterrebit*); to discourage through fear or warning</note> him and teach him that he must by no means live in such a way as long as he remains as he is. However, he will always permit and offer those things by which the one he loves may be practiced, so that he might turn out to be of the **best character** <term>optimam frugem: literally "the best fruit," a metaphor for a person reaching moral maturity or excellence</term>. Finally, the end is this: that from love, **friendship** <note>original: *amicitia*</note> is formed.

---

**Injustice** is truly such a great evil that it is better to suffer an injury than to inflict one.
<margin>To inflict injury</margin>
For indeed, to inflict an injury upon anyone is the work of a wicked man, while to be affected by an injury is the passion <note>original: *passio*; here meaning a passive experience or something endured</note> of a weak man. Both, indeed, are shameful. But to inflict an injury is worse in that it is more disgraceful. And it is worthwhile for an unjust man to voluntarily submit himself to a judge, just as a sick man commits himself to a physician; for all punishment is a kind of **medicine** for a delinquent soul.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of human psychology and ethics, specifically following the incomplete sentence from the previous page: "Since, moreover, there are many virtues concerned with the affections (affectibus)..." This page defines why these "affections" or "emotions" are considered irrational movements and how they relate to our perception of good and evil.</meta>

...or evil. It is called an **irrational movement** <term>irrationalis motus: an impulse that occurs in the soul without the direct consent of the reasoning mind</term> because emotions are neither judgments nor opinions, but movements in the souls of rational beings. For they are movements occurring within the rational part of the soul; although these are our own actions, they are nonetheless not within our power. Therefore, involuntary movements of this kind often arise within us and conflict with our will. For even though it happens that we recognize that the things occurring are neither harsh nor sweet, nor to be expected, nor even to be feared, we are nonetheless dragged along by them. We would certainly never suffer this if **disturbances** <note>original: *perturbationes*; often used in Stoic philosophy to describe passions that overrule reason</note> were the same as judgments. For we reject judgments when we establish our position through deliberation, whether we act as we should or not.

Furthermore, "for the sake of some good or evil" was added to the definition. For emotions are not stirred up by an indifferent encounter. It is from the fact that something appears either good or evil that all disturbances are born. If a good seems to be present, we are flooded with **pleasure** <note>original: *voluptate*</note>. If it is to come, we **desire** it. Conversely, when an evil appears present, we feel **pain** <note>original: *dolemus*</note>; if it is future, we fear it. There are two simple and primary emotions: namely, **pleasure** and **pain**. The others are formed from these as if from certain elements.

He also describes another republic based on a hypothesis <note>original: *ex suppositione*</note>, of the kind he established in the books *On the Laws*, and which appears in a revised form in his *Epistles*. These are cities that have already obtained a distinct territory and people chosen from every age, so that—according to the differences of their natures and regions—they are shown to require their own specific discipline for importing and exporting, and for weaponry. For those who border the sea will apply themselves to navigation and handle naval battles. Those who live on the continent, however, will be more suited to infantry combat and lighter armor; those who inhabit the mountains...

For that which possesses reason is distinct by nature from that which is subject to perturbation <term>perturbationi: passions or emotional disturbances</term>. Reason, indeed, belongs to humans alone, while the part obedient to passions is common to all other living things. Therefore, since the rational and irrational portions are distinct by nature, they must also be separate in their locations. For these two are often found to fight against each other. But nothing can fight with itself; nor indeed can those things which are contrary to each other be present in any way concerning the same thing, according to the same respect, at the same time.

Moreover, in Medea <note>the mythological sorceress who killed her children to spite her husband Jason</note>, anger is seen to fight with reason. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul’s internal structure, specifically addressing the distinction between the rational and irrational parts before transitioning into a series of proofs regarding the soul's immortality, primarily drawing from the Platonic tradition.</meta>

# On Immortality. III

...was resisting: For he thus says, "Alas, alas, this divine evil among men: for though one knows the good, one does not use it." <note>original: *heu heu diuinum hoc hoïbus malum: qm̃ quis bonū nouit: sed minime utitur*; this reflects the concept of *akrasia* or "weakness of will," where a person acts against their better judgment.</note> Likewise, it will be plainly evident from this that one part is rational <term>rationalem: the part of the soul capable of logic and reasoning</term> and another is irrational, because the cure for each is different. For the former is cured by instruction, while the latter is cured by habit and exercise.

Furthermore, Plato demonstrates by these reasons that the soul is immortal. To whatever thing the soul is present, it brings life, as life is naturally grafted into it. That which provides life, however, does not admit death. That which is of this kind exists as immortal. But if the soul is immortal, it will also be incorruptible. For its incorporeal essence is, according to its substance, immutable, intelligible <term>intelligibilis: able to be understood by the mind rather than perceived by the senses</term>, invisible, and uniform. Will it not, therefore, be both simple and indissoluble? The body, on the contrary, is sensible <term>sensibile: perceptible by the five senses</term>, visible, dissipable, composite, and multiform. Furthermore, when the soul descends through the senses of the body to those things which are sensible, it is distressed and troubled; nor can it be like that thing by whose
<margin>
The Intelligible
The immortal soul
</margin>
presence it is disturbed. Wherefore, it is more like the intelligible than the sensible. Moreover, the intelligible is always incorruptible by its own nature. Added to this is the fact that the soul by its own nature dominates the body; and that which by its nature rules and commands is akin to the divine.

<summary>This page outlines Plato’s arguments for the immortality of the soul, contrasting its simple, divine, and "intelligible" nature with the composite and "sensible" nature of the body.</summary>
<keywords>immortality, Plato, rational soul, irrational soul, incorruptibility, intelligible vs sensible, divine nature, dualism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument for the soul's immortality, specifically transitioning from the soul's similarity to the divine and intelligible realm to the Platonic argument of opposites and the theory of Recollection (Anamnesis).</meta>

<page-num>38.</page-num>

<margin>The soul he gave to the body</margin>
Therefore, the soul, being nearest to God, will be immortal. Likewise, <gloss>the soul</gloss> whatever things are opposites without an intermediary, and not according to their own essence but according to a certain accident <term>accidens: a property that a thing possesses but which is not essential to its nature</term>, are so established that souls may mutually arise from one another. Indeed, that which men call "living" and that which is called "death" are opposites. Just as,
<margin>
death
Life
</margin>
therefore, death is the separation of the soul from the body, so life is the union of the soul with the body. For the soul existed before the body; and if it existed both before and will exist after death, it seems consistent that it is eternal. Nor can anything further be imagined that might destroy it.
<margin>recollection</margin>
Add to this that if learning is a kind of recollection <term>reminiscentia: the Platonic theory of *anamnesis*, suggesting that "learning" is actually the soul recovering knowledge it possessed before birth</term>, it is necessary that its nature be immortal. Indeed, we affirm that recollection is so: for we cannot learn anything in any other way than through the remembering of those things which we once knew. For if we understood universals <term>universalia: general concepts or qualities (like "Redness" or "Justice") that exist apart from individual instances</term> from individual things, in what way could we run through those individual things themselves, since they are infinite, to arrive at the universal? But how have we perceived these commonalities from only a few individuals? We would be deceived and mistaken, just as if we were to judge that everything which uses respiration <note>original: *respinatione*</note> belongs to the soul alone, or that these primary intelligences in us were the fundamental principles. Therefore, we have understood by recollecting from certain small and more certain things, through those providing a way, remembering those things which long ago...
