...we have known, and whose forgetfulness we first suffered then: <note>original: "oblivione tunc primum perpessi sumus"</note> when souls first descended into bodies. Furthermore, if a nature is not corrupted by its own inherent evil, it will never be destroyed by the evil of another, nor simply by anything else at all. And since it is of such a kind, it will remain incorruptible. 

Likewise, that which moves itself, insofar as it is the principle <term>principium: the source, origin, or fundamental cause of something</term> of motion, is always moved, and that which is of this kind is immortal. The soul indeed moves itself, and that which is moved by itself is the principle of all motion and generation <note>the process of coming into being</note>. However, a principle remains unbegotten and incorruptible. Wherefore, perhaps all souls of men and of other living beings are rational and participants in the same mixture. He calls it "self-moving" or the soul, because it has innate life, always operating according to itself. Therefore, according to Plato’s opinion, anyone can assert that rational souls are immortal.

...otherwise they enter into bodies, adhering to already formed fetuses, and are able to migrate again into many bodies—both human and others—always carrying the same things with them; and this occurs either by the will of the gods

Indeed, the body and the soul have a certain kinship between them, such as that between fire and bitumen <note>bitumen: a naturally occurring tar-like substance; the author uses this metaphor to show how the soul is "ignited" or attracted to the physical body like fuel to fire</note>. Since even the souls of the gods have obtained a power of discerning, which can also be called the faculty of distinguishing. Furthermore, they also possess the power of undertaking action, which we can call the vigor of resolution. After these, they possess a certain power by which they dwell together and govern. These natures, though they exist within the souls of men, are transformed once they have entered into bodies. For the faculty of dwelling together and governing is transformed into concupiscence <term>concupiscentia: strong physical desire or longing</term>

while the vigor of undertaking action is transformed into irascibility <note>original: "iracundiam"; refers to the "spirited" part of the soul which, in the physical world, manifests as anger or passion</note>.

### **On Fate**

Moreover, such are certain of Plato's views on this matter. All things, he says, are "in fate," yet not all things are "decreed by fate." For fate, acting in the manner of a Law <term>lex: here meaning a universal or divine ordinance</term>, by no means says: "this man will do such things," or "this man will suffer such things."
<margin>
<image-desc>A small manicule pointing to the text discussing fate and free will.</image-desc>
</margin>
For the progression would be infinite, since those who are born are infinite in number, as are the things that happen to them; and thus no liberty <term>libertas: free will or the capacity for independent choice</term> would remain, and praises and punishments <note>original: "vituperationes"; meaning blame or formal reproaches</note> would be entirely abolished. Rather, they proclaim fate in this way: "Whatever soul shall choose such a life, and certain things of this kind..."

<summary>This page explains the "kinship" between soul and body using the metaphor of fire and bitumen, and introduces Plato's argument that Fate acts as a general law rather than a specific script, thereby preserving human free will.</summary>

is free
</margin>

...has completed. Consequently, it will produce such outcomes. Therefore the soul is free, and it is left to its own judgment <note>original: "arbitrio"; the power of the will to choose</note> either to act or not to act, nor is it forced in this by anyone.

However, that which follows an action will be fulfilled by fate itself. For example: the fact that Paris will seize Helen is an act that remains within his own choice; but from that act, it follows that the Greeks will go to war over Helen. In this way also, Apollo predicted to Laius himself: "If you beget a son" <note>The oracle warned King Laius of Thebes that if he had a son, that son would kill him; the author argues the act of begetting was a choice, but the tragedy that followed was fate.</note>—for the act of begetting pertains to Laius himself. Indeed, both Laius and the begetting of a son were long ago encompassed [in the prophecy]. Yet it is established by fate what follows from the begetting of a son.

But the nature of the "possible" <term>possibile: that which is able to happen but is not yet determined as a fact</term> stands midway between the false and the true. By its own nature, which is indefinite, a free and rational power is brought to bear upon it from every side. However, once we have made a choice and the act is carried out, it immediately becomes either true or false. 

This differs greatly from that which is "in potency" <term>potentia: the inherent capacity or raw talent to become something</term>—that is, what exists as a "habit" <term>habitus: a settled disposition or acquired skill, like knowing how to read</term> when it eventually emerges into "act" <term>actus: the reality of doing something in the present moment</term>. For that which is in potency signifies a certain faculty and aptitude for things that have not yet achieved their proper order. Just as a boy is said to be a grammarian, a flute-player, or a blacksmith in "potency"; the function or habit of these roles will then be established when he has learned them and has acquired one of these habits. But he is finally said to be in "act" only when he works according to the habit he has acquired. But what shall we call "possible"? Neither of these...

<summary>This page explores the intersection of free will and fate, arguing that while individual choices (like those of Paris or Laius) are free, their consequences are governed by fate. It also introduces the philosophical distinctions between potentiality, habits, and actualized actions.</summary>

<keywords>Fate, free will, Paris and Helen, Laius, potentiality (potentia), habit (habitus), action (actus), choice (arbitrium), Aristotle, Plato</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the philosophical discussion regarding the nature of "the possible" and transitions into a summary of Platonic ethics. It moves from the logic of human choice to the metaphysical foundation of the "Highest Good" (Summum Bonum) as the source of all virtue and happiness.</meta>

...it is. For the indeterminate and indifferent thing <note>original: "Indeterminatum enim atqz indifferens"; referring to a situation where the outcome is not yet fixed</note>, as it has pleased [the actor] through their own freedom—the scale, as it were, tipping—immediately becomes either true or false out of the "possible" <note>This continues the previous page's argument: once a person makes a choice, a "possible" event becomes a fixed "true" or "false" fact.</note>.

Next, let us briefly run through the things that pleased Plato regarding ethics <note>original: "de moribus"; literally "concerning customs/morals"</note>. He thought that the most venerable and supreme good
<margin>The Highest Good</margin>
is neither easily found, nor, once you have found it, can it be safely revealed to anyone. Therefore, he communicated the contemplation of this highest good <term>Summum Bonum: the ultimate goal or "Greatest Good" in Platonic philosophy</term> with only a very few people, and indeed only those who were most intimate and well-chosen. Our own good, however—if anyone examines his books diligently—will be found to be placed within the contemplation of that first good itself. 
<margin>God and the highest good</margin>
Indeed, one may call this good both "God" and the "First Mind." For all things which are called "good" by men, he wished to be named good for this reason: that in some way they participate in that first good. Just as all things that are sweet or hot are allotted their names through a certain participation in a "first sweet" or a "first hot" thing. However, out of all the things that are within us, only mind and reason <note>original: "mentem atqz roem" (mentem atque rationem)</note> attain a likeness of that good itself. Wherefore, according to him, our good may be called something beautiful,
<margin>Happiness.</margin>
venerable, divine, lovable, and well-proportioned, as well as "happiness" <term>beatitudo: a state of supreme blessedness or flourishing</term>.

<summary>This page concludes a discussion on how human choice determines truth from possibility and introduces Plato’s ethical theory, which identifies the Highest Good with God and the First Mind.</summary>

Nor, however, does he call one blessed for the sake of the honor which a life of this kind has produced, nor for the sake of any other reward; rather, even if his state remains hidden from all people, and even if those things commonly thought of as "evils" of fortune—exile and death—should befall him, he will nevertheless be blessed. Conversely, he considers a person without the knowledge of God—even if they possess all the things the common crowd proclaims as "goods," such as wealth, kingdoms, health, strength, and beauty—to be not a bit more blessed. Consistent with and following all these points, he introduced the "end" <term>the *finis* or *telos*: the ultimate purpose and goal of human life</term>, which is to become like God as far as the human race is able to attain it. 

He attempts to reach this in various ways. 

<margin>
Likeness to God
</margin>
For sometimes he says that the likeness of God is to be wise, just, and holy, as he declares in the *Theaetetus*. From this, he warns that we must admit a need here to flee toward divine things. This "flight," however, is the likeness of God; and the likeness of God is to become wise, just, and holy. At other times, he also demonstrates that we are able to become like God through justice alone, as in the final book of the *Republic*. For God never overlooks anyone who strives so greatly to become just, and who, through the duty of virtue itself, renders himself as much like God as is permitted. In the *Phaedo*, however, he describes the likeness of God through temperance...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Platonic ethics. It moves from the goal of becoming like God to the practical means of achieving that state: specifically, the role of the liberal arts as spiritual purification and the division of virtues according to the parts of the soul.</meta>

...if we live in harmony with nature, and in our character, way of life, and perception according to the law; and especially through that doctrine, so that we may live always far removed from human affairs and devoted to divine contemplations. Moreover, if there should be need for greater expiations, these defenses and a kind of purification from former spirits <note>original: *daemonum*; in this Platonic context, these are not "demons" in the modern sense, but intermediary spiritual influences that can cloud the soul</note> are achieved through the mathematical disciplines: through music, arithmetic, astronomy, and geometry. Care of the body must also be maintained through gymnastics, which makes it more ready for the works of both peace and war.

<margin>Virtue</margin>
Truly, virtue—and virtue is a certain divine treasure—is indeed a certain disposition and a perfect and optimal life, making a man harmonious and stable in his virtues, both in acting and in speaking, toward himself and others alike. Among the virtues, however, some concern the rational part and others the external parts <note>referring to the non-rational parts of the soul described below</note>. For since the nature of the rational part is one thing, the power of anger <note>original: *irascendi*; refers to the "spirited" part of the soul, which provides the drive for courage and self-assertion</note> another, and that of desire <note>original: *concupiscendi*; the "appetitive" part of the soul, related to physical needs and wants</note> yet another, these perfections must also be diverse. Hence, prudence <term>original: *prudentia*; practical wisdom or the ability to judge the right course of action</term> will pertain to the first, fortitude to the second, and temperance to the last. And indeed, prudence is the knowledge of good and evil and of indifferent things. The matter...

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical breakdown of the Platonic virtues. Having previously mentioned prudence, the text now details the remaining three cardinal virtues—temperance, fortitude, and justice—and argues for their "connection" or unity, meaning one cannot truly possess one virtue without the others.</meta>

<margin>Temperance</margin>
Temperance <note>original: *Temperantia*</note> is a permanent order regarding pleasures and desires, ensuring that they are made obedient to reason. When we attribute order and obedience to temperance, we wish it to be understood that there is a certain power by which desires become handmaidens to nature, or rather, to the master <note>the "master" here refers to the rational mind</note>. <margin>Fortitude</margin> Fortitude <note>original: *Fortitudo*; also translated as courage</note> is the preservation of a lawful instruction, or rather, the preservation of one’s own principles in the face of difficulty. That is, it is the faculty of a constant mind that carries out the lawful purpose of reason. <margin>Justice</margin> Justice is a certain harmony of all the parts of the soul; it is indeed a power through which the three forces of the soul <note>referring to the rational, spirited, and appetitive parts mentioned on the previous page</note> sound together in unison. In this state, each part performs its own duty according to its dignity, so that justice serves as a complete and whole perfection of these three virtues: prudence, fortitude, and temperance. While reason leads and commands, the remaining parts of the soul remain willingly obedient to their own duty toward the superior part. <margin>The connection of the virtues</margin> From this, it must be considered that the virtues follow one another in a certain union. For since fortitude is the preservation of lawful instruction, it will belong to a reason that stands firm in what is right. Right reason, however, proceeds from prudence. Since prudence also depends on fortitude—for it is surely the knowledge of good things—no one can discern the good while they are blinded by fear and the disturbances <note>original: *perturbationibus*; often referring to overwhelming emotions or "passions" that cloud judgment</note> that follow it. And in the same way, a prudent man has no fellowship with intemperance. To the extent that anyone is seized by such a disturbance, which interferes with reason, they will always be far from justice.

<summary>This page defines the four cardinal virtues—Temperance, Fortitude, Justice, and Prudence—explaining how they create harmony within the soul and why they are fundamentally interconnected.</summary>
<keywords>Cardinal Virtues, Temperance, Fortitude, Justice, Prudence, Harmony of the Soul, Platonic Ethics, Reason, Passions</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the cardinal virtues, specifically narrowing in on the practical application of prudence in governance. It follows the previous page's description of the unity of virtues (temperance, fortitude, and justice) by detailing how prudence functions in a legal and social context before transitioning into a comparison between the true philosopher and the deceptive Sophist.</meta>

<margin>
legislative
prudence . i .
Judicial
</margin>
**PRUDENCE** <note>original: *prudentia*; here referring to practical wisdom or discretion</note> indeed manages personal affairs, domestic matters, and the state. When it governs the republic, it is called **civil science** <note>original: *civilis scientia*; the equivalent of political science or statecraft</note>. Its duties are to establish laws and to protect those already established. The first is called **legislative** <note>original: *nomothetica*; from the Greek for "law-giving"</note>, that is, the power of making laws; the second is called **judicial** <note>original: *dicastica*; from the Greek for "judgment"</note>, that is, the power of the courts. Finally, civil expertise considers those things which pertain to peace and those which pertain to war; and in this, among many other points, it focuses on this specifically: whether a war should be undertaken or rather refused.

<margin>The Sophist</margin>
Thus far, it has been stated what kind of man the philosopher is. The **Sophist** <note>original: *Sophista*; a teacher of rhetoric often accused by Plato of using fallacious arguments for profit</note>, however, differs from the philosopher both in his method and his subject matter, and in the way he invents his modes of speaking. He prefers to appear good and famous rather than to actually be so. Regarding the subject matter, the philosopher is occupied with those things which are always the same and remain in the same state <note>referring to eternal truths or the "Forms"</note>. The Sophist struggles and labors around that which "is not" <note>the realm of falsehood, illusion, or non-being</note>, fleeing into sudden darkness which, being so dark, can hardly be perceived. For he does not at all oppose "that which is" against "that which is not" as simple contraries. For "that which is not" neither exists, nor shares in any **substance** <note>original: *substantia*; the essential nature of a thing</note>, nor can it even be understood. Indeed, if anyone tries to utter or think it, he will go astray and stumble inconsistently because he conflicts and disagrees with himself. But as for that which "is not," insofar as it is heard when spoken, it is certainly not a bare appearance in that matter, but comes with a certain suspicion and a subtle insinuation of something else <note>implying that the Sophist's lies mimic the form of truth to deceive the listener</note>.

<summary>This page defines the political dimensions of prudence (legislative and judicial) and begins a comparison between the philosopher, who seeks eternal truths, and the Sophist, who deals in the "darkness" of non-being and illusion.</summary>
<keywords>prudence, civil science, legislation, justice, war and peace, philosopher vs sophist, substance, non-being</keywords>

<insert>
The Golden Precepts 
of Pythagoras

which indeed, in some way, accompanies that which is itself. For if those things which participate in that which is not <note>original: *qd non est*; referring to the Platonic concept of "non-being" or the material world's lack of true essence</note> were not to do so, they would in no way be distinguished from others. Now, however, as many things as there are that *are*, so many modes of that which *is not* have proceeded. For that which is not one specific thing among those that *are*, surely *is not*.

And let these things suffice as an introduction to the Platonic discipline; some have been treated in their proper order, while others were introduced scattered without a fixed order. Nevertheless, they have been handed down in such a way that, by following their tracks, it is possible to hunt for the remaining meanings of Plato.

# The Golden Precepts of Pythagoras translated by Marsilio Ficino for Giovanni Cavalcanti

<margin>laws of the land</margin> First, honor the immortal gods as the laws command. Revere your oath. Then, honor the blessed heroes of the earth, and afterwards the spirits <note>original: *demones*; in the Neoplatonic sense, these are *daemons* or intermediary spirits between humans and gods, not "demons" in the modern sense</note>. Observe the laws that have been instituted. Honor your parents and your relatives. From all others, make that person your friend <margin>friend</margin> who is most outstanding in virtue. Yield to gentle words and useful works. Do not turn your soul against yourself <note>or "make your soul an adversary to you"</note> for the sake of a slight error. Bear what you can, for power dwells near necessity.

### Golden Sayings

...commit anything shameful, neither in the presence of others, nor with yourself.
<margin>Justice.</margin> But above all, revere yourself. Practice justice in both word and deed. Accustom yourself to doing nothing without 
<margin>Death.</margin> reason. Know that death is appointed by fate to everyone. Learn to accumulate 
<margin>Money.</margin> money at the proper time and, again, to spend it when the situation demands. 
And as for those things which happen to men as adversities through chance 
<margin>Adversity.</margin> occurrences, whatever lot you may have, bear it without distress. It is necessary to remedy <note>original: *mederi*; in this context, to heal or mitigate the effects of misfortune</note> it as much as you can. Know that fortune does not bring many of these [evils] to good men. 

Many and varied speeches, some good and some bad, are circulated among the 
<margin>Various speeches</margin> common people; let none of these move you, nor allow yourself to be constrained by them. But if they should speak something false, be patient <note>original: *moderatus fueris*; suggests maintaining a moderate or calm temperament even in the face of lies</note>. What I say now, fulfill entirely: let no one lead you, by either action or word, to that 
<margin>Deliberate before work</margin> which is not best for you. Deliberate before a task, lest you act rashly. For it is the mark of a wretched man to speak or act without thought. But perform those things which [later] <note>original: *qum dote*; likely a variant or error for *cum deinde*, meaning "afterward"</note> will not bring you regret. 

Do not do the things you are 
<margin>Do nothing through ignorance.</margin> ignorant of, but learn as much as is necessary. And thus you will pass through life most pleasantly. 
<margin>Health of the body</margin> Do not neglect the health of the body; but apply moderation <note>original: *mensuram*; the classic Pythagorean and Platonic concept of "the measure" or "the golden mean"</note> to food, 
<margin>Exercise of the body.</margin> drink, and exercise. By moderation, I mean that which brings you no distress. Accustom yourself to a pure and simple way of life. Avoid those things which <unclear>provoke</unclear> envy.

### Page 45.

->the things said before<-

Do not squander the blessings of fortune beyond what is appropriate,

<margin>Moderation</margin> is best in all things. Do not do those things which might harm you. Consider

<margin>Sleep</margin> you have revolved the day’s works three times in your mind. 
<margin><note>manicule pointing right</note></margin> Where did I travel? What did I do? What task that should have been done

When you have done something shameful, accuse yourself. 
But when you have completed good deeds, congratulate yourself. 
Practice these things, meditate on these things, you ought to love these things. These will set you 
in the footsteps of divine virtue. By him who

nature. Set out for your work with the gods having been invoked <note>original: *votis*; translated here as the act of making a vow or prayer before beginning labor</note>. 
<margin>vowed</margin> For in this way, you shall know the condition of the immortal 
gods and of mortal men, 
how all things proceed and are contained. You shall know how 
justice and divine law exist, so that you might not hope for things 
that should not be hoped for, nor let anything that is hidden 
escape you. You shall know that men are miserable 
because they are the cause of their own evils. 
They neither see nor hear the good things that are near to them. 
Indeed, very few understand the release from their evils. 
Such a fate harms the minds 
of men, who are carried by certain revolutions from one thing to another, 
subject to endless evils. Deadly discord, 
implanted in them, stands in their way. You, tame it by yielding, and 

<summary>This page contains the core of Pythagorean ethics, including the famous requirement to review one's actions three times before sleeping and the oath by the "fourfold fountain" (Tetractys). It concludes by explaining that human suffering is self-imposed through a failure to recognize the good that is nearby.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the translation of the "Golden Verses of Pythagoras" and begins the "Symbols of Pythagoras." The previous page ended with the promise that through these teachings, the reader would understand the divine law that governs both gods and men.</meta>

<margin>· Verses ·</margin>
Once this [knowledge] has come to you, you will not increase your suffering. O Father Jupiter <note>original: *Iuppiter pr̃*; Jupiter the Father</note>, you would indeed free men from many evils, or show them what spirit <note>original: *demone*; refers to a guiding spirit or destiny</note> they possess. But you, take courage, for a divine race exists in men. To them, sacred nature, bringing forth all things, demonstrates the truth. 

If any of these things have been revealed to you, you will abstain from those things which I command you to avoid. When you have applied this remedy, you will also be freed from those labors <note>original: *laboribz*; referring to the struggles or sufferings of the soul</note>. But abstain from the mortal things which we mentioned before, in the purification and the release of the soul. Consider each thing with right judgment. I propose that the best insight should act as your charioteer <note>original: *aurigam*; a classic Platonic and Pythagorean metaphor for reason guiding the soul</note>. When you have laid aside your body and proceed freely to the heavens <note>original: *ethera*; the upper air or divine realms</note>, you will escape your human state and become an immortal god, ethereal and nourishing.

### The Symbols of Pythagoras, translated by Marsilio Ficino 
### for Giovanni Cavalcanti
<note>Marsilio Ficino was a central figure of the Italian Renaissance who translated the works of Plato and other ancient sages for his patrons, the Medici, and his close circle of friends like Cavalcanti.</note>

<meta>This page concludes the list of "Symbols of Pythagoras," a series of cryptic moral and philosophical precepts translated by the Renaissance Neoplatonist Marsilio Ficino. <margin>Concerning God</margin>
¶ Concerning the gods and divine matters, say nothing so marvelous
that you would not believe it <note>original: *nihil tam mirabile dicit quod non debeas credere*; Pythagoras taught that the divine power is limitless, so one should not be skeptical of "miracles" or divine mysteries</note>.
Abstain from unrestrained laughter;
Do not bite your nails during the performance of sacrifices <note>this likely warns against being distracted or anxious during religious duties</note>;
Do not offer your right hand easily to everyone <note>a warning against making hasty friendships or alliances</note>;
When clearing away the straw bedding, smooth over the shape itself <note>original: *Straminis purganti collige ipsa figuram cosudo*; a common Pythagorean rule to leave no physical trace or "impression" of the body in the bed-straw, symbolizing a lack of attachment to the material self</note>;

<margin>The Heart</margin>
Do not devour your heart <note>original: *Cor ne uores*; a famous metaphor meaning one should not consume themselves with grief, anxiety, or internal resentment</note>;
Do not eat the brain <note>this symbolizes not wasting one's intellect on trivial or worldly things</note>;

<margin>Hair</margin>
After you have shaved off the excess of your hair and nails,
you must spit upon the clippings <note>an ancient folk-practice intended to prevent others from using one's physical remains for harmful magic</note>;
Do not take up anything red <note>this typically referred to not picking up anything that fell from a table, particularly if it was associated with blood or sacrifice</note>;
Stir the trace of the pot heedlessly <note>original: *Ollo uestigium temere cofunde*; an instruction to smooth over the ashes in a cooking pot so no mark is left behind—again, a metaphor for leaving no trace of the lower, material life</note>;
Do not associate with one who has gold for the purpose of procreating children <note>a warning against marrying for wealth rather than virtue or intellectual compatibility</note>;
Honor above all the figure and the altar,
the figure and the pence which are yours <note>original: *figuram ac prius dinariorum tuis*; this likely refers to the "Pythagorean abacus" or the geometric figures used in their mathematical philosophy, which were to be valued more than common currency</note>;

<margin>Abstain from beans</margin>
Abstain from beans <note>the most famous and debated Pythagorean rule; it may have been for health reasons, but many Renaissance thinkers saw it as a metaphor for avoiding the "flatulence" of public politics or as a belief in the migration of souls</note>;
Sow the mallow herb, but do not eat it;

<margin>The swallow</margin>
Do not receive the swallow into your house <note>swallows were seen as talkative and restless; the "house" represents the mind, which should not admit chatterers or fair-weather friends</note>;
Abstain from animal meat;

Disease,

</margin>
It is fitting to put to flight with all zeal, and to send away by every means:
To cut away with fire and sword disease from the body, ignorance from the mind,
luxury from the belly, discord from the home, sedition from the city,
and finally intemperance from all things. ~ The End.

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the Pythagorean symbols to a discussion of Platonic philosophy, specifically drawing from the *Theaetetus*. It introduces the central Neoplatonic theme of the soul's ascent and the distinction between the divine governance of the mind and the material fate of the body.</meta>

SOCRATES, in that book by Plato titled *Theaetetus*, fortified his friend, the geometer Theodorus, against the blows of fortune—not with weapons of iron, as most are accustomed to doing, but with golden ones <note>original: *aureis... muniuit*; "golden weapons" refers to the incorruptible and precious nature of philosophical wisdom compared to physical defenses</note>—in this manner: "It is impossible," he said, "O Theodorus, for evils to be utterly uprooted."

<margin>
Something is opposed to the good
</margin>

For it is necessary that there be always something contrary to the good.

<margin>Evils</margin> Indeed, among the gods above, evils cannot exist. But they necessarily circulate through this mortal nature and this lower region. Therefore, we must strive to flee from here to there as quickly as possible. 

<margin>Similar to God</margin> But let us say that "to flee" is to make oneself as similar to God as possible. Moreover, prudence, holiness, and justice are what make a man similar to God. You shall hear in brief how this divine precept of our Plato <note>Ficino often refers to "our Plato," signaling his deep personal and intellectual devotion to the philosopher</note> should be understood. Just as God is the creator and likewise the governor of immortal souls, so the world is the progenitor and governor of bodies.

Since, moreover, the soul is a "son of God" and the body is a "member of the world," our soul is led by God as if by a father, according to the laws of providence, with mercy and sweetness; our body, however, is dragged by the fated powers of the body of the world, like a certain small particle pulled by its entire mass with a violent force. Nor does the force of fate <term>Fate: in this context, the deterministic physical laws of the material universe</term> penetrate our mind, unless the mind itself has voluntarily cast itself into the body which is subject to fate. 

Therefore, let no one trust so much in their own counsel and strength that they hope to be able to entirely avoid the members of the body and their fractures <note>meaning physical injuries, illnesses, or the general "breaking" of the material self</note>. 

<margin>
In the members
</margin>

Let them receive...

<summary>Ficino uses Socrates' dialogue with Theodorus to explain that while evil is inherent to the material world, the soul can escape it by practicing virtue and becoming "like God." He establishes a dualism where the mind is governed by divine providence while the body remains subject to the violent, fated movements of the physical universe.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Plato, Theaetetus, Providence, Fate, Virtue, Dualism, Soul and Body, Theodorus, Marsilio Ficino</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Platonic flight from the material world. Ficino is interpreting Socrates' advice from the *Theaetetus* on how the soul can escape the "plague" of the physical body and the whims of fortune by retreating into the mind and aligning itself with God through specific virtues.</meta>

...away from the plague of bodies, let each and every person collect himself into his own mind. Then, fortune will not be able to exercise its violence upon bodies. Yet, fortune will not diminish its efforts. The wise man will not fight in vain against fate; rather, he will resist by fleeing <note>original: *sed fugiendo potius repugnabit*; Ficino suggests that true resistance to material destiny is not a physical battle, but a spiritual withdrawal</note>. Adversities cannot be entirely avoided, but they can be fled from. Therefore, we are commanded by Plato to flee from here to there—away from the cave of bodies and from the regard for external things—toward the soul and the worship of God.

Otherwise, evils cannot be shunned. He added that as quickly as possible, we should separate our whole mind, setting it apart from the communion of the body, so that we may take hold of the soul. Through this divine habit, may the lover <note>original: *ames*; likely referring to the Platonic lover of wisdom</note> be rendered similar to God, not without merit, by this flight; for indeed, the mind then escapes as a mistress, free from the contagion of bodies. We are set free by virtues of this kind, especially by three: prudence, justice, and holiness. 

<margin>Prudence</margin> Prudence is that by which one observes what is owed to God and what is owed to the world. <margin>Justice and Holiness</margin> Justice is that by which one gives the world its due, and Holiness is that by which one gives to God what is His.

Therefore, the prudent man grants the body—which is of the world’s power—to the revolution of the world itself, to be driven by whatever may happen. But the soul, the daughter of God, he separates from the communion of bodies and commits to the governance of divine providence, as if to a fatherly protection <note>original: *patrio... gubernandum*; emphasizing God's role as the "father" of the soul compared to the "progenitor" world of the body</note>. If we follow this golden precept of Plato, my sweetest John <note>Referring to Giovanni Cavalcanti, Ficino’s close friend and the frequent addressee of his letters</note>, we shall sail immediately and safely through this vast whirlpool of fortune to the harbor of happiness, with the breath of heaven favoring us.

->Farewell.<-

<summary>Ficino concludes his Platonic exhortation, arguing that while the body is subject to fate and the "whirlpool of fortune," the soul can find safety by retreating into the mind and practicing the virtues of prudence, justice, and holiness.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Giovanni Cavalcanti, Neoplatonism, soul and body, fortune vs providence, prudence, holiness, justice, allegory of the cave</keywords>

# Preface to the little work of Lucian "On Sacrifices"
# translated from Greek into the Latin language by
# Antonius Tudernus: To the most noble youth
# Rodulfus Lottus, citizen of Florence

<margin>Socrates</margin>
The philosopher **SOCRATES**, when he was asked what was the most pleasant thing in life, replied: the study of virtue and the history of those things which are no longer held <note>original: *rerum quę nō tenerentur*. This likely refers to the study of the past or intangible intellectual truths which cannot be physically grasped.</note>. Truly a most wise sentiment. For there is no other pleasure that does not eventually weary the mind and the stomach; and once a point of satiety is reached, it brings much more ill and sadness than the joy we initially pursued in the pleasure itself. This Socratic joy, however, brings no less sweetness in its absence than in its very possession. This clearly seems to be established by the nature of the thing itself: that this

<margin>That this kind of honorable pleasure is the most honorable.</margin>
kind of honorable pleasure is the most honorable. Therefore, since I had intended to present some sign of my affection toward you at this time, I wished to dedicate this little book in your name. My hope was that, through reading it, I might provide you with a most honorable kind of joy. Indeed, I believed this would be dearer to you than if I had gifted you a spirited horse or the most precious of garments. For since now...

<meta>This page concludes the dedicatory preface by the humanist translator Antonio da Todi (Antonius Tudernus) to his patron Ridolfo Lotti (Rodulfus Lottus). ...a man of almost singular integrity and the highest virtue, I believe this little gift <term>munusculum|A "little gift"; a common humanist term for a small literary work offered to a patron to show modesty.</term> should be added to the glory of your name. You have achieved so much in your very brief studies of the disciplines and are endowed with such talent that I foresee you will emerge as a most excellent and noble citizen and a most distinguished man. But I ask one thing of your kindness: that in this matter you do not embrace the little gift itself, but rather my spirit, which has been previously <gloss>moved</gloss> by your kindnesses toward me. If I had the ability, my spirit would deal with you through great gifts as the occasion requires. For I am not unaware that, both for your dignity and for your supreme love toward me, you ought to have been honored with a much greater gift. Yet I do not doubt that, in accordance with the gentleness of your soul, you will receive whatever this may be with a grateful heart. I have therefore undertaken Lucian’s *On Sacrifices* <note>original: *Luciani... de sacrificiis*</note>, where he shows much petulance <note>original: *petulantiam*; Lucian was a 2nd-century satirist known for mocking the irrational behavior of traditional Greek gods.</note> from the pagan gods and much foolishness from men.

But it is not my intention to review anything about the loves by which **Apollo** was wretchedly captured, <margin>Apollo</margin>nor the death of **Hyacinthus** <note>original: *iacinti mōtē*; likely a scribal error for *morte* (death). Both of them, on account of a certain perjury of the king <note>Laomedon, King of Troy, famously refused to pay the gods for their labor after they built the city's walls.</note>, devoted their labor to shaping bricks and constructing walls, yet in the end, they received no payment at all. Do you not judge such deeds concerning the gods to be unworthy of the veneration given by the poets? <meta>This page continues Lucian of Samosata’s satirical critique of religious myths in "On Sacrifices." Having previously mocked Apollo’s misfortunes and his time as a mortal servant, the text now turns its attention to the king of the gods, Jupiter (Zeus), and his father Saturn (Cronus).</meta>

At the beginning of a poem, the poets themselves implore the Muses for inspiration, so that, inspired by divine power—which they would have us believe is true—they may sing of how **Saturn** <note>The Roman name for the Titan Cronus.</note> brought death to his father **Caelus** <note>The Roman name for Uranus; the name literally means "The Heavens."</note> and ruled in a strange manner. They sing of how his son [Jupiter] was produced like the Argive **Thyestes** <note>In Greek myth, Thyestes was served the flesh of his own children at a banquet. Lucian compares this to Saturn devouring his children to prevent them from overthrowing him.</note>. 

<margin>Jupiter</margin>
Then, as **Rhea** <note>The OCR "suberasta area" appears to be a corruption of a reference to Rhea, the mother of Jupiter.</note> says, she substituted a stone [for Saturn to swallow], and the infant was exposed on the island of **Crete** and nourished by a goat—just as **Telephus** <note>A son of Hercules who, according to legend, was suckled by a doe.</note> was by a doe, and **Cyrus** <unclear>hmā</unclear> <note>Cyrus the Great, founder of the Persian Empire, who according to legend was raised by a dog.</note> by a dog. 

Later, after his father was expelled and cast into prison, he [Jupiter] took command himself. After entering the embrace of many women from the beds of others, he approached **Juno** <note>Hera.</note>, the eldest among his sisters, and with persistent prayers made her his more devoted wife. 

With her, he was not slow to produce **Mars** <note>Ares.</note> and **Venus** <note>Lucian (or the scribe) here follows a tradition associating Venus with Juno's lineage, though she is more commonly born from sea-foam or Jupiter and Dione.</note>; he filled the heavens even more with the number of his offspring and established certain illegitimate children, born from mortal and human mothers, in positions of honor. 

Furthermore, he would turn himself now into a noble bird <note>Jupiter often took the form of an eagle.</note>, now into water <note>A reference to the "golden rain" he used to reach Danaë.</note>, now into a shifting **Proteus** <note>A sea-god capable of changing into any shape; here used as a metaphor for Jupiter's many disguises used in his affairs.</note>. 

But **Minerva** <note>Athena.</note> alone he generated from his own head; since he was without offspring <note>That is, a child produced through the traditional womb.</note>, he conceived her in his brain. **Bacchus** <note>Dionysus.</note>, however, he rescued half-formed from the womb of his mother while she was still burning and in the midst of labor... <note>Bacchus’s mother, Semele, was consumed by the fire of Jupiter's true divine form. Jupiter then sewed the unborn Bacchus into his own thigh to complete the gestation.</note>

<meta>This page continues the critique of divine myths, moving from the birth of Jupiter to the strange origins of Bacchus and the misfortunes of Vulcan and Prometheus.</meta>

They say that he <note>Referring to Jupiter.</note> snatched away the pains of the one in labor and placed the child into his own thigh. <note>A reference to the birth of Bacchus (Dionysus). When his mother Semele died, Jupiter rescued the unborn infant by sewing him into his thigh.</note> They write of similar wonders regarding **Juno**. For the poets report that she, without the knowledge of a man, gave birth to **Vulcan** <note>Hephaestus, the god of fire and craftsmanship.</note>, who commands the winds. <note>The Latin text reads *ventis* (winds), though Vulcan is traditionally the god of fire; this may refer to the winds used to stoke the forge's bellows.</note> But not much happiness resulted for him. For, covered in constant smoke and ash, he stoops over his metals and fires as a master <gloss>as if at Etna</gloss> <note>The gloss *eca* likely refers to Mt. Etna, where Vulcan's forge was said to be located.</note> in his character as Vulcan. Those who are occupied with the **mechanical arts** <term>mechanica: manual crafts or trades, often viewed as undignified for gods or elites in classical satire.</term> suffer other indignities; furthermore, he does not walk with even feet. For they say that at the time when Jupiter cast him headlong from heaven, he was made lame in one foot. And if we listen to those who wish to be witty: because he was treated so unjustly in that fall, then just as **Astyanax** <note>The young son of Hector, who was thrown from the walls of Troy during its fall.</note>—who was cast down from a tower—our Vulcan himself would have met his end. But the aforementioned Vulcan was, in fact, fortunate enough.

<margin>Prometheus</margin>
As for **Prometheus**, what things he suffered! He was not a little more curious about human affairs than was expected. For when Jupiter had brought him to **Scythia** <note>A vast region in Eurasia, often depicted in myth as a wild, remote wilderness.</note>, he set him bound at **Mount Caucasus** and provided an eternal liver to be tortured daily; and thus he lives out his life, quite deservedly according to the dignity of his merits. But perhaps it is also necessary to commemorate these things.

<meta>This page continues the satirical critique of divine behavior, concluding the section on the scandals of the goddess of the Moon before transitioning to a poetic description of the layout of the heavens based on the works of Homer and Hesiod.</meta>

She did not blush to commit shameful acts, even when she was already older and past her prime, and though she was the mother of so many gods. Seized by love for a boy <note>Referring to the moon goddess Selene (or Diana) and her love for the mortal youth Endymion.</note>, she used to carry him about in a bed; and indeed, as long as he was still at that foolish and useless age, the less anyone summoned her to the works of **Venus** <note>Venus here represents the act of love or sexual desire.</note>, the more she loved him. Some accused her of adultery, while others accused the **Moon** <note>original: "lunā"</note> because she so often diverted her path from its very middle to go toward **Endymion**.

1 Now, having almost set aside such talk, we ascend to heaven itself, setting out from the poetry of **Homer** and **Hesiod**. We have inspected with what ornaments the higher realms were furnished, and we have heard from Homer himself that the upper parts are made of **bronze** <note>original: "ereas" (brazen). In the Iliad, Homer describes the sky as being made of bronze or iron, reflecting its strength and permanence.</note>. However, to one who is ascending and stands just a little higher, clearly upon the thresholds, the most splendid and brightest light of the sun appears; the brightest stars form the pavement, and the entire day is golden. In the very first entrance, the **Hours** <term>Horae: the goddesses of the seasons who, in mythology, were also the guardians of the gates of Olympus.</term> hold their posts. Then **Iris** and **Mercury**, acting as doorkeepers, guard the approaches; they also perform the duties of messengers and ministers for **Jupiter**. Next, **Vulcan** <note>Hephaestus, the god of fire and craftsmanship.</note> has his blacksmith shops where every craft is practiced; after that, however, is the home of the gods...

->the **basilica** <term>basilica: in this context, a grand royal hall or palace building.</term> of **Jupiter**: all of which **Vulcan** <note>The god of fire and craftsmanship, known as Hephaestus in Greek.</note> constructed with ornament and magnificence.<-

They contemplate earthly things and, leaning over, they survey everything all around with their eyes to see if from anywhere they might spot a flickering fire or one brought forth with a savory smell and a <gloss>spark</gloss> of fire enveloped in smoke; and indeed, there was a certain victim upon the altars. 

For they all feasted by gaping at the smoke, and they drank the blood poured upon the altar just like flies. However, those who took their food at home dined on **nectar** and **ambrosia** <note>Nectar and ambrosia were the traditional drink and food of the gods, which granted them immortality.</note>; and for a long time **Ixion** and **Tantalus** <note>Ixion and Tantalus were legendary figures of Greek myth who were famously invited to dine with the gods but betrayed their hospitality.</note> were at the banquet, while they enjoyed the kindness of the gods and drank together with them. But after they began to pursue everyone with insults and the insolence of their tongues, they were afflicted with torments, and even now they are being tortured. 

For mortals are now kept away from heaven. Such is the life of the gods. For these reasons, men gather for the sake of sacrifice and frequent the temples; they have dedicated the mountains and forests of all things to the gods, and individuals have consecrated birds and plants to specific gods. Then they worship them throughout the various nations, and they enroll the citizens in their cities <note>Referring to the practice of naming a specific god as the patron or "citizen" of a city-state, such as Athena for Athens.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the critique of pagan religious practices, moving from the previous discussion of gods feasting on the smoke of sacrifices and the exclusion of mortals like Ixion and Tantalus from the celestial banquet, to the specific locations where these gods are said to dwell and how they are depicted by men.</meta>

For **Delphi** and **Delos** worship **Apollo**; **Athens** [worships] **Minerva** <note>Also known as Athena; the text notes that the city's name itself proves its "familiarity" or connection to her.</note>, or the one who testifies to her familiarity by her very name; and **Argos** [worships] **Juno**; and the **Mygdonian** <note>A region in ancient Macedonia and Thrace.</note> people worship **Rhea**; the island of **Paphos** [worships] **Venus**.

And those things which happen against our will—such as the falling of rain, the flooding of rivers, and all other such things—we believe are the work of **Jupiter**. Yet he was spoken of as having died long ago and being buried among the **Cretans**. 

[Men], erecting temples for the gods so that they would not be without houses, and summoning **Praxiteles**, **Polyclitus**, or **Phidias** <note>The most famous sculptors of ancient Greece, celebrated for creating the massive gold-and-ivory statues of the gods.</note>, they erected statues in his likeness. Those who enter the temples behold a bearded **Jupiter**, but **Apollo** as an eternal boy, **Mercury** just as he is leaving his youth, **Neptune** with black hair, and **Minerva** with grey-blue eyes. 

While looking upon those statues, they do not think they are seeing ivory from **India** or gold brought from **Thrace**, but they believe the son of **Saturn** <note>Jupiter/Zeus.</note> himself and the king of the gods has been summoned to earth from the heights by **Phidias**, and that he dwells within <gloss>the statues</gloss>. Who would not wonder if someone [for five <unclear>years</unclear>?] <note>original: "ap-anos"</note> [believes] he has properly seen the divine reality on **Olympus**?

<meta>This page continues the critique of pagan religious practices. ...having established for themselves altars and, once the invocations and ritual sprinklings have been performed, they lead forward the sacrificial victims <term>hostias</term> <note>Animals destined for ritual slaughter.</note> and the plow-ox. The farmer and the shepherd [bring] a fat kid and a goat; someone else [offers] incense and a sacrificial cake <note>original: "placentā"; a flat cake often made with flour, honey, and cheese used in religious offerings.</note>. But a poor man, wishing to make the god favorable to him, merely kisses his own right hand <note>original: "dexterā quatit"; likely referring to the ancient gesture of "adoratio" where one kissed the hand and waved it toward the deity.</note>. Others, however, are sacrificing—for I wish to return to them—and crowning the victim <term>victima</term> and examining it long beforehand to see if it is deficient in any part, lest they use anything invalid in the ritual slaughter itself. They drive it to the altar and, before the eyes of the gods, they slaughter <term>mactant</term> the weeping and lowing victim. And it is considered fitting that the half-dead victim, twitching <note>original: "respondentem"; literally "responding," referring to the movements or omens observed in the dying animal.</note> well in every part, is laid down, lest anyone think the gods command them to look at these signs for knowledge. Furthermore, anyone with impure hands is forbidden from entering within for the sprinklings <note>Ritual purification involving water.</note>. Yet the priest stands there spattered with blood, and as he brings forth the entrails <term>exta</term> <note>The internal organs—especially the heart, liver, and lungs—which were examined for divinatory purposes.</note> from the cut-open body, he pulls out the heart and pours the blood around the altar. But what is it that he would not justify in the name of piety? For when the fire has consumed everything and he has placed the goat's fleece upon it, the "divine odor" is raised up and, just as if it were the animal itself <term>pecudem</term>, it sets out toward heaven, little by little and dispersed.

<meta>This page continues the critique of religious rituals, moving from general sacrificial practices to specific regional customs and the zoomorphic (animal-shaped) deities of Egypt. It maintains the skeptical tone regarding how humans attempt to influence the divine.</meta>

The Scythians <note>original: "Sythę"; ancient nomadic people of Central Eurasia, often characterized in classical texts as having distinct, sometimes harsh, religious customs.</note>, however, setting aside all other sacrificial victims <term>hostias</term> <note>Animals offered for sacrifice.</note> because they consider them somewhat lowly, desire to seek the favor of Diana herself with oxen. By this means, they believe the goddess is brought back to a state of love and benevolence. These practices are perhaps moderate, as are those performed by the Assyrians and others, and the modest rites of the Phrygians. 

But if you were to come to Egypt, you would see many things supposedly "venerable" and worthy of heaven. For you would observe Jupiter the Greatest <note>original: "iovem optimū"; likely referring to the Egyptian god Amun, whom the Romans identified with Jupiter.</note> worshipped in the form of a ram, and Mercury in the likeness of a dog <note>Referring to Anubis, the jackal-headed god.</note>. You would see Pan as a complete goat, and another as an Egyptian bird <note>likely the Ibis, sacred to Thoth.</note>, another as a crocodile, and yet another as a Syrian <note>This may refer to the fish-goddess Atargatis or other Syrian deities often depicted with animal attributes.</note>. 

If, however, you desire to understand these things more clearly, listen to what is told by the sophists <note>Teachers and intellectuals in the Greek world.</note>, writers, and various distinguished men. First, they use a proclamation of this sort: "Approach the gates, O ye profane!" <note>original: "Portas aggredimini o profani"; a traditional formula used to warn the uninitiated to stay away from sacred mysteries.</note>, suggesting that the gods, fearing the rebellion of enemies and giants, came into Egypt as if they were going to destroy their enemies here. It is well known that one god took the form of a goat, another the form of a ram out of fear, and another the image of a bird; for this reason, these forms are still preserved for the gods to this day. These accounts lie neglected, written in the inner sanctums <note>original: "aditis"; the most sacred, restricted parts of a temple.</note> for more than a thousand years. <meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion on Egyptian ritual and Greek skepticism to a new section: a 15th-century Latin translation of Lucian of Samosata’s "Patriae Encomium" (Praise of One's Fatherland). The translator, Antonio Pacino, begins his preface by dedicating the work to Piero Pazzi, shifting the tone from religious critique to Renaissance Humanist concerns regarding civic duty and the love of one's home.</meta>

## Antonio Pacino’s Preface to the little work of Lucian
## regarding the praises of the fatherland: translated by him from the Greek lan-
## -guage into Latin, dedicated to the most noble
## young man Piero Pazzi <note>original: "petrum Pazzum"; Piero Pazzi (1416–1464) was a prominent Florentine nobleman, bibliophile, and member of the Pazzi family, known for his interest in Humanist scholarship.</note>, a citizen of Flor-
## -ence ————

Ulysses of Ithaca <note>original: "Ulixes hytacus"; the Latin name for the Greek hero Odysseus.</note>, whom they say was a man of the greatest wisdom, wandered many various seas in a long journey after Troy was conquered, and was driven to the shores of Circe by bold winds. There, while Circe held him with her for a long time, she treated him with great honor and great pleasures, and she strove with the greatest zeal and strength so that she might not be deprived of his presence and companionship. Therefore, after making the greatest and most abundant promises of all possible pleasures, she said she would make him a god if, setting aside his return home, he were willing to lead his life with her.

<margin>✓ Though Ulysses was burdened <note>original: "honeraretur"; likely a variant of *oneraretur* (burdened or weighed down), though the context suggests he was being "overwhelmed" by her offers.</note> by such immense promises, he was nevertheless overcome by love for his fatherland and preferred to return to that small and rocky Ithaca rather than accept the most delightful pleasures and the enjoyment of divinity. By this, the opinion of this most wise man declares how dear the fatherland is, and how we are held by such a wonderful love for it.</margin>

<summary>This page contains the opening of Antonio Pacino's preface to his translation of Lucian’s "Praise of the Fatherland," using the story of Ulysses choosing his rocky home over Circe's offer of immortality to illustrate the power of patriotism.</summary>
<keywords>Antonio Pacino, Lucian of Samosata, Piero Pazzi, Ulysses, Ithaca, Circe, Humanism, patriotism, Florence</keywords>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues Antonio Pacino’s preface to his translation of Lucian. It completes the comparison between the Greek hero Ulysses’ love for his humble home and the dedication of this work to the Florentine nobleman Piero Pazzi. The translator justifies his choice of text by arguing that love for one’s fatherland is a universal and noble virtue.</meta>

...ment and judged the fatherland to be more delightful than all human affairs, especially since it was not Mycenae, nor Lacedaemon, nor Athens—those most illustrious cities—but only that rugged soil that was his fatherland. You might argue that if he had been born in the most wealthy cities, we would think him far less deserving of such great admiration. But since he preferred to return to the village of Ithaca <note>original: "hytacam"; referring to the island home of Odysseus/Ulysses.</note>, a most unrefined place situated among uneven rocks, rather than become a god, this truly seems to strike our minds with no small admiration. 

Therefore, as I wished to present you with the fruits produced by my labors and studies, I did not know what more precious or more pleasant gifts I could send you than these *Praises of the Fatherland*. Since nothing should be more pleasant or more lovable to you than this, I judge that these praises of the fatherland will not be unwelcome to you. For men are usually affected by the greatest pleasure when those things they love dearly are praised in their presence. 

It was also added that, since you possess a fatherland that is both most splendid and most illustrious <note>The translator is likely flattering Piero Pazzi by equating the general "fatherland" of Lucian's text with the city of Florence.</note>, I thought I would gratify you more fully if I translated the praises of <margin>that</margin> <note>original: "illius"; the marginal note clarifies that the praises are for "that" fatherland (the subject of the book).</note> place from the Greek speech of Lucian into the Latin tongue in your name. But so, exceedingly...

<summary>Antonio Pacino concludes his introductory anecdote about Ulysses, arguing that the hero's preference for rocky Ithaca over immortality proves the supreme value of one's homeland. He then dedicates his Latin translation of Lucian's "Praises of the Fatherland" to Piero Pazzi, framing the scholarship as a gift that honors Pazzi's own "splendid" city of Florence.</summary>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page concludes Antonio Pacino’s personal dedication to Piero Pazzi and begins the formal translation of Lucian’s "Patriae Encomium" (In Praise of the Fatherland). The translator moves from praising Pazzi's individual character to the universal theme of devotion to one's home.</meta>

What prompted me to make you worthy of this matter was your excellent character <note>original: "indoles"; referring to innate disposition or talent.</note>, your most noble intellect <note>original: "ingenium"</note>, and your incredible kindness <note>original: "humanitas"; a key Renaissance term for the culture and refinement that makes one truly human.</note>—by which many have acquired the highest glory—and your immense desire for learning. In all things, you have provided your fellow citizens <note>original: "ciuibus"</note> with great expectations of you. For there is no doubt that, by these excellent virtues of yours—if the Fate <note>original: "parca"; Pacino refers to the Parcae, the three goddesses of destiny, hoping that death does not cut Pazzi's life short.</note> does not snatch you away with a bitter and undeserved death—you will turn out to be a most eminent and famous man. Therefore, you will receive this little gift <note>original: "munusculum"; a modest term for his literary work.</note>, and you may expect that I shall give you even greater things in the future.

<margin>The fatherland is more pleasant than all things.</margin>
Since it has long been agreed by the judgment of all that mortals can receive nothing sweeter than their own fatherland, the fatherland is more pleasant than all things. But what else is more worthy of greater honor? What is more divine? For among all things that are considered divine by the common consent of men, this fatherland—which provided life, nourishment, and education <note>original: "eruditionem"</note>—is both the source and the teacher. Many admire the beautiful size, splendor, and magnificence of cities. Yet all are affected by love for their fatherland. For which reason, though other [cities] may be most powerful...

<summary>Antonio Pacino concludes his flattering dedication to Piero Pazzi, predicting his future greatness, and begins the translated text of Lucian's "In Praise of the Fatherland," arguing that one's home is the source of life and education.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Latin translation of Lucian’s "Patriae Encomium" (In Praise of the Fatherland). The text transitions from observing the external beauty of foreign cities to the internal, moral duty of loving one’s own home, regardless of its relative splendor.</meta>

No one, by looking down <note>original: "despiciens"; looking with contempt upon one's own humble origins compared to grander cities.</note> [on their own], is truly deceived. Therefore, it is not right that
we should fall into forgetting our homeland because of our admiration
for the rare excellence of others. Whoever, therefore,
considers it a <insert><unclear>greater</unclear></insert> <note>original: "maiori"</note> thing to have had the good fortune

distressed in his soul <note>original: "animo"</note> if he sees another city more adorned
than his own homeland. To me, it only adds to the joy
to venerate the very name of the fatherland. But
truly, it is appropriate for those who wish to compare
cities to investigate their size,
beauty, and other ornaments.
But when a choice of cities must be made,
no one, having abandoned his homeland, thinks that a more
charming place should be chosen as his own <note>original: "priam"; likely a contraction for "propriam" (one's own).</note>;
instead, each person prays that his own home might
become like those splendid cities. For though he might choose
whatever his soul desires, the same thing is clearly done
by just sons and the best parents.
For a son who is endowed with beauty and goodness
will not treat another man with greater honor than his own father.
Nor will a father, neglecting his own son, be moved
by love toward another youth; but parents <note>original: "pres"; likely an abbreviation for "parentes" (parents).</note>...

The text transitions from the general duty of a son to his father to the higher duty one owes to the fatherland, arguing that even the gods prioritize their places of birth over the rest of the world.</meta>

...overcome by love, they are all the more affected toward their own kin when they see them surpassing others in size, beauty, and all other ornaments and virtues in the eyes of all men. Any father who does not feel this way toward his son would hardly seem to have a father's eyes <note>original: "prīs hrē oculos"; a contraction for "patris habere oculos," meaning to see with the natural affection of a parent.</note>. The name of the Fatherland far exceeds all other things in its primacy and intimacy, for nothing is more familiar to us than our homeland. If anyone honors his father for his merits and dignity—as both law and nature <note>original: "nā"; abbreviation for "natura."</note> command—he will deservedly honor his fatherland <note>original: "prīam"; a contraction for "patriam."</note> with even greater honor than anything else. For even the father himself is a possession of the fatherland, as are all the other ancestors, back through the lineage and progression of the gods who were born in that land.

<margin>
The gods rejoice in the fatherland
</margin>

For the gods also rejoice in their fatherland and, as is right, they watch over all things that concern men. Though they consider the whole earth and all the seas to be their possessions, they favor the city from which they drew their origin with greater honor than other cities. For they hold those cities that serve as the fatherlands of the gods—where their lineages <note>original: "gnā"; abbreviation for "genera," referring to the divine family lines or ancestral origins.</note> are celebrated—to be more venerable and divine. These sacrifices, therefore...

<summary>This page argues that the fatherland is the ultimate source of identity, claiming that even the gods hold their places of birth in higher esteem than the rest of the world. It establishes a hierarchy of honor where the duty to one's country surpasses even the duty to one's father.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the translation of Lucian’s "Patriae Encomium." Having established that gods favor their own birthplaces, the text now argues that humans owe an even greater debt to their fatherland as the site of their first sensory and intellectual experiences.</meta>

62.

It is thought pleasing to the gods <note>original: "dijs"; a common variant of "diis."</note> when anyone, returning home, performs a religious rite. Therefore, if the name of their birthplace <note>original: "pariennomen"; likely a contraction of "pariendi nomen," referring to the place of one's labor or birth.</note> is held in honor by the gods, what reason is there that the fatherland <note>original: "patria"</note> itself should not be much more venerated by men? Clearly, all men first saw the sun from their fatherland. <margin>They saw the sun in their own land</margin> Although this god [the Sun] is common to all, he must be considered as "belonging to the fatherland" <note>original: "patrium"</note> by each person; for we first beheld him from the fatherland itself. There we also first learned to use our own voice and speech, and there we first came to know God. If, however, it happens that one is allotted such a fatherland that there is need for another, greater training <term>disciplinam</term><note>Here meaning formal education or specialized skill beyond basic upbringing.</note> in affairs, one should at least have gratitude <note>original: "grām"; abbreviation for "gratiam."</note> toward the fatherland for these initial teachings. For one did not know the name of a city before this training, nor did he learn to have a life without the fatherland.

¶ I clearly believe that all men acquire training and education <note>original: "eruditionēq"; abbreviation for "eruditionemque."</note> for themselves so that they may make themselves far more useful to their fatherland. For those who seek and acquire wealth do so for the sake of generosity and magnificence, so that they might be able to provide for the expenses of the state <note>original: "rei .p."; abbreviation for "rei publicae," the republic or public affairs.</note>. I believe this is rightly done. For one must not be unmindful of a benefit received. Indeed, if among men gratitude must be returned for a favor, according to the dignity of the deed itself...

<summary>This page argues that the fatherland is the source of a person's first light, language, and knowledge of God, making it the most venerable of all places. <meta>This page concludes the argument regarding the innate love for one's fatherland. ...they might see others adorned and wealthy with wide and lush fields for grazing, and with various spreading plains or orchards, nevertheless they themselves by no means forget the praise of their fatherland. And if they were offered the gift of immortality <note>original: "divinitate donari"; literally "to be gifted with divinity," implying eternal life.</note>, they would consider it a thing not to be desired because of that longing for an ancestral grave <note>Refers to the ancient Greek and Roman cultural importance of being buried in one's family plot.</note> which dwells in our minds. 

<margin>The smoke of the fatherland is brighter.</margin> 
Truly, the smoke of one's own fatherland would seem brighter to anyone than the fire of a foreign land. <note>This is a reference to a classical proverb, famously used in Lucian's "Patriae Encomium," suggesting that even the most humble sign of home (smoke) is better than the greatest comforts (fire) of a strange place.</note> Thus, clearly all men judge their fatherland worthy of honor; for this reason, we see that lawmakers everywhere have established the greatest punishments <note>Referring to the punishment of exile or banishment, which was considered a "civil death."</note> for those who betray it.

But in military law <term>iure militari</term> <note>The specialized legal code and traditions governing the conduct and discipline of soldiers.</note>, things are not quite like this. For they judge it most effective for soldiers in the heat of battle to be reminded that the war is being pressed for the sake of the fatherland. Whoever hears this prepares himself bravely for the fight itself; for the name of the fatherland makes a brave man even out of a coward.

<meta>This page marks the beginning of a new treatise by the humanist scholar Guarino da Verona. # The book of the most famous man, Guarinus of Verona, to the illustrious Lord Leonellus d'Este, on the difference between a friend and a flatterer, begins auspiciously.

<margin>P</margin>
<margin>Plato.</margin>
It has been recorded in memory, Prince Leonellus <note>Leonello d'Este (1407–1450), the Marquis of Ferrara and a noted patron of Renaissance humanism.</note>, that Plato—a most learned man and easily the foremost of philosophers <note>original: "phor̄", an abbreviation for "philosophorum."</note>—once sailed to Sicily for this specific purpose: to instruct and train Dionysius the Younger <note>Dionysius II, the tyrant of Syracuse. Plato’s voyages to Sicily were a famous attempt to realize the "philosopher-king" ideal.</note>, a youth indeed of excellent natural talent and singular promise, in discipline and the liberal arts <term>bonis artibus</term> <note>Literally "good arts," referring to the studies—such as rhetoric, ethics, and music—worthy of a free man.</note>. For that wisest of men understood that just as a city is often infected by the desires and vices of its princes, it is likewise adorned and corrected by their righteous conduct and self-restraint. He believed, therefore, that by the example of a well-instructed youth, both individual subjects and entire populations could either be passionately inspired toward virtuous character or restored to their former health, bringing happiness to all. Indeed, Plato’s own virtue and diligence would have granted him his wish <note>original: "voti compotem," a classical idiom meaning to have one's prayer or desire fulfilled.</note>, for the beginnings of the endeavor were so well-matched to the man’s magnificent plans.

<vocab>Guarinus Veronensis, Leonellus Estensis, Plato, Dionysius, Sicilia, amicitia, assentator</vocab>

Plutarch <note>Plutarch (c. 46 – 119 AD) was a Greek philosopher and biographer. His essay "How to tell a flatterer from a friend" is the basis for this text.</note> is a man illustrious for the defenses he provides, for his teaching, for the actual strictness of his mastery, and for his most prudent method of living rightly; he is certainly a philosopher to be admired. He himself will dutifully protect and advise you and other princes, and will arm you against those most ferocious beasts: the flatterers. 

The goal is that, once their snares are uncovered, you may distinguish the flatterer from the friend, embracing the <margin>$\oint$</margin> latter with kindness and sweetness, while harshly and relentlessly repelling and abominating the former. Plutarch knew well that the flatterer’s nest and 
<margin>$\oint$</margin> refuge was established especially within the courts of the powerful. <meta>This page continues Guarino da Verona’s preface. On the previous page, Guarino explained that self-love is the "handle" by which a flatterer grabs hold of a victim. He ended by suggesting that when a person is too eager for praise, they lose the ability to judge themselves truly.</meta>

[those things he es]timated <note>This completes the word *existimatis* from the previous page, meaning "judged" or "valued."</note> certainly ought not to be praised: since it involves false and doubtful things in great errors.

The former [disposition] <note>Referring to the natural human drive for truth.</note> especially touches upon human nature; for "we are all," as Cicero says, "drawn and led by a desire for knowledge and science": <note>original: "omnes... trahimur: et ducimur ad cognitionis et scientię cupiditatem"; a quote from Cicero’s *De Officiis* 1.18.</note> in which we consider it a beautiful thing to excel. However, to slip, to err, to be deceived, and to be ignorant, we consider both an evil and a disgrace.

But if truth is primarily a divine gift and the "cause and origin of all good things for gods and men alike"—as one reads in Plato <note>A reference to Plato’s *Laws* 730c, where he describes truth as the beginning of all goodness.</note>—it follows that a flatterer <term>assentator</term> <note>Specifically one who agrees with everything said, regardless of the truth.</note> is an enemy to the gods: especially to Apollo, whom the poets called "Pythian"; for the flatterer opposes Apollo's oracle in the most extreme way. That oracle is: **Know thyself.** <note>The famous Greek maxim *Gnothi Seauton* inscribed at the Temple of Apollo at Delphi.</note>

For since truth is defined by Marcus Tullius <note>The Roman orator Cicero.</note> as "that through which things that are, or were, or will be, are stated without change," <margin>truth</margin> <note>original: "veritas... per quam ea quę sunt: aut fuerunt aut futura sunt īmutata dicuntur"; from Cicero's *De Inventione* 2.161.</note> the flatterer envelops each person in a singular blindness and in their own ignorance. For he does not allow the mind to discern what goods and what evils are within it, darkening the mind, spinning frauds, and substituting fictions for truths; he creates an ignorance of one's own true nature, so that those qualities that ought to have been perfected and adorned through study and diligence...

<summary>Guarino argues that flattery is a divine offense because it violates the Delphic command to "know thyself." By substituting lies for truth, the flatterer prevents a person from recognizing their own flaws and virtues, effectively blinding the mind.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Guarino da Verona’s discussion on the dangers of flattery. 66.

...making [them] defective and unpolished. But if the flatterer <term>assentator</term> <note>Literally "one who agrees"; a person who uses constant agreement as a tool for manipulation.</note> only attacked the humble and common people, it would surely not be such a grave and horrible monster.

<margin>A</margin> Just as worms crawl into soft matter, so [flattery] creeps into the hearts of the modest and those who love honor. Once flattery <term>adulatio</term> is admitted within and begins to gnaw deep inside, whole principalities <term>principatus</term> and empires <term>imperia</term> are dragged into ruin and 

<margin>†</margin> destruction. For these reasons, there is a need for foresight and no small amount of attention, since flattery can be identified by certain signs. This is necessary so that it does not bring about both damage and accusations while wearing the mask of a friendly person. Above all, one may notice that the flatterer does not prey upon the downcast, the beggars, or the weak; instead, he falls upon those who are outstanding in either fortune <term>fortuna</term> or dignity. This is the nature of 

<margin>} lice</margin> 

lice. <margin>X</margin> Just as they slip away when a human body grows cold after death, [so do flatterers leave when wealth fades]. Nor indeed should one wait to test a flatterer until favorable fortune has already left and the winds of adversity have begun to blow; for at that point, [the test] is not useful, nor is the flatterer then harmful or dangerous <note>The author implies that once you are poor, the flatterer has already vanished, making it too late to "detect" them to save yourself.</note>. It is indeed difficult and troublesome that...

<summary>Guarino describes flattery as a "monster" that destroys empires by preying on the powerful and honorable. He famously compares flatterers to lice, who only stay on a body while it is warm and full of life, abandoning their host the moment fortune fades.</summary>
