--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues Guarino da Verona's treatise on discerning friends from flatterers. The previous page ended with the observation that flatterers are like lice: they cling to a body while it is warm with wealth and status, but vanish once the "body" of fortune grows cold. Guarino is now emphasizing the necessity of testing friends before a crisis occurs.</meta>

...at that time, you realize you have no friend, just when the situation itself most demands friends. At such a moment, you are unable to exchange an inconstant and deceitful person for one who is firm and true <insert>if</insert> <margin>friend</margin>. On the contrary, like money <term>pecunia</term> <note>The author compares testing a friend's character to "proving" the purity of a coin.</note>, a friend should be tested and known through experience; he ought to be stored in the place of a treasury <term>thesauri</term> before the day of necessity arrives.

Otherwise, if you try to test a flatterer <term>assentator</term> <strike>the thing</strike> while the damage of a wound is already pressing down, it will surely happen as it does with lethal poison to those who test it: one’s life is over even as the judgment is being perceived and decided.

Furthermore, since a friend is counted among things that are honorable <term>honesta</term> and useful <term>utilia</term>, I would not want us to immediately believe that those who have spoken somewhat to gain favor, or who have conducted their speech with sweetness, are harsh flatterers. For from this comes the joy and sweetness of friendship <term>amicitia</term> itself. Nor should we believe that a name so lovable, so honorable, and so sought after by everyone was attained through bitterness and mere authority. 

On the contrary, whatever good and whatever honor friendship possesses, it carries tempered with a certain sweetness, pleasantness, and desire. Therefore, [this] desire and its "contagions" <term>contagia</term> <note>In this context, *contagia* refers to the way emotions or qualities "touch" or influence others, similar to the modern idea of an "infectious" personality.</note> hold those dwellings <term>habitacula</term>. Nor is the sight of a good friend pleasant only to those in the midst of calamities...

<summary>Guarino argues that friends must be tested and "treasured" before a crisis, warning that discovering a flatterer during a disaster is as fatal as testing poison by drinking it. He also defends the idea that true friendship should be characterized by sweetness and joy rather than just stern authority.</summary>
<keywords>friendship, flattery, testing character, loyalty, social ethics, Guarino da Verona, treasury of friends</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how to distinguish a true friend from a flatterer. Having previously warned that one should test a friend’s character before a crisis occurs, the author now addresses the difficulty of this task: because friendship is naturally pleasant and sweet, a flatterer can easily mimic these qualities to gain entry into one’s confidence.</meta>

<page-num>67.</page-num>

...it is: but it also brings favor, delight, and the seasonings of life everywhere to the fortunate. And just as we perceive that no small pleasure for mortals and convenience for our uses proceeds from fire, so when God implanted friendship into our lives, He mixed it with all things and willed it to be filled with joy, sweetness, and gentleness <note>original: "hilaritatis dulcedinis: lenitatis: p̃r̃e [plene/pariter] refertā eẽ voluit"</note>. 

Since the clever flatterer <term>assentator</term> <note>literally "one who always agrees"</note> understands this to be the case—and that friendship is by no means an enemy to pleasures—he enters the ears and the mind by the guidance of pleasure. Just as debased gold <note>meaning counterfeit or low-quality gold</note> imitates the yellow appearance and the refined look of pure gold, so also the flatterer expresses only the sweetness and the gracious encounters of a friend. He shows a cheerful brow and a flourishing face, provided that he holds to his purpose: namely, to comply with all [our] ways <note>original: "mrīs" likely for *moribus* (customs) or *modis* (ways)</note> and to oppose none. 

Since these things are so, if there should be someone who—having first displayed examples from antiquity—extols with praise your moderation, your generosity, your clemency, your easy accessibility, and your other virtues, you certainly ought not immediately suspect him to be in the number of flatterers. For praise <term>laus</term> is a part of friendship, and no less...

<summary>This page explores why flattery is so deceptive, noting that since true friendship is naturally joyful and pleasant, flatterers can easily mimic these traits like counterfeit gold. However, the author notes that receiving praise for one's virtues does not automatically mean the speaker is a flatterer.</summary>
<keywords>friendship, flattery, pleasure, virtue, praise, deception, gold metaphor, social graces</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on distinguishing a true friend from a flatterer, moving from the role of praise to the manner in which corrections and reproofs should be handled within a friendship.</meta>

...that refutations <note>original: "redarguitiones"</note> should not be made at times when they seem to be relevant <note>In this context, meaning that corrections should not be merely opportunistic or harsh</note>. On the contrary, nothing can contribute so much to the dissolving of the bond of friendship and intimacy as a peevishness <note>original: "morositas"</note> applied to all things, whether said or done, and a "censor’s brow" <note>An allusion to the Roman Censors, who were known for their stern moral oversight</note>, so to speak. Meanwhile, on the other hand, benevolence returns rich and ready praises for benefits and merit. 

Admonitions, likewise, and bold speech should be done not unkindly but amiably: let them be done gently and without heat <note>meaning without anger or passion; original: "nullo cum ardore"</note>, and the friend bears them with a calm mind. 

Therefore, someone will say that a great difficulty is presented in distinguishing a flatterer <term>assentator</term> <note>literally "one who always agrees"</note> from a friend: if they have no distinction between them in terms of praises or pleasure—especially when, in ministering and performing services, one may often see the flatterer appearing more attentive than the friend. 

Therefore, those who do not disdain to take these small commentaries <note>original: "comentariolos"</note> into their hands, we advise so that they may recognize both: let them observe that there are two types of flatterers. One is skillful <note>original: "artificiosum"</note>, and the other is simple <note>original: "simplex"</note>. As for this second kind which we mention, it is not obscure or hidden, nor does it offer itself uninvited; as for instance...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on types of flatterers. This aligns with the previous page's focus on identifying different degrees of sycophancy.</meta>

...no one except the plainly superfluous would remind us: just as no one would be ignorant of those
<margin>Cyprian</margin> female flatterers of Cyprus who, having left their native soil and crossed over into Syria, earned the nickname of "the stairs" <note>original: "scalarum"; these women, historically known as *klimakides*, were said to prostrate themselves so that royal women could use their bodies as steps to mount horses or carriages</note>: because they would prostrate themselves in the manner of harlots before those about to rule wealthy kingdoms, and offer themselves to be stepped upon and trampled.

<margin>A</margin> Again, there is a type of flatterer whom you should carefully avoid: those who, while they by no means profess to be flatterers, appear least like them. They do not frequent taverns; they do not count the shadows of the lines <note>original: "linearum umbras"; a reference to the ancient practice of telling time for dinner invitations by measuring the length of one's shadow on a sundial</note> for the meal themselves; they are almost constantly sober investigators of affairs for the conduct of all things: and they may advise on secrets so that they are admitted as participants at the critical moment. Since these men present themselves as semi-serious and severe men, they are enemies worthy of everyone's hatred: For as Plato <note>A reference to Plato's *Republic*, 361a</note> defines it: the extreme kind of injustice <note>original: "iniustitie"</note> is when, though you are unjust, you so pretend that you seem to be just. This flattery <note>original: "adulatio"</note> must be considered difficult to detect: it does not reveal itself but hides; it does not produce jokes but handles serious matters; it also creates its own industry and snares, so that even true things may sometimes appear suspicious and full of faithlessness.

<summary>This page contrasts the debased "ladder-women" of Cyprus, who served as literal footstools for royalty, with a more dangerous class of "serious" flatterers. These sophisticated manipulators avoid the typical behaviors of parasites, instead using a mask of sobriety and honest counsel to infiltrate the secrets of the powerful.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the "skillful" flatterer, specifically how he uses comparisons to manipulate his target. While the previous page used the metaphor of a sieve to distinguish between friendship and flattery, this section details the specific rhetorical tricks—the "snares"—used to make the flatterer appear inferior in virtues but equal or worse in vices.</meta>

<margin>
Snares
</margin>
He weaves the snares of deceit. For in making comparisons, whenever mention of good qualities arises, he always attributes the highest excellence and the greatest part to the other <note>The person being flattered</note>: even though in all respects and by right he may be equal and similar, he insists that he takes second place and is always left as the lesser. But on the other hand, in evils and vices, he claims an equal place for himself. "You are indeed somewhat difficult; but I am harsh. You are superstitious; I am distraught <note>original: *lymphatus*—a term meaning driven to a frenzy or "nymph-struck," used here to suggest a more extreme mental state than mere superstition</note>. You are prone to anger; I am insane. You were laughing a little foolishly; I was absolutely dying of it. You sleep a long time, I admit; but in sleep, I surpass the dormice <note>Dormice were famous in antiquity for their long periods of hibernation; the flatterer claims to be even lazier to make the target feel better</note>. You give lavishly, but I squander." Conversely, in good things, it is a pleasure to hear him yielding: "How swift I am in the charge; but you do not merely run, you fly. I ride a horse not ungracefully; but you move like a centaur <note>A mythological creature, half-man and half-horse; here used to describe someone who is "at one" with their horse</note>. I do not make unpleasant songs; but you are another Orpheus or Amphion <note>Legendary musicians of Greek myth whose music could move stones and tame wild beasts</note>." When a friend praises truths, you will not see him pouring out praises with such heat; note that he keeps to his own business in both praises and censures, following the rule of "nothing in excess" <note>original: *nequid nimis*—a famous ancient Greek maxim advising moderation in all things</note>. These and similar differences between the flatterer and the friend in making comparisons [can be seen...]

<summary>This page describes how a flatterer uses tactical self-deprecation to exalt his target, claiming to be worse in vice and inferior in virtue. It contrasts this hyperbolic behavior with the moderation and honesty of a true friend.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous leaf, where the author began distinguishing the "pleasure" (voluptas) provided by a friend from that of a flatterer. <margin>Scaevola</margin> You know that Scaevola often played ball with his people,
<margin>Alexander</margin> and that King Alexander <note>Alexander the Great</note> frequently did the same 
with his friends. You ought to feel less indignant,
Prince Leonello, toward his playfulness, 
as you exercise yourself nimbly and most aptly;
since it served as a relaxation from labors and 
even for the health of the body, 
so much should you emulate the king. Add to this
the symposia <term>symposia: Greek-style drinking parties or banquets accompanied by intellectual discussion</term> of Plato,
Xenophon, and so many of the most moderate philosophers,
in which pleasure was admitted for the sake of recreating the mind. 
Furthermore, no one who is even moderately 
learned is ignorant of the jokes of the ancients 
and of great men—a custom in which 
almost all the Socratics <note>Followers of the philosopher Socrates</note> and our own Plautus <note>Titus Maccius Plautus, the famous Roman comic playwright</note> excelled.
And Marcus Tullius <note>Cicero, the Roman orator</note>, whose wit is attested

is said to have composed regarding his patron's jokes.
But the flatterer <term>assentator: literally "one who always agrees," a sycophant or "yes-man"</term> sets this end,
this mind, and this purpose for himself: 
that he intends and directs play, 
or advice, or action toward pleasure 
and for the sake of pleasure. 
And to finish in a few words, 
the flatterer thinks that in order to be pleasant, everything 
must be both done and endured. 
The friend, however, continually does those things 
which ought to be done...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the distinction between a true friend and a flatterer. The previous page established that while a friend may provide pleasure, their ultimate goal is different from that of a sycophant. This page uses the analogy of a physician to explain why a friend must sometimes be "bitter" or harsh to be truly helpful.</meta>

<page-num>72.</page-num>

...acting often as a pleasant person and often as an unpleasant one, achieving this: not establishing this or that for its own sake, but if the situation requires it, avoiding neither. This is also the case in the practice of physicians <term>medicis: doctors or healers</term>. For sometimes they wash the sick person gently, anoint them lightly, and in a certain humane way offer sweet medicines <term>pharmaca: medicinal drugs or potions</term>. But sometimes, heavily crushing heavy ingredients like hellebore <note>original: "eleborum" — A plant used in ancient medicine to treat mental illness and physical ailments; it was notoriously bitter and dangerous in high doses.</note> and oil, they force the patient to drink a bitter potion—not proposing bitterness in the one case, nor sweetness in the other, as a goal for himself, }
but seeking in both instances the good health of the patient. }
In the same way, I believe a friend must act }
so that through both favor and praise he may cultivate <note>original: "aret" — likely from "arare," to plow or cultivate, though the scribe's usage here implies the labor of striving toward a goal.</note> his duty more eagerly. From time to time, according to the matter at hand, speech must be lashed, and biting oratory must be employed, so that with both a heavier tone of voice and a more unrestrained word, a careful admonition <term>commonefactio: a formal reminder of duty or a stern warning</term> may rouse the soul to virtue if it has been negligent in any way. For in order to help a friend, it is necessary to make him sad; but you should not make him sad so that it destroys the friendship. Thus it happens that a friend is not always sweet and is sometimes harsh, yet is found to be perpetually useful. But the flatterer <term>assentator: a "yes-man" or sycophant</term>, always following one and the same line toward pleasure and toward favor...

<summary>The author compares a true friend to a doctor, explaining that just as a physician must sometimes prescribe bitter medicine to restore health, a friend must sometimes use harsh words to guide a companion toward virtue.</summary>
<keywords>friendship vs. flattery, medical analogy, hellebore, virtue, duty, admonition, ethics of speech</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the distinction between a true friend and a flatterer. While the previous page used the analogy of a bitter medicine to justify a friend's occasional harshness, this page examines the flatterer’s habit of constant agreement and how to distinguish genuine praise from empty sycophancy.</meta>

...accustomed to speak or be silent: he knows neither how to oppose nor to sadden by deed or word so that he might bring help; instead, he aims to follow every movement of the mouth and every nod at all times. It is truly a royal thing <note>original: "Regale nimirum" — implying a noble or kingly quality of character</note>: that praises should be freely offered by those who also have the will to rebuke. For this must be considered the duty of friendship <term>amicitia: in this context, a bond based on virtue rather than mere utility</term> when someone is so gracious and cheering that he is also willing to be harsh and opposing whenever the situation requires it. But it is not fitting for a man to live with perpetual pleasure and grace [that is] <unclear>mermariu~</unclear> <unclear>tiseq</unclear> devoid of all correction <term>castigatio: formal correction or discipline meant to improve character</term> and full of suspicion.

<margin>+</margin> Judgment <term>iudicium: the power of discerning or making a moral decision</term> must be awakened and caution must be applied; with vigilance <term>vigilantia: watchful care</term>, one must discern whether the person themselves or their deeds are being praised. Praises ought to be given more sparingly [than...] For the praise of a deed is truly celebrated when those who are absent are praised more than those present: and not only we, but also all those endowed with the same character seem to be commended. And what is most important, let us pay attention: if, when we are praised, we are led instead by repentance <term>penitentia: a feeling of regret or a turning away from faults</term> and shame <term>pudore: a sense of modesty or healthy embarrassment</term>, lest we should wish to have spoken or acted to the contrary. That is a sign not of true praise, but of a fabricated commendation <term>collaudatio: a formal or shared praise</term>.

<summary>This page distinguishes a true friend’s duty—which includes the willingness to be harsh or offer correction—from the flatterer’s constant, subservient agreement. It suggests that genuine praise should focus on deeds rather than the person and should inspire humility rather than pride.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on discerning true friendship from flattery. The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing how a friend's praise should lead to internal reflection and repentance rather than pride. This page begins by completing the word "judgment" (iudicium).</meta>

...judgment is internal: for judgment <term>iudicium: the capacity for moral discernment</term> and the witness of our own minds, which opposes and rejects your praises, is sincere and uncorrupted, and has by no means fallen into the power of the flatterer <term>assentatoris: one who habitually agrees or flatters to gain favor</term>. But in our own case, I cannot help but wonder and even feel indignant when I see so many people so out of their minds that they shrink from their own ailments; in their calamities <term>calamitatibus: misfortunes or disasters</term>, they admit no comfort, but instead find more and more delight in their own weeping and lamentation.

<margin>So</margin> Likewise, those who have committed faults think that if anyone tries to correct those faults—instilling a sting of regret and repentance by criticizing them—that person is an enemy and an accuser. On the contrary, whoever approves of their deeds and speaks well of them, they greet; they rejoice in his arrival, embrace him, and receive him as a benevolent and true friend. 

We must take great care lest we fall into this particular vice. Against the arts of the flatterer, all skill should be applied and every effort expended, lest his cunning subvert us—especially <term>praesertim: particularly or above all</term> in this one matter: that such people confuse the boundaries of words, mixing up virtues <term>virtutes: moral excellences</term> and vices <term>vitia: moral failings</term> because of their similarities. As was said by the satirist <term>satyro: a writer of satire, such as Juvenal or Horace</term>: "he who turns black into white" <note>original: "qui nigrum in candida vertit"</note>, so that shame is cast upon vices and...

<summary>This page explores the psychological tendency to reject painful but necessary correction while embracing the comforting lies of flatterers. It warns that flatterers subvert the truth by intentionally confusing the definitions of virtue and vice.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of how a flatterer manipulates language to deceive their target. It follows the previous discussion on how a flatterer "turns black into white," effectively blurring the lines between good and evil behavior.</meta>

...follows virtue. It is worthwhile to note when he calls a spendthrift <term>prodigum: one who wastes money recklessly</term> "generous" <note>original: "liberalem"</note>. He adorns fear with a somber name. He calls violence a sharp intellect. Cowardice <term>pusillanimitatem: literally 'small-spiritedness' or a lack of courage</term> he calls modesty. He calls his own lustful pursuit "familiarity in studies." He labels the hot-tempered man "exalted and manly," and the weak and humble man "humane." 

The listener, captured by these flattering descriptions, grows accustomed to delighting in vices as if they were virtues, and feels not the slightest repentance for his transgressions. 

The practice of this disease and plague—to borrow a theme from the ancient poets—brought about the ruin of Troy. This happened when Paris, inflamed by the flattery <term>adulatione: excessive or servile flattery</term> of his companions, set out to plunder the marriage-beds of others as if it were some glorious and spirited deed; for so did his companions declare it to him as he went about his boasting. 

From this same cause, Mark Antony was stirred up to bring how many disasters upon the Republic and upon himself! He did this while his flatterers—either urging him on or failing to restrain him—persuaded him that his excessive and unrestrained lusts <note>original: "lascivias"</note> were merely a form of merriment <note>original: "hilaritates"</note> and acts of gentleness <note>original: "mansuetudinis"</note>, which he was entitled to practice at such a height of fortune and power. 

Indeed, what can be said of that madness for singing, the theatrical attire, the lyre, the masks, and other foolishnesses of levity? <note>The author likely refers here to the Emperor Nero, who was famously encouraged by flatterers to perform as a stage actor and musician, behaviors considered beneath the dignity of a Roman leader.</note>

<summary>This page describes how flatterers deceive by renaming vices as virtues—such as calling cowardice 'modesty'—and cites the falls of Paris and Mark Antony as historical warnings.</summary>
<keywords>flattery, deception, virtue and vice, Paris of Troy, Mark Antony, morality, rhetoric</keywords>

--- Page 151 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the dangerous influence of flatterers, moving from historical examples like Mark Antony to the specific techniques used by "parasites" to manipulate their targets without being detected. The author explains why he prefers to use ancient examples rather than contemporary ones to avoid the backlash of telling the truth.</meta>

73.

...imposed: except for the praises of parasites and flatterers, which are accustomed, as the comic poet <note>The Roman playwright Terence in his play 'The Eunuchus'</note> says, **to make insane people out of fools**. I mention the ancients in this section more willingly, for their examples bring us no danger of giving offense. For as the satirist <note>The poet Juvenal</note> says: "You may safely pit Aeneas and the fierce Rutulian <note>Turnus, the rival of Aeneas</note> against each other; the wounded Achilles is a burden to no one." There will be no shortage of others whom our own age has produced. But **truth breeds hatred** <note>original: "veritas odium parit," a famous maxim from Terence</note>; when truth comes against you, "restrain your lip with your finger" <note>An instruction to remain silent and cautious to avoid the anger of powerful people</note>.

Wherefore, flatterers diligently observe men with various words, and not always with soft flattery. When the flatterer perceives himself to be held in suspicion in a matter—for indeed, **conscience is a thousand witnesses** <term>conscientia mille testes: a legal and moral proverb meaning that one's own internal knowledge of the truth is more powerful than any external evidence</term> and not merely a single, reliable authority—he devours them safely "with the whole bean," as the saying goes <note>original: "tota faba commanducat," a proverbial expression meaning to consume something entirely or to act without hesitation</note>. He handles his praises with great skill.

When he notices those distinguished men of greater gravity, who are attentive and watch themselves carefully, he insinuates his commendations not in a direct way, but **obliquely**. After seeking a long, circuitous route and gradually "stroking" them, he tests their character. Just as orators are accustomed to lead the minds of others through the gates <note>original: "per foras," likely referring to the "doors" of the ears or the external senses</note> and use feigned speeches, so also the flatterer [deals with] others...

<summary>The author explains his strategy of using ancient literary figures to critique flattery to avoid modern political backlash, while detailing the subtle, "oblique" psychological tactics flatterers use on intelligent, serious men.</summary>
<keywords>flattery, Terence, Juvenal, truth and hatred, conscience, psychological manipulation, rhetoric, ancient examples</keywords>

...listeners to the proclamations and public praises of great deeds. I recently happened upon a gathering of most noble and elderly men with <insert>great</insert> pleasure <gloss>he</gloss>: they were commemorating many good things and admiring exceptional virtues. This was not only very pleasing in itself, but I also understood your judgment to be confirmed by a noble joy.

<margin>A</margin> For one should certainly not be ignorant of a certain method of flattery <term>assentandi: from the Latin 'assentatio,' meaning the act of smooth or insincere agreement; adulation</term> that has been devised in no common way. For he [the flatterer] fashions certain light and false accusations, by which he might seek an opening for praise. 

> "But," he says, "no one has inquired curiously or without cause why you called me sleepy and cold <note>original: "somnolentum atque frigidum." In a rhetorical context, being "cold" or "sleepy" meant being slow-witted or lacking the fire of passion and action</note> in recent days, as if I were a man in the winter." 

But when the other man denies it, as is natural, the flatterer ensnares the unwary man with praises: "For immediately," he says, "I was wondering and was greatly troubled if you had spoken ill of a close friend—one who embraces you with exceptional love—since, because of your moderation and greatness of soul, you are not accustomed even to offend your enemies by name, and you usually avoid speaking foolishly. Or perhaps you had attacked the boundaries of my little field—you, who because of your innate zeal for liberality <note>The virtue of "liberalitas" refers to the noble-minded habit of giving freely</note>, even [contribute] your own [property] out of kindness..." <insert>to</insert>

This technique, likely drawn from the philosophy of Plutarch, illustrates how flattery can be disguised as a defense of one's character.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the detailed analysis of the flatterer's psychological manipulation. ...and turn your munificence <note>original: "munificentiam." This likely refers to the "zeal for liberality" mentioned on the previous page.</note>. Because if they are plainly unable to praise a vice, they set out to condemn its opposite, using mockery and insults. In this way, by appearing to approve of the qualities their target possesses, they secretly weaken the virtues that might otherwise restrain them. 

For example, toward a lewd man, they pluck away at <term>temperantiam: temperance or self-control</term>, treating it as nothing more than a strange, religious austerity. Toward the spendthrift, they rebuke <term>parsimoniam: thrift or frugality</term> as a "useless stinginess" that offers nothing to the human race. 

Toward those who are idle and seeking a retreat from the city to the countryside, the flatterer is not ashamed to call them back <note>original: "revocatos." The text suggests the flatterer criticizes those who are still involved in civic duty to make the target's idleness seem like a virtue.</note> from urban administration, describing the management of others' affairs as a superfluous burden, and the <term>magistratuum: the office of a magistrate or public official</term> as an ambition that is empty and fruitless, even in its very name.

Consider also that singular wickedness: the way a <term>assentator: a flatterer or "yes-man"</term> casts himself down and disapproves of himself in order to prove his friend’s worth and extol him. This tactic is even loathed by the wealthy, yet the flatterer makes himself lowly and humble so that the patron may despise any potential rival. 

The flatterer says: 
"I hate myself: because when it is time to sail, I lie there like a cheap <term>mancipium: a slave or piece of property</term>, trembling with seasickness. Because of my weakness and fatigue, I succumb to labor as soon as possible. When I hear ill spoken of me, I am vexed by a frenzy like that of a weak little woman <note>original: "muliercula." A diminutive, often derogatory term for a woman, used here in a rhetorical comparison to denote a lack of "masculine" stoicism.</note>." 

But to this "truly great man"—the one he is flattering—nothing seems terrible, nothing too difficult, and nothing seems to be a disturbing or troublesome burden. He handles all things bravely...

...rejoice and triumph. Let those who are <unclear>more astute</unclear> <note>original: "vafriores." Therefore, they should disagree not only in words but also in deeds, both less subtly and more vigorously. Next, let us explain the primary distinction between a flatterer and a true friend.

That distinction is found in their speech: namely, audacity or <term>licentia loquendi: freedom of speech</term> <note>The author is referring to the concept of *parrhesia*, the frank and honest speech that was considered the duty of a true friend in classical philosophy.</note>. Rightly, the story of Patroclus comes to mind: when he was about to charge the enemy, he pretended to be Achilles. Dressed in Achilles' armor and driving his chariot and horses, he nevertheless did not dare to touch or handle the spear alone, which they called the <term>Peliad: Pelian Spear</term> <note>The massive spear of Achilles' father, Peleus; in the *Iliad*, it is said that only Achilles had the strength to wield it.</note>. 

In the same way, the flatterer, though he wears all the other outward signs and decorations of a friend, leaves untouched only the freedom of speech. He treats it as a friend’s unique weapon—heavy, massive, and solid—and he omits it entirely, for it is by no means easy for him to imitate. If a flatterer ever pretends to use this frankness, it is found to be soft, inconsistent, and lacking any lasting power. However, the true freedom of speech that proceeds from a friend, by firmly and constantly addressing faults, produces a somber...

...and furthermore, his "salutation" <note>original: "salutare." In this context, it refers to the flatterer’s superficial greetings and empty courtesies.</note> hinders genuine care. Moreover, the **flatterer** <note>original: "assentator." He rebukes a person for a messy haircut, or for the color of their pack animals. He might treat wives disgracefully or hold kinsmen in contempt. He squanders his wealth, takes pleasure in the company of shameful companions, or commits adulteries. In these matters, there is no fear: regarding these grave faults, you would say the flatterer is utterly mute and tongueless.

76.

[Flat]ter. To avoid the frauds of this kind of person, one thing must be noted and held fast in the memory; it is recognized as very beneficial: that we should first discern the force <note>original: "vim." Here referring to the internal power or driving spirit of the human person.</note> [of the soul]. For as it has been handed down by those who have searched into the causes and secrets of things: there is one part belonging to **reason** <note>original: "ratio."</note>. Its throne, so to speak, and its sovereignty is located in the head; it is always loving and most eager for truth, integrity, and all the best things.

The other part is devoid of reason, dwelling beneath the **midriff** <note>original: "praecordia." In ancient and Renaissance anatomy, the area around the heart and diaphragm, often considered the seat of emotions and base appetites.</note>; it is an enemy to reason and a friend to lying and pleasure. A friend acts as a **counselor** <note>original: "consiliarius."</note>, protector, and helper to the former and truly better part. He will constantly observe it, so that, like a physician, he may preserve and increase its health with all care and diligence.

On the other hand, the **flatterer** <note>original: "assentator." One who habitually agrees with others to gain favor.</note> truly clings to the worse part, which he provokes with itching and tickling <note>The author uses "pruritu et tillatione" metaphorically to describe how a flatterer stimulates the senses and ego.</note>. He urges it on with every skill so that, like a rebel, it might unlearn reason. He devises wicked **lusts** <note>original: "lubidines."</note> for it. He provides lunches, dinners, and prostitutes; he stirs up envies, breeds suspicions, and summons it to even worse things. "Are you angry?" he says, "Take revenge. Do you desire something? Claim it. Does your wife not suit your **spirit** <note>original: "animus."</note>? Hunt for a **mistress** <note>original: "pellicem." A concubine or illicit lover.</note>; and do not let the years <unclear>unclear</unclear>..."

How do I appear to you? A friend never promises help before he has offered counsel, and has first discussed whether the action is either fitting or **expedient** <note>original: "expediat."</note>. The flatterer, however, does not offer any...

75.

He will become a physician, a magician, and all things. One particular deed of the **flatterer** <note>original: "assentatoris."</note> is wrapped in sharp cunning: for since he might imitate a lion's roar, a dog's bark, and the sounds of all other living creatures in order to appear "good" at his craft, so also he understands that freedom of speech <note>original: "dicendi licentiam." This is the Latin translation of the Greek term *parrhesia*, meaning frank, uninhibited, and honest speech. In classical philosophy, this was considered the most essential trait of a true friend.</note> is the proper voice of friendship. He realizes that a modesty which prevents one from speaking is something not at all noble, and certainly not fitting for a friend. He does not leave this part <insert>or</insert> [this tactic] untried, but expresses and imitates it—not that frankness which is true and beneficial, but a false and "tickling" kind. To catch him in this act is a greater task and a greater difficulty. This is similar to what happens with certain **small creatures** <note>This refers to animals like the chameleon or the octopus (polypus), which were standard metaphors in ancient literature for a flatterer's ability to blend into his social surroundings to avoid detection.</note> to whom it is granted by nature that, as they change into various colors according to the place or the sea beneath them, they hide themselves more easily through that similarity and bypass the seeker with deceit. Therefore, let it be our task, by setting out the differences between the two in their proper place, to detect and strip him bare—he who, through a lack of his own character, tries to polish himself with the colors and the garments of others. Soon, therefore, let us consider the beginning. It has been said that the origins of friendship stand out chiefly or especially from here. <margin>Friendship.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the examination of how the flatterer (assentator) gains access to a victim by mimicking their character, following the previous discussion on his use of false "frank speech" and his chameleon-like nature.</meta>

...since the same pleasure, the same interests, and the same efforts are observed in their habits and customs. From this, the proverb was born: <margin>S <note>The Greek letter Sigma (Σ) in the margin likely stands for *Sententia*, marking a proverb or pithy saying.</note></margin> **"Like is most easily gathered with like."** <note>original: "pares cum paribus facillime congregantur." A common classical proverb used to explain that people are drawn to those similar to themselves.</note> Do not the elder with the elder, the boy with the boy, the woman with the woman, and all others with their own kind more naturally agree? Since the **flatterer** <note>original: "assentator."</note> recognizes this, he first strives to approach more closely by this path: <margin>through</margin> the same exercises and the same roles, he gradually attaches himself until, having seized an opportunity here, he cunningly creeps into your intimacy. Therefore, whatever things, whatever way of life, or whatever kind of people you might disapprove of, he also faults and rails against. Conversely, whatever has pleased you, he extols with immoderate praise. If one says "yes," he says "yes"; if one says "no," he says "no." Are you laughing? He is shaken by a louder guffaw. He weeps if he sees the tears of a friend, yet he feels no grief. If you ask for a little fire in the winter time, he puts on a **heavy cloak** <note>original: "endromidem." A thick, shaggy wool wrap used by athletes and those braving the cold.</note>; if you say "I am sweltering," he breaks into a sweat, as the **Satirist** <note>original: "satyrus." This refers to the Roman poet Juvenal, specifically his *Satire III*, which mocks the parasitic flatterers of Rome who mirror their patron's every physical sensation.</note> says. In these matters, he confirms his hatred or his love to such a degree that he is thought to speak not out of mere impulse or emotion, but from a carefully considered judgment. Since, therefore, he does not truly possess the likeness of a friend, but merely imitates and fashions it, in what way [can he be] detec...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "assentator" (flatterer), focusing on how to distinguish a true friend from a deceptive mimic. It follows the previous page's description of the flatterer as a chameleon who mirrors the emotions of his victim.</meta>

...and by which signs he may be cleverly distinguished. 

Above all, the constant and steady delight of the man must be observed: whether he rejoices in the same things as you and commends those same things. Does he establish and direct his life toward a single course and example, and remain constant? These traits pertain to a simple and true practitioner of friendship and familiarity; this is the duty <term>offitium</term> <note>original: "offitiū." The moral or social obligation one owes to friends or society.</note> of friends. 

But the **flatterer** <term>assentator</term>, as one who has no single, stable seat for his own character, does not seek a way of life pleasing to himself, but rather a way of living that will please another. He composes and conforms himself to someone else’s principles; indeed, he transforms himself—not one, not simple, but multiform and varied, taking on one figure and image after another. He behaves in the manner of water, which becomes now round, now oblong, according to the shape of the vessels that contain it. 

But if we apply our minds, we will catch the multifaceted man just as we do **monkeys** <term>simias</term>; for although they emulate humans in many things—both in standing and in moving—nevertheless they are <unclear>recognized</unclear> <note>The OCR reads "inonoscunt," likely a corruption of "agnoscuntur" (are recognized) or "ignoscuntur" (are overlooked/forgiven). Finally, all things that thrive do so because they endure and are held together by love. ### Oration on the praises of Maro delivered in the Florentine Academy by the most illustrious Cristoforo Landino
<note>Original title: "Christophori landini viri clarissimi oratio de laudibus maronis ingymnasio florentino habita." "Maro" is the cognomen of the Roman poet Virgil (Publius Vergilius Maro). The "Gymnasium" refers to the Studio Fiorentino, the university of Florence.</note>

As I considered which of all the Latin poets I should especially undertake to interpret for you in this current year of ours, none could come to mind more quickly than he who—whether you seek the most exquisite principles of speaking well, or the best and holiest model of living rightly—so surpasses all the rest that, when I have rightly placed him first, you will find no one to whom a seat may then be granted unless a long interval is left between them. 

I, moreover, since I see that the duty of educating the youth in eloquence has been entrusted to me by the Republic <note>Original: "re. p." for "res publica." Landino held a state-appointed professorship in Florence.</note>, gladly embrace the opinion of the greatest orator and excellent philosopher... 
<note>The text likely refers here to Quintilian or Cicero, who both highly recommended Virgil as the foundation of an education in rhetoric and philosophy.</note>

<summary>This page finishes a memorial for a Franciscan theologian and begins Cristoforo Landino's introductory lecture on Virgil. Landino argues that Virgil is the supreme model for both eloquent speech and moral conduct.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the oration by the humanist scholar Cristoforo Landino. He is currently referencing the theories of the Roman statesman Cicero to establish the ideal relationship between eloquence (the art of speaking) and wisdom (the knowledge of how to live rightly), which serves as a justification for his study of the poet Virgil.</meta>

46.

For indeed, Cicero <note>Landino is paraphrasing the opening of Cicero’s *De Inventione*, a foundational Roman textbook on rhetoric.</note> states that although in many respects human beings are weak and inferior to animals, they excel over beasts in one thing especially: the fact that they are able to speak. Therefore, it seems that someone who excels other men in the very thing that makes men excel beasts has achieved something truly remarkable. Finally, however, he concludes the whole passage in such a way as to argue that silent wisdom—or wisdom lacking the power of speech—has not been of much benefit to humanity. On the other hand, he shows that eloquence without wisdom <note>original: "Eloquētia nō sine sapientia." Cicero famously argued that "wisdom without eloquence does little good for states, but eloquence without wisdom is often highly detrimental and never helpful."</note> has been the cause of the greatest disasters for both those who possessed it and for their cities and peoples. 

From this, it follows that only he has truly benefited himself and his country who has applied a significant amount of wisdom to the highest eloquence. <margin>This section highlights the teacher's moral responsibility to their students.</margin> For this reason, I consider that person alone worthy of the name of **teacher** who so instructs youth in the principles of oratory that he—through dignity, holiness, and the highest virtue—either sustains that age which is by its very nature prone to desire and pleasures (being as easily bent toward vice as if it were made of wax <note>The "wax" metaphor for the impressionable nature of youth is a classical trope, famously used by Horace in his *Ars Poetica*.</note>), or lifts and raises it up if it has already fallen. And let him select writers of this kind to be read, from whom the students may be taught to think rightly and live honorably.

<summary>Landino argues that eloquence is the defining human trait but warns that it is dangerous without wisdom; he defines a true teacher as one who uses great literature to provide both rhetorical skill and moral guidance to impressionable youth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the oration by the humanist scholar Cristoforo Landino. Having previously discussed Cicero’s views on the necessity of combining eloquence with wisdom, Landino now shifts to the philosophical foundations of character building and the divine nature of poetry, citing Aristotle and Plato to justify the study of literature for young students.</meta>

Aristotle teaches most excellently <gloss>magnificently</gloss> <gloss>nobly</gloss>, as he does in all other matters, that the virtues by which the disturbances of the soul are calmed—so that the appetite itself is rendered obedient to reason—are nourished in us not by nature alone, but through custom and habit. For this reason, it is of great importance in what way we are accustomed to things even from our most tender years.

Nothing at all is sweeter than the work of a poet. For a poem, as it pleases the prince of all philosophers, Plato, is neither a result of chance nor a work of mere craft, but is the most excellent gift of the immortal God granted to mortals. And as the same Plato affirms in the *Phaedrus* <note>Plato’s *Phaedrus* is a foundational Greek dialogue on the nature of the soul, beauty, and the "divine madness" of poets.</note>, the song of a true poet is infused to such an extent with divine frenzy <note>original: "divino furore." Landino refers to the concept that poets are "possessed" by a higher power when they create.</note> that those discordant parts of our soul are tempered into a single harmony. 

Such a great harmony is created that nothing soft or weakened can then creep into our minds; instead, being focused and intense, it clings to the very center of the <term>heartstrings</term> <note>original: "praecordiis." In ancient physiology, the *praecordia* (the area around the heart or the diaphragm) was seen as the seat of the mind and the emotions.</note>, so to speak. It is drunk in by its own sweetness so that it cannot be torn away except with the greatest difficulty. 

"What therefore do you think should be done?" someone might say. "Will he banish poets from water and fire <note>original: "interdicet... aqua et igni." This was the formal Roman legal phrase for exile or banishment.</note>, and so that..."

<summary>Landino argues that virtue is formed through early habits (citing Aristotle) and that poetry is a divine gift capable of harmonizing the soul's discord (citing Plato).</summary>

<meta>This page continues Cristoforo Landino’s defense of poetry. After establishing that poetry is a divine gift and a source of harmony for the soul, Landino now addresses a common historical objection: the fact that Plato famously "expelled" poets from his ideal city. Landino seeks to reconcile this with his own humanist praise of literature by clarifying Plato's true intentions.</meta>

Plato drove <insert>them</insert> out from his Republic: shall we thus banish them from our own practice? Not at all. For neither did Plato declare war against poets, nor do I; though many, relying on his authority <margin>Plato's error</margin> <note>Landino suggests that those who cite Plato to attack poetry are misinterpreting or "erring" in their understanding of the philosopher's complex views.</note>, completely abuse all poets. I shall defend the poets. For what does he say in his book *On Laws* <note>original: *De Legibus*, Plato's last and longest dialogue, which discusses the role of the arts in a well-ordered society.</note> regarding poets? Does he say that poetry should be rejected? Never did he say this; rather, he said that only that poetry should be rejected which, in the tragic manner <gloss>style</gloss>, imitates disturbed souls. But see what he immediately adds: "That poetry," he says, "is to be accepted which sings the praises of the gods, describes established customs, educates our character, extols upright men, and [denounces] the wicked." <margin>discovery</margin> Here also, as when in the *Lysis* <note>A Platonic dialogue focused on the nature of friendship.</note> he attributed the <margin>the same</margin> beginnings of wisdom to the poets. Furthermore, in the work he wrote *On the Supreme Good* <note>original: *De summo bono*. Landino likely refers to the *Republic* or the *Philebus*, where the ultimate aim of human life is discussed.</note>, he contends in a single book that all arts, both divine and human, are to be found in the poems of Homer. If, therefore, by the testimony of such a great philosopher <note>The OCR uses the abbreviation "phi" for *philosophi*.</note>, Homer can be the best guide for those reading Greek toward attaining the supreme good, shall we not also employ our own Latin Homer <note>A common humanist title for Virgil, the author of the *Aeneid*, whom Landino viewed as the Roman equivalent and moral successor to the Greek poet Homer.</note> to achieve that same happiness? For it is necessary, just as sailors [guide] their ships...

<summary>Landino defends poetry against the charge that Plato rejected it, arguing that Plato only excluded "disturbing" tragedy while praising poetry that honors the gods and builds character. He concludes by positioning Homer (and Virgil) as essential guides to achieving the "supreme good."</summary>

<meta>This page continues Cristoforo Landino’s discourse on the path to wisdom. Having previously discussed how poetry can lead the soul toward divine truth, Landino now transitions into a more formal philosophical framework, distinguishing between the ultimate goal of life (the "End") and the moral actions (the "Duties") required to reach it. He invokes the authority of Marcus Varro, Plato, and Aristotle to define human happiness as a state of contemplation.</meta>

[...port]: <note>The text begins mid-sentence, likely completing a metaphor comparing the arrival at a harbor to the achievement of a peaceful life.</note> so we wish to direct the course of our life toward blessedness and tranquility, so that we may hold to that path which is far from [common] customs and belongs to **philosophy**. This is handed down and distributed in such a way that in one part, it establishes a certain <term>end</term> <note>original: *finem*. In classical ethics, the "end" is the *summum bonum* or the highest good toward which all human action aims.</note> and, as it were, an "ultimate" goal. This defines that whatever we do—whether we are acting or whether we are **contemplating**—all things should be referred back to that goal for which everything else is done; yet that end itself should be sought for its own sake.

In the other part, it treats of <term>duties</term> <note>original: *officiis*. This refers to the moral obligations and daily conduct explored by thinkers like Cicero.</note>, by which, as if by certain means and a path, we are led toward that very end. Furthermore, it should be noted in this place that the opinions among philosophers concerning the "ends of good and evil" <note>original: *de finibus bonorum et malorum*.</note> are not only varied but even contradictory among themselves. **Marcus Varro** <note>Marcus Terentius Varro (116–27 BCE), a Roman scholar. Landino is likely referencing Varro's work *De Philosophia*, preserved by St. Augustine, where Varro famously calculated all possible philosophical positions on the "supreme good."</note> tracked all of these so thoroughly—not only those which once had their adherents, but even those that *could* have had them—that he extended the number of these sects to two hundred and eighty-eight.

But, omitting the rest, let us follow the most authoritative. **Plato** and, after him, **Aristotle**, place human happiness in **contemplation**. To achieve this, it is surely necessary that we be pure from all vices; for never [will one enter] into the knowledge of divine things...

<meta>This page continues Cristoforo Landino’s philosophical introduction to his commentary on Virgil. Having established that human happiness lies in contemplation, Landino now argues that this state cannot be reached without moral purification. He bridges the gap between classical philosophy (Socrates) and Christian scripture to demonstrate that the soul must be cleansed of worldly "filth" before it can grasp divine truth, eventually introducing Virgil's Aeneas as the supreme model of this journey.</meta>

Knowledge cannot come to impure souls. Wherefore the most wise **Socrates** said that it is a crime for the impure to touch what is pure <note>original: *purum t[amen] puro attingi nefas esse*. A reference to Plato's *Phaedo* (67b), suggesting that the mind must be free from bodily distractions to perceive truth.</note>: a truth which the **sacred letters** <note>A common Renaissance term for the Bible.</note> teach most clearly. From this comes that golden saying: "the spirit of wisdom will not enter into a malevolent soul" <note>original: *in malivolam animam non introibit sapientiae spiritum*. Quoting the Wisdom of Solomon 1:4.</note>; and the father of this most wise king <note>Referring to King David, father of Solomon.</note> said that the "fear of the Lord is the beginning of wisdom."

It follows, therefore, from those things which we have very briefly discussed, that he who is to attain true happiness must be adorned with those **virtues** by which the soul is cleansed from all the filth of mundane things—which they call the **moral** virtues—and by those virtues through which we are led toward the <unclear>great</unclear> <unclear>image</unclear> of the knowledge of things, which <unclear>properly</unclear> are called **intellectual** <unclear>virtues</unclear>.

Concerning all these things, our divine **Maro** <note>Maro is the cognomen of the poet Publius Vergilius Maro, known in English as Virgil.</note>, being a fervent orator and desiring to exhort us not only to speak well but to **live well**, sought to benefit the human race as much as possible. Therefore, he crafts his **Aeneas** with great reason, so that we might look upon him as a **model for living**. Indeed, who has ever governed a republic with better laws and institutions? In whom...

<summary>Landino argues that moral purification is a prerequisite for wisdom, citing Socrates and the Bible, before presenting Virgil's Aeneas as the ideal pedagogical model for both private virtue and public leadership.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Cristoforo Landino’s argument that Virgil’s poetry is not merely aesthetic but a profound philosophical journey. Having just explained that happiness requires both moral and intellectual virtue, Landino now shifts to showing how Virgil (Maro) illustrates these virtues through the character of Aeneas, moving from the practical duties of a citizen to the spiritual purification of the soul.</meta>

...I ever consider: whether by analysis or by logistics <note>original: *analyssis : aut logistis*. Landino is arguing that Virgil's work contains more than just "war stories."</note> [in his work]. And since not all people in a **republic** can be leaders or be placed in charge of those who work, and far more live as private citizens than in positions of power, he [Virgil] very frequently reminds us how one must act with oneself, with one's family, with fellow citizens, and with foreigners.

Nor does he only command that **prudence** be awakened, **piety** cultivated, **justice** observed, **fortitude** maintained, and **temperance** applied in practical affairs, as well as maintaining **decorum** in civil life. But finally, raising that supreme **Aeneas** beyond mere humanity, he leads him toward that genus of virtues which the **Platonists** call "purifying" <note>original: *purgatorias*. In Neoplatonic philosophy, these are the virtues that cleanse the soul of bodily passions to prepare it for divine contemplation.</note>—to those very things which exist in a purified soul, by which, finally, happiness is perfected.

All these things, even if I do not explain them as I desire, or as the subject itself demands, I will nevertheless indicate them as far as my own strengths allow. For as to the fact that some accuse him of being too wanton in his <insert>subject matter and style</insert> <note>original: *materia* and *loco*.</note>, as if his <term>foot</term> <note>original: *pes*. <summary>Landino argues that Virgil’s Aeneid provides a universal moral guide for both leaders and private citizens, ultimately serving as a Neoplatonic allegory for the soul's purification. <meta>This page continues Cristoforo Landino’s defense of Virgil. He is responding to critics who argue that Virgil’s descriptions of romantic passion (specifically between Aeneas and Dido) are morally corrupting. Landino argues instead that Virgil depicts the destructive power of love as a cautionary tale to show how it can "overturn" even the most virtuous mind.</meta>

[il]lurements to entice <note>This continues the word *illecebris* from the previous page.</note>, they act most unjustly: since, on the contrary, he is to be highly praised when he portrays the mind driven by certain modes of the **passions** <note>original: *affectuum*. In Renaissance philosophy, these are the "perturbations" or emotions that challenge reason.</note> to such an extent that he demonstrates not only that human minds are disturbed, but are utterly overturned. Who will not love [this approach], so that he might rather **hate love**? 

Consider the **Queen** <note>Dido, the founder and Queen of Carthage.</note>, once most chaste: who, with the highest **modesty** <note>original: *pudicitia*. This refers to her original vow of faithfulness to her deceased husband, Sychaeus.</note> and a great spirit, was most ready to perform all duties. Yet after she succumbed to love, she was so changed that she became:

*   **Forgotten** of that city which, in a hostile land with such great danger and labor of her own, she had begun to build most beautifully; 
*   **Forgotten** of her reputation <note>original: *famae*.</note>, which she had maintained with integrity until that day; 
*   **Forgotten** of the labor and the [threats] that were imminent from a most hostile king and those same most fierce barbarian peoples; 
*   **Forgotten** of the safety of her dearest citizens, who, having left their homeland, followed her through a difficult voyage <note>original: *navigatione*.</note> to most savage nations; 
*   **Forgotten**, finally, of herself, so driven out of her mind by love that she repents her [union with] an impure man <note>original: *incesti uiri*. Landino likely refers to Aeneas here, or rather Dido's view of the "unholy" nature of their union because it violated her religious and moral vows.</note>.

So that later, when he is about to depart...

<summary>Landino defends Virgil’s depiction of Dido, arguing that the poet shows how romantic passion (Amor) destroys a leader's sense of duty, reputation, and civic responsibility.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Cristoforo Landino’s argument. He is explaining that Virgil’s depiction of Dido’s downfall is not meant to celebrate passion, but to serve as a moral warning about how love can destroy a noble mind.</meta>

...the vices which she preferred as dearer [than her virtue]. And he marks in this passage: what else does the **most weighty poet** <note>Virgil.</note> seem to do, if not to warn us, so that [passion] might be dreaded and deterred? He does this lest we allow our senses to be soothed by **allurements** <note>original: *blanditiis*.</note> of this kind in such a way that the mind utterly perishes. This is his aim; this is his intention: to show that these most unhappy women—after they were unwilling to drive away so great a **malady** <note>original: *morbum*. In Renaissance thought, intense passion was often categorized as a "disease" of the soul that overpowers reason.</note>—spent whatever remained of their lives entirely full of disgrace and sorrow. And a little later, they were forced to undergo the same madness in a premature and shameful death. 

I judge, therefore, that poets of this kind—in whom we find, besides the **skill of speaking** <note>original: *dicendi facultatem*. This refers to rhetorical excellence.</note>, a correct **model for living**—should be employed for the instruction and shaping of **youth**. 

However, as for the **Ovids**, the **Propertiuses**, the **Tibulluses**, the **Catulluses**, and the rest of the **erotic bards** <note>original: *veneriores vates*. Poets whose work focuses primarily on *Venus* or romantic love. Landino distinguishes Virgil's "serious" epic from their "lighter" elegiac poetry.</note>, I do not reject them. For there are, indeed, many most excellent things in them which can be of great value to us; but I reserve them for a **more mature age**. For since that teaching...

<summary>Landino concludes that Virgil is a primary moral educator for youth because he depicts the destructive consequences of passion, whereas more overtly erotic poets like Ovid should be reserved for mature readers.</summary>
<keywords>Virgil, Dido, morality in literature, Ovid, Propertius, Tibullus, Catullus, education of youth, Renaissance rhetoric, Stoicism</keywords>

--- Page 185 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how to read the "erotic bards" (Ovid, Catullus, etc.) safely. Landino uses the famous classical metaphor of the bee to explain that once a reader is mature and morally grounded, they can extract useful lessons from these poets while ignoring their more scandalous elements.</meta>

...and once we have been so strengthened by practice that we can [maintain] a temperate limit <note>original: *modum brevem*. This suggests a life of moderation and self-control.</note> in our lives: then I think we should approach them no differently than bees approach flowers: who, once they have plucked those things which are conducive to their work, let the rest go. 

I have spoken of those things which pertain to life and character <note>original: *mores*. In the Renaissance, this referred to both personal ethics and social conduct.</note>: as for those things contained in the art of speaking <note>original: *dicendi ratione*. This refers to rhetoric, style, and the technical mastery of language.</note>, because they are understood with less effort, and because they were demonstrated elsewhere by us <gloss>see above, folio 89</gloss> in this very place through a somewhat lengthy <unclear>deduction</unclear> <insert>order</insert>, I deliberately leave them aside for the present so that I may postpone them to more suitable places. 

I likewise pass over how great a knowledge of history he <note>Virgil.</note> possessed, and how most diligent he was regarding antiquity: that he stood out as an investigator not only of our own affairs <gloss>but also of the Greeks and</gloss> of all <insert>nations</insert> peoples: how most observant he was of ancient words: how he shaped his own style by a certain elegant standard: how he grasped nearly the full power [of the language]: furthermore, civil law: I omit pontifical law <note>original: *ius pontificium*. The body of law governing the religious duties and rituals of Roman priests.</note>: I say nothing of the law of augury <note>original: *iure augurii*. The ancient practice of interpreting the will of the gods through the behavior of birds.</note>: nothing of haruspicy <note>original: *aruspicina*. The practice of divining the future by inspecting the entrails of sacrificed animals.</note>: and whatever the Etruscan writings <note>The Romans believed their systems of divination were inherited from the earlier Etruscan civilization.</note> once handed down concerning this matter.

All of these things he held in such a way that not from others 

<summary>Landino argues that mature readers should treat lighter poetry like bees treat flowers—taking only what is useful—and begins listing Virgil's vast expertise in history, law, and ancient religious rituals.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Landino’s argument that Virgil should be the primary model for students. Having established that even the more "earthly" parts of poetry can be read safely by the mature mind, he now transitions into a passionate exhortation to his students to embrace Virgil as the ultimate source of both linguistic skill and moral formation.</meta>

...to have received it, if he himself should seem to have established it.

For this reason, which of you, most studious young men <note>original: *adolescentes*. In a Renaissance university setting, this addressed the students directly.</note>, will refuse to embrace with a most burning spirit—and, as they say, with all your might—so admirable and so divine a poet? For from him you will receive the elegance of grammar; from him, you will receive the power and ornament of the oratorical art <note>The "oratorical art" refers to rhetoric, the skill of persuasive and beautiful speaking.</note>. 

In his company, you will be able to have your minds formed and fashioned by the best principles <note>original: *institutis*. These are the foundational rules or customs that guide a civilized life.</note>. Even if these subjects were treated in a way so meager, so dry, and so uncultivated <margin>were</margin> as they were handled <gloss>^</gloss> [by others], they would still have to be learned with zeal because of their utility. 

But now, since they are distinguished by an abundance of words, illuminated by the weightiness of thoughts <unclear>sententiarum</unclear>, and set forth with such happy variety of healthy matters and <unclear>deeds</unclear>, who would not add whole nights, if need be, to their daily readings? For the greatest pleasure is joined to the greatest utility. 

Give yourselves, therefore, to these things before your own [affairs]; for to you, as with most of you, neither industry nor resources are lacking. And [may you see] a most flourishing empire <unclear>affirm-</unclear> that its citizens, just as in many other most beautiful things, so also in learning and eloquence, might [surpass] the rest of mortals...

<summary>Landino exhorts his students to study Virgil passionately, arguing that the poet provides the perfect foundation for both grammar and rhetoric. He emphasizes that because Virgil combines great moral utility with aesthetic pleasure, students should dedicate themselves to his works day and night.</summary>

It is advantageous to <unclear>advance</unclear>: Give [this effort] to both your parents; for then at last they will not regret having fathered us <note>original: *genuisse*. Landino shifts between "you" and "us" to emphasize a shared community of scholars.</note> when they see that we have turned out not merely equal to them, but far superior. Give it to your kinsmen and friends, to whom no small part of our praises and honors <note>original: *dignitatum*. This refers to "dignities"—the high social or political offices one holds in society.</note> will redound. 

Finally, give it to ourselves, since this is the one thing by which we differ from the rest of the living beings <note>original: *animantibus*. This refers to the classical philosophical idea that reason and speech are what distinguish humans from the "animal" world.</note>, through which you may surpass other men by just as much. And indeed, there is nothing that we should despair of attaining with this [study] as our guide. I pass over wealth; I pass over riches; I pass over honors. I also pass over in silence how many men in our age and in former centuries have risen from the lowest fortune to great honors, and how many have arrived at the highest renown from the most obscure origins. 

But besides that, I consider it a more important point that either no other way <note>original: *ratio*. Here meaning a method, system, or faculty of reason.</note> can be found, or the studies of the liberal arts <note>original: *bonarum artium*. Literally "the good arts," referring to the humanities that were thought to make a person truly "human" and free.</note> are the only things that can provide us with a quiet life along with tranquility, and a freedom from all disturbances; insofar as...
